//角落書屋/Slices/HSK 1
爱	ài	love, to love, to cherish — Love in all its forms: romantic, familial, and the Confucian ethic of universal benevolence.
八	bā	eight; the luckiest number and the eight trigrams — Eight is the most coveted number in modern China through its near-homophony with 发 fā (to prosper), and the classical foundation of the eight trigrams that structure the Yijing.
白	bái	white, clear, plain, in vain — Sunlight on a single point. The character that names the color of mourning, the color of clarity, the empty plate, and the speech that hides nothing.
百	bǎi	hundred; all kinds of — 百姓, the hundred surnames, is classical Chinese for the common people, because the full range of ordinary family names stands in for everyone who is not nobility.
本	běn	root, origin, this, a book — A tree with its root marked: the character for where a thing comes from. From root grows origin, the fundamental, one's own, the principal sum, and the bound volume you hold in your hand.
病	bìng	illness, disease, to fall ill, fault — A person sweating on a sickbed with the phonetic 丙 inside: the radical 疒 frames every Chinese word for being unwell.
不	bù	no, not, negation — The most used character in Mandarin, and the foundation of every denial, refusal, and negation.
菜	cài	vegetable, dish, cuisine — Plants gathered to be eaten. From the picked greens grows a whole table: the vegetable, the cooked dish, the regional cuisine, and the slangy put-down for anyone weak at a task.
常	cháng	constant, ordinary, often — A long banner of cloth that came to mean what endures: the constant and the lasting, the ordinary and the common, and the things that happen again and again.
唱	chàng	to sing, to chant, to call out — A mouth beside the sun-doubled 昌: the radical names the act of singing while 昌 carries only the sound.
吃	chī	to eat — To eat, and the extraordinary range of Chinese life encoded in a single food verb.
穿	chuān	to pierce, to bore through, to wear, to put on — A tooth gnawing through the roof of a cave: one image for boring a hole and for the body slipping into clothing.
错	cuò	error, mistake; wrong, incorrect — A metal character whose oldest sense was inlay and grinding; from crossed, interlocking lines it slid to things crossing wrongly, the modern sense of error.
打	dǎ	to hit, to strike; the most versatile verb in Mandarin — To hit, and a hundred extensions: make a call, play a sport, pack a bag, get an injection. The most productive light verb in Chinese.
大	dà	big, great, large — Big, great, large: a person with arms wide open, the original measure of magnitude.
蛋	dàn	egg, egg-shaped thing, scoundrel — A pictophonetic egg: the 虫 radical for small creatures sits under a sound element, and in slang it turns people into scoundrels.
地	dì	earth, ground, land — Heaven above, earth below: the second power of the cosmos, and the root of every word about place, ground, and belonging.
第	dì	the ordinal prefix, rank, mansion — Bamboo over younger-brother, an image of things laid out in sequence. From it grows the prefix that turns a count into an order, plus the older senses of examination rank and grand residence.
点	diǎn	dot, point, a little, o'clock — From a single dot of ink the character spreads into points and decimals, into the hours of the clock, into ordering food and lighting a flame, and into the small quantity that softens a sentence.
东	dōng	east; eastern — The sun rising behind a tree, and the direction that anchored China's cosmological, political, and poetic imagination.
动	dòng	to move, to act, to stir — Force applied to a thing sets it in motion. From that one idea grow the words for sport and activity, for the stirred heart, for the drive that pushes a machine or a life forward, and for the verb itself.
读	dú	to read aloud, to study — Reading as the act of voicing what is written: the everyday verb for studying, declaiming a text, and (figuratively) interpreting a person, a situation, or a sign.
多	duō	many; much; more; how — Two evenings stacked: the character that encodes abundance, comparison, and the most common question about quantity in Mandarin.
二	èr	two; second — The number two encodes the foundational split that drives all of Chinese cosmology: yin and yang, the two forces from which everything else unfolds.
方	fāng	square, direction, method — One glyph that runs from the literal square to the cardinal directions, to the place a thing sits, to the method by which it is done. The geometry of orientation and approach.
房	fáng	building, house, room — A door radical leaning over the phonetic 方: the side-chamber under a roof that grew into every word for house, room, and the family branches that fill them.
飞	fēi	to fly, to dart; high, swift — A single bird caught mid-flap, one outstretched wing and the rising line of its body, frozen at the instant it leaves the ground.
非	fēi	not, wrong, un- — Two wings spreading in opposite directions: from that image of opposition grows the great negator. Not and non-, the line between right and wrong, and the un- prefix that turns a word against itself.
分	fēn	to divide, minute, point, cent — A knife splitting a thing in two. From the cut grows everything that comes in parts: the minute on the clock, the point on a test, the cent in your pocket, and the act of separating itself.
哥	gē	elder brother, a familiar term for an older young man — Two 可 stacked into one glyph; the doubled phonetic came to carry the warmest word in the family, elder brother.
歌	gē	song, lyrics; to sing, to chant — The radical 欠 (an open, exhaling mouth) drives the meaning of song, while 哥 supplies the gē sound.
个	gè	the universal measure word, individual — The general-purpose measure word that counts almost anything, and the root of individual. From the bamboo-stalk tally grew the everyday classifier 一个 and the words for individual, personality, and build.
关	guān	to close, to concern, the frontier pass — The bolt that closes a gate, the mountain pass that controls a frontier, and the relation that ties two people together. One character holds all three.
馆	guǎn	a public building, hall, shop, establishment — A food-radical building: the eating-house that grew into the library, the museum, and the embassy.
国	guó	country, nation, state — A walled enclosure protecting its people: the Chinese concept of nationhood written in a single character.
孩	hái	baby, child, children — A child-radical fused with the earthly branch 亥: 子 carries the meaning, 亥 lends the rising sound hái.
汉	hàn	the Han river, dynasty, people, and a man — A river that named a dynasty, and a dynasty that named a people. From the water of 汉水 grew 汉族 the Han ethnicity, 汉语 and 汉字 the Chinese language and script, and 好汉, the brave fellow.
好	hǎo	good; to like — Good, well, to like, and the tone split that doubles its expressive range.
喝	hē	to drink; (hè) to shout, to cry out — A mouth radical paired with the phonetic 曷, splitting cleanly into the everyday hē you drink with and the older hè you shout with.
和	hé	harmony, and, with; peace — 禾 (grain) beside 口 (mouth): 和 is both Mandarin's everyday "and" and the Confucian ideal of harmony without uniformity.
很	hěn	very; the empty intensifier — 很 is the word for very, but its strangest job is doing nothing: Chinese needs a degree adverb before a plain adjective, and 很 fills that slot so quietly that 我很好 means simply I am fine, not I am very well.
后	hòu	back, behind, after; empress — One simplified character collapsed two completely separate traditional forms: 後 (a trailing foot in motion) and 后 (a commanding mouth), binding the spatial sense of behind with the imperial sense of empress in a single glyph.
候	hòu	to wait, time, season, climate — A person standing watch beside the road. From the figure of one who waits and watches grows the everyday word for when (时候), the act of awaiting (等候), and the long rhythm of the weather (气候).
花	huā	flower, to spend, patterned — A plant caught in its transformed, blooming state: the flower, the act of spending money or time, and the patterned, fancy, or blurred. One glyph that runs from the garden to the wallet to the dazzled eye.
话	huà	words, speech, talk, language — Speech beside a tongue: the everyday character for spoken words. From it grow talking and conversation, the telephone, a dialect or standard language, and the topics and sayings people pass around.
坏	huài	bad, rotten, spoiled; to break down — A picto-phonetic built from 土 earth and 不 not: spoiled soil that yields nothing, extended to anything gone bad or broken.
欢	huān	joy, merry, to be pleased — A breath of open delight: the hand and the open mouth that make laughter. From this glyph come joy and merriment, to like and to welcome, and the lively, high-spirited mood.
回	huí	to return, to turn back, a time — Two squares nested like a coiling eddy. The character for coming back: returning home, turning around, replying, and counting the times a thing happens.
火	huǒ	fire, flame, anger, popularity — Three flames rising: the character for fire and one of the most productive radicals in Chinese.
鸡	jī	chicken, fowl, rooster — The simplified 鸡 swaps a chicken-coop hand 又 in where the traditional 雞 kept the sound-bearer 奚 beside the bird.
记	jì	to remember, to record, a mark — Speech set beside the self: the act of fixing something so it will not be lost. From that image grow three senses: to remember, to write down, and the mark left behind.
家	jiā	home, family — House, family, school of thought, nation (国家): the fractal of belonging.
间	jiān	space between, interval, room — Sunlight in the crack between two doors: the character for the space between things. From that gap grow time, the middle ground, the human world, and the room you count one by one.
见	jiàn	to see, to meet, to appear — An eye on legs. The character that built Chinese verbs of seeing, meeting, opinion, and the moment a thing first comes into view.
教	jiào	to teach, teaching, religion — A hand with a rod beside a child at the lesson. From that scene of instruction grows a wide field: to teach and educate, to teach a skill in the spoken jiao reading, and the teaching that became the word for religion.
姐	jiě	elder sister, miss, young lady — A woman radical fused to the sound-lender 且, 姐 names the elder sister and stretches to cover every miss and young lady in modern address.
进	jìn	to enter, to advance, to take in — A foot on the road and a bird that only ever walks forward. From that image grows everything that moves ahead and moves in: to enter, to advance, to progress, to carry out, and to take goods in.
九	jiǔ	nine; the imperial number and the highest of the yang — Nine is the largest single digit and the highest yang number, reserved for the emperor and the heavens: nine dragons, nine gates, the Nine-Five supreme position, the ninth heaven.
开	kāi	to open, to start, to operate — Two hands lifting the bar across a closed gate. From that single image flow most of modern life: opening a door, starting a meeting, driving a car, writing a check, blooming.
看	kàn	to look, to watch, to read, to visit — 看 = hand above eye, shielding from the sun to see in the distance: visual attention with effort, encoded in a gesture everyone has made.
来	lái	to come, to arrive, toward the speaker — Oracle bone wheat plant turned direction-word: 来 pulls everything toward the speaker, including time itself.
里	lǐ	inside, within; Chinese mile — From field stacked on earth, 里 named the bounded cultivated interior of a settlement and became the unit of distance that measured the realm's interior reach.
六	liù	six; the six directions; a smooth and lucky number — Six closes space into the six directions (the four compass points plus up and down) and, through its near-homophony with 流 liú (to flow), became a number for things going smoothly.
马	mǎ	horse, a common surname — A three-stroke pictograph of a horse in profile that hides the mane, legs, and tail of its towering ancestor 馬.
门	mén	door, gate, family, field — A picture of a two-leaf gate. From the swinging doors grow the gateway, the household behind them, the school or trade you enter, and the measure word for whole courses of study.
面	miàn	face, surface, side — A pictograph of a face inside its outline that opened outward into surfaces, sides, and aspects, became a measure word for flat things, and in simplified script swallowed the separate character for flour and noodles.
名	míng	name, fame, reputation — 名 is 夕 dusk plus 口 mouth: darkness falls, you cannot see who approaches, so you call out a name. Identity asserted through speech.
木	mù	tree, wood — A tree seen whole: trunk, branches, roots. The pictograph that became a radical, a wuxing element, and the building block of 林, 森, 本, and 末.
你	nǐ	you — The everyday second-person pronoun whose formal twin 您 embeds 心 (heart) directly into the character: address with warmth.
年	nián	year — A person carrying a harvest of grain: year as the cycle of agricultural labor, and the heart of the Chinese New Year tradition.
牛	niú	ox, cow, cattle; (slang) awesome — A four-stroke pictograph of an ox head seen straight on, the horns still visible; in modern slang it means impressively good.
朋	péng	friend, companion; of the same group — Two strings of cowrie shells hung side by side, an old unit of value, borrowed early for companions of equal standing who keep close company.
票	piào	bank note, ticket, vote, a slip of paper — Flames rising over an altar where spirit money burned: 票 carries the smoke of an offering into the modern banknote, the ticket stub, and the ballot.
七	qī	seven; the rhythm of mourning, festival, and the soul — Seven measures the cycles of death and renewal in Chinese culture: the seven-day periods of mourning, the seventh-night lovers' festival, and the seven emotions of the heart.
钱	qián	money, coin, currency — Money: the character that drives markets, proverbs, and the Chinese conversation about wealth.
去	qù	to go, to leave, to depart, to remove — The character that marks every departure, from leaving the house to the euphemism for death. 去 is the verb of things moving away.
热	rè	hot, heat, fever, ardent, popular — The four dots of flame beneath 执 carry physical heat into the heat of crowds, fervor, and whatever is trending.
人	rén	person, humanity — Two strokes, barely changed since the oracle bones, write 人 rén; add 二 and you get 仁, Confucian benevolence.
肉	ròu	meat, flesh, the pulp of fruit — A pictograph of a meat slab with its marbling drawn inside; squeezed onto the left of a character it becomes 月, the flesh-moon of the body radicals.
三	sān	three; thrice; repeatedly — Three is the number of completion in Chinese cosmology (Heaven, Earth, Human), and the numeral that generates the ten thousand things in Daoist thought.
山	shān	mountain, hill, sacred peak — Three peaks rising from the earth: the oldest pictograph and the axis of Chinese sacred geography.
上	shàng	above, up, to go up, to mount — The most productive spatial primitive in Chinese. 上 starts as a notch above a line and ends up meaning everything from logging on to getting addicted.
少	shǎo	few; little; young — Small with a stroke removed: the character for scarcity that doubles as the word for youth when read in falling tone.
生	shēng	life, birth, raw, student — A sprouting plant: life emerging from the earth. The most generative radical in Chinese.
十	shí	ten; complete; cross — 十 is written as a cross (a vertical stroke cutting through a horizontal one), the point where all four cardinal directions meet, making ten the numeral of total completion.
时	shí	time, hour, season — The sun moving: time as something visible, measurable, and morally weighted.
事	shì	matter, affair, thing-that-happens — The character for an affair, a matter, a thing that happens. From an ancient pictograph of a hand holding the implements of office, it became Chinese for everything abstract that occupies us: business, jobs, incidents, and the everyday "no problem."
是	shì	to be — The copula, the cleft construction, classical 是非 right-and-wrong.
水	shuǐ	water — The first of the five elements, the radical that runs through hundreds of compounds, and the Daoist image of how to act in the world.
说	shuō	to speak, to say, to explain — To speak, say, explain, and persuade: the character at the center of all communication.
四	sì	four; the four directions; the homophone of death — Four organizes the Chinese world into quarters, the four directions, four seas, four seasons, yet its near-homophony with 死 sǐ (death) makes it the most avoided number in modern China.
送	sòng	to see off, to send, to give — A figure waving 关 at someone whose feet 辶 are already carrying them away: 送 is farewell built into the road itself.
他	tā	he, him; another — 他 was gender-neutral for a millennium until 她 was coined in 1917 to solve a translation problem that Classical Chinese had never needed to solve.
她	tā	she, her — 她 is the youngest character in everyday Chinese, invented in 1917 to translate "she," and the cause of one of the loudest grammatical fights in modern Chinese letters.
太	tài	too, extremely, highest, grand — The character 大 (big) with one extra dot, pushing big past its limit into too-much. From that excess grow the everyday too, the highest and grandest, the sun, and the supreme pole of Taiji.
体	tǐ	body, substance, to experience — The person beside the root: the body as a person's base. From the physical body grow substance and system, the whole as against the part, and the act of grasping a thing by living through it.
天	tiān	sky, heaven, day — Heaven as moral authority, weather, time, and the ground of 天命 the Mandate of Heaven.
听	tīng	to listen, to hear, to obey — The simplified 听 = mouth + axe, but the traditional 聽 encodes whole-body Confucian listening: ear, eye, heart, and upright virtue arrayed together in a single glyph.
同	tóng	same, together, with — Voices joined under one cover: the character for sameness and shared ground. From it grow the words for being alike, doing things together, and the people who share your role, your class, your cause.
外	wài	outside, outer, foreign, besides — A diviner reading the shells at the wrong hour, off the normal schedule. From that image grows everything beyond the boundary: the outside, the outer surface, the foreign land, and the relatives on the mother's side.
万	wàn	ten thousand; myriad; all — 万 is the Chinese hyperbole unit. Where English reaches for millions, Chinese reaches for 万 to express the immeasurable, because the written number 10,000 originally meant scorpion.
问	wèn	to ask, to inquire, to ask after — A mouth at the gate, asking. From that image grow the everyday verb to ask, the noun for a question or problem, the warm act of asking after someone, and the stern act of holding to account.
我	wǒ	I, me, myself — The character for 'I' began as a toothed bronze halberd: the assertive grip of a weapon became the grammatical mark of a self.
五	wǔ	five; the organizing number of correlative cosmology — Five is the center plus the four directions, the number that completes the Chinese cosmos: five phases, five colors, five tones, five flavors, five viscera, all keyed to one master grid.
西	xī	west; western — A bird settling into its nest at sunset: the direction that gave Chinese the word for "things" when it paired with east.
喜	xǐ	joy, to like, happy events — A festive drum sounding above an open mouth: the sound of joy and the smiling mouth that answers it. From this image grow the words for liking, delight, and every happy occasion, above all the wedding.
系	xì	to connect, system, department — A hand holding silk threads. From that image of holding strands together come the great connecting words of modern Chinese: the system, the relationship, the link, the university department, and the colloquial act of tying a knot.
下	xià	below, down, to descend, to issue — The mirror image of 上: a notch below the line that governs everything from going downstairs to issuing imperial decrees.
现	xiàn	to appear, present, now — The luster of jade coming into view. From that glint of something becoming visible grows the whole vocabulary of appearing, of the present moment, and of what is ready and on hand.
小	xiǎo	small, little; humble — Three minimal strokes that became the Confucian axis of moral character: the 小人 (petty person) and the 君子 (exemplary person) are the two poles every learner of classical Chinese must know.
笑	xiào	to laugh, to smile, to mock — Bamboo over a bending person: a form that has split scholars since the Song over whether laughter or rustling reeds came first.
写	xiě	to write; to describe — 写 simplified from 寫, which shows a magpie under a roof: the bird that mimics and transmits, giving its voice to the written mark.
星	xīng	star; celestial body; spark — Born of the sun radical and a sprout, 星 names the stars, the planets, the week, movie fame, and the spark that starts prairie fires.
学	xu é	to study, to learn, learning — Hands guiding a child beneath a roof: the character for learning as both action and institution.
样	yàng	shape, kind, manner, sample — A word once rooted in wood that grew into the language of appearance and kind: how a thing looks, what type it is, the sample held up as a model, and the everyday this-way and how of manner words.
要	yào	to want, must, essential — Want, need, must, and will: the grammar engine disguised as a single character.
也	yě	also; too; classical particle — A pictograph that lost its original image entirely and became one of the most load-bearing particles in classical Chinese: the gavel at the end of every verdict.
一	yī	one; unified; as soon as — The simplest mark a brush can make carries the heaviest philosophical weight in Chinese civilization: 道生一，一生二，the Dao generates One, and One generates everything.
有	yǒu	to have, to exist — Possession, existence, and the have/be distinction that reshapes grammar.
月	yu è	moon, month — A crescent moon in profile, and one of the most poetically charged characters in Chinese literature.
在	zài	to be at, located, in progress — The character that locates: a seedling rooted in earth, expanded into location, preposition, and progressive aspect.
站	zhàn	to stand, to halt, a station, a stop — A person planted upright on the ground, holding their place: from the body that will not move grows the station, the stop, and the website you log on to.
长	cháng	long; to grow; chief — A person with flowing long hair. One character, two readings: cháng (long) and zhǎng (to grow, elder), the richest dual-reading in Mandarin.
正	zhèng	upright, correct, exactly, in the midst of — A foot halting squarely on the target line. From that image grows everything straight and correct: the upright, the standard, the exact moment, and the ongoing right-now.
中	zhōng	middle, China, to hit the mark — An arrow piercing through the center of a target: the character that names China as the Middle Kingdom and encodes the Confucian virtue of equilibrium.
重	zhòng	heavy, important; again, to repeat — A figure bearing a heavy load. From that weight grows everything that carries gravity: the heavy, the important, the grave. Read again as chóng, the same load comes back, layered and repeated.
子	zǐ	child, seed, Master — A picture of a swaddled infant that grew into one of the busiest characters in the language: a child and a son, the honorific for a Master, the empty noun-suffix on a thousand words, and the seed of a thing.
字	zì	character, written word — The character that names itself, and the whole system of 汉字.
走	zǒu	to walk, to go, to leave — A figure in motion with feet below: the basic verb of movement that Chinese uses where English uses dozens of different words.
作	zuò	to do, to make, to compose — A person setting something in motion. From the act of beginning grows the whole vocabulary of doing, making, and composing: the job you work, the work you create, and the role you act out.
做	zuò	to do, to make, to act as — 做 is the everyday verb for doing and making, a late character built to carry the work that 作 had grown too literary to do, and the two still split the labor between them.
东西	dōng xi	things — East-west as the word for 'stuff': a window into directional cosmology.
多少	duō shao	how much, how many — Chinese builds its "how much" by welding many to few, part of a system where opposites fuse into the scale between them.
关系	guān xi	relationship, connections — The untranslatable social fabric: relational leverage, it matters.
国家	guó jiā	nation; state; country — A compound of the two most culturally loaded characters in Chinese: country and family, that encodes the Confucian political imagination in two syllables.
汉字	hàn zì	Chinese characters — The world's oldest continuously used writing system: how Chinese characters work, why they look the way they do, and the categories every learner needs to know.
江山	jiāng shān	rivers and mountains, the realm — The classical Chinese word for the nation: not a territory but a landscape; not a state but a living geography that dynasties rose and fell to possess.
君子	jūn zǐ	the exemplary person — The Confucian moral ideal that replaced aristocratic birth with cultivated virtue as the basis of social authority: anyone who does the work of 仁义礼智信 qualifies.
轮回	lún huí	samsara; the cycle of rebirth — 轮回 renders Sanskrit saṃsāra as 'wheel' plus 'return': the karmic cycle carrying beings through six realms until liberation.
面子	miàn zi	face, honor — Giving, losing, and saving face: the social grammar of dignity.
朋友	péng you	friend — The everyday word for friend, and the rich web of relationship terms (好朋友, 男朋友, 交朋友) built around it.
人间	rén jiān	the human world; the mortal realm — The world of people: where karma plays out, where flowers fade in April, and where the only beings who can hear the Dharma are born.
人生	rén shēng	human life; one's life journey — Human life: not biological existence, but the arc of a life lived: its meaning, its seasons, its reckoning.
山水	shān shuǐ	mountains and water; the landscape — The paired word that names the Chinese landscape: not "scenery" in the Western sense, but a cosmological pairing of the still and the flowing that became the central subject of a thousand years of painting and poetry.
身体	shēn tǐ	body; health — The word for body doubles as the word for health, asking about it is a greeting, and its second character got luckier in simplification than any other.
时候	shí hou	time, moment — The everyday word for a point in time, and the indispensable 的时候 ("when") pattern that every learner needs.
时间	shí jiān	time — Time: the compound that anchors all temporal expression in Mandarin.
天地	tiān dì	heaven and earth; the cosmos — The foundational dyad of Chinese cosmology, pairing Heaven and Earth as the two poles of the created order within which all human life unfolds.
天下	tiān xià	all under heaven — The pre-nation-state Chinese political imagination: the civilized world.
喜欢	xǐ huan	to like — The everyday verb for liking, and how it differs from loving (爱), enjoying (喜爱), and being willing (愿意).
现在	xiàn zài	now — The everyday word for now, where it sits in a Chinese sentence, and how it differs from 目前 (at present), 此刻 (this very moment), and 如今 (nowadays).
小人	xiǎo rén	the petty person — Confucius took a word that meant "commoner" and turned it into the permanent opposite of the gentleman: the small-hearted schemer of every office.
一会儿	yí huì r	a short while — The everyday word for a little while, how it marks both a short duration and the near future, and how it differs from the verb-softener 一下 and from 等会儿.
一起	yì qǐ	together — The everyday word for "together," the 跟…一起 frame for joint action, the "in one place" sense, the "altogether" use of 一起, and how it differs from 一块儿, 一同, and 一共.
缘分	yuán fèn	karmic connection — Buddhist karmic fate: 500 years of cultivation to share a boat with a stranger, a thousand to share a pillow.
正在	zhèng zài	currently doing, progressive aspect — The primary progressive marker in Mandarin -- placed before a verb to signal an action caught in mid-motion, exactly now, still in progress.
中国	Zhōng guó	the Middle Kingdom; China — Two characters older than the country they now name. 中 (center) and 国 (state) joined three thousand years ago to mean "the central states"; the modern country took the phrase for itself only in the 19th century.
中医	zhōng yī	Traditional Chinese Medicine — Two thousand years of systematic medical thought: qi, yin-yang, the Five Elements, acupuncture, and the living tradition that treats the person, not the disease.
爱莫能助	ài mò néng zhù	willing but unable to help — Literally "to love but be unable to aid," used when you sympathize and would gladly help, but cannot. A gracious, slightly rueful way to decline: 我也想帮你，可惜爱莫能助 ("I'd like to help, but sadly it's beyond me"). The 助 again is the aid of 帮助.
把握时机	bǎ wò shí jī	to seize the moment; to grasp the opportunity at the right time — 把握 bǎwò (grip; certainty; grasp) + 时机 shíjī (the right moment). A common exhortation in business, speech, and motivational contexts. Also used as a noun: 有把握 yǒu bǎwò = "to be confident, certain" (lit. to have a grip on it). 没有把握 = "not sure, not confident."
百家争鸣	bǎi jiā zhēng míng	a hundred schools contend — free intellectual debate; a golden age of ideas — Lit: "hundred schools contend and cry out." The standard Chinese phrase for the Warring States philosophical explosion. Still used today for any period of open intellectual competition.
百折不挠	bǎi zhé bù náo	unbowed after a hundred setbacks — perseverance through repeated failure — 百 (hundred) + 折 zhé (to break, to bend; a setback) + 不 (not) + 挠 náo (to scratch; to give way, to yield). Literally: a hundred snappings, yet never yielding. The material image is bamboo or a sword repeatedly bent but not permanently deformed.
班门弄斧	bān mén nòng fǔ	Showing Off Before the Master — Brandishing an axe at Lu Ban's door: the idiom for presuming to display skill before someone who has mastered it.
绑票勒索	bǎng piào lè suǒ	to kidnap and extort: to seize a hostage and demand ransom — 绑 bǎng = to tie up; to bind. 票 piào = here, a kidnapped person held for ransom (the slang 肉票 ròupiào, a human "ticket" to be cashed in). 勒索 lèsuǒ = to extort; to blackmail. A four-character description, not a classical chéngyǔ, but a fixed legal and news expression for kidnapping for ransom.
保家卫国	bǎo jiā wèi guó	"defend the family, guard the country" — patriotic sacrifice — 保家 (protect the family) + 卫国 (defend the country). The phrase that encodes the family-state bond as a call to military service: you fight for the country by fighting for your family; you fight for your family by defending the country.
本末倒置	běn mò dào zhì	to put root and tip in the wrong order: to mistake the essential for the trivial — Lit: root-tip-upside-down. 本 (the root) and 末 (the branch-tips) are the two ends of the tree. To reverse them is to spend effort on the minor while neglecting what truly matters.
逼上梁山	bī shàng liáng shān	forced up Mount Liang — driven to a desperate course — From the heroes' repeated path of being pushed by injustice until they have no refuge but the outlaw stronghold of Liangshan. The chengyu now means being driven by circumstance to take a drastic step one would never have chosen freely, the very pattern by which the novel's decent men become rebels.
鞭长莫及	biān cháng mò jí	the whip is long but cannot reach — distance defeats even great strength or authority — Lit: whip-long-cannot-reach. From the Zuǒzhuàn (左传), a Spring and Autumn period chronicle. A ruler may have power, but he cannot govern what is too distant from him.
辨方正位	biàn fāng zhèng wèi	"to discern direction and rectify position" — to orient oneself correctly before acting — From the Zhouli (周礼). The phrase belongs to the rituals of city-founding and palace-construction: before laying the foundations, the official must determine the cardinal directions and align the building correctly. By extension, in any major undertaking, one must first establish one's bearings.
宾朋满座	bīn péng mǎn zuò	guests and friends fill the seats: a house full of company — Lit: guests-friends fill the seats. 宾朋 pairs guest with friend; the image is a hall packed with visitors. Close kin to 高朋满座, used of a lively, well-attended occasion where every seat is taken.
兵马俑	bīng mǎ yǒng	soldier-and-horse figures — the terracotta army — The name breaks down as 兵 bīng (soldiers) + 马 mǎ (horses) + 俑 yǒng (tomb figures, funerary effigies). The key word is 俑: a sculpted human or animal figure made specifically to be buried with the dead in place of living sacrifices.
病入膏肓	bìng rù gāo huāng	the illness has entered the vitals: beyond cure — From the Zuǒ Zhuàn: a duke dreams the disease hides in the 膏肓, the region between heart and diaphragm that no needle or medicine can reach. Used today for any situation, not only medical, that has decayed past saving.
博爱	bó ài	universal love; philanthropy — broad, impartial love for all — 博 bó = broad, extensive. Sun Yat-sen's Three Principles include 博爱 as a founding ideal. Also the Chinese word for philanthropy and humanitarian love. Related to the Mohist concept of 兼爱.
博采众长	bó cǎi zhòng cháng	"broadly gather the strengths of many" — to learn from many sources — 博 (broad) + 采 (to gather, to select) + 众 (many, the crowd) + 长 (strengths, advantages). A virtue in learning: drawing on the best from many teachers and traditions rather than following a single school. Used for scholarship, artistic development, and leadership style.
博大精深	bó dà jīng shēn	"broad, great, refined, and deep" — vast and profound — 博 (broad) + 大 (great) + 精 (refined, essential) + 深 (deep). The standard four-character formula for describing Chinese culture in formal contexts: 中华文化博大精深 is a set phrase appearing in textbooks, official speeches, and tourist literature.
博闻强记	bó wén qiáng jì	widely read and retentive: of broad learning and a powerful memory — Lit: broad-hearing-strong-memory. Praise for a person who has read and heard much and remembers it all. Also written 博闻强识, with 识 read zhì in the sense of "to retain."
不耻下问	bù chǐ xià wèn	"not ashamed to ask those below" — intellectual humility; willingness to learn from anyone regardless of status — From the 论语 (Analects) , Confucius praising the minister Kong Wenzi: 敏而好学，不耻下问，是以谓之'文'也 (Quick-minded and fond of learning, not ashamed to ask those of lower rank , this is why he was given the posthumous title 'Wen').
不慌不忙	bù huāng bù máng	neither panicked nor rushed — composed and unhurried — Lit: not-panic-not-hurry. Does not contain 慢 but is its most important semantic companion. Where 慢 can carry a negative charge (negligence, arrogance), 不慌不忙 frames the same unhurriedness as mastery: the person who is slow because they are in control, not because they are lazy or contemptuous.
不三不四	bù sān bù sì	neither one thing nor another — shady, dubious, not upright — Lit: not-three not-four. Three and four are "middle" numbers: something that is neither here nor there, neither proper nor improper. Used for shady people or behavior that doesn't conform to standards.
不闻不问	bù wén bù wèn	neither hear nor ask: to pay no attention to a matter at all — Lit: not-hear-not-ask. To stay wholly indifferent, neither listening for news nor inquiring after it. Often a reproach for someone who ignores what should concern them.
不着边际	bù zháo biān jì	wide of the mark; irrelevant; rambling without getting to the point — 不 + 着 zháo (achieve, land) + 边际 (edge, boundary). Literally "not landing on any boundary" , the talk or argument fails to touch the relevant point. Said of speech, writing, or proposals that circle around without engaging the actual issue.
趁热打铁	chèn rè dǎ tiě	strike the iron while it's hot: seize the moment — 趁 while, 热 hot, 打铁 to forge iron. The blacksmith's wisdom, identical to the English proverb. Used to urge acting on momentum before it cools.
穿针引线	chuān zhēn yǐn xiàn	thread the needle and lead the thread: to act as go-between — Literally the seamstress's first move. Figuratively, to broker a connection between two parties, matchmaking a deal, a marriage, a meeting.
错综复杂	cuò zōng fù zá	crisscrossed and tangled: intricate and complicated — Lit: interlaced-gathered-doubled-mixed. Keeps the "crossing" sense of 错: relationships or situations so interwoven and layered that they are hard to untangle. 错综复杂的关系 "a web of tangled relationships."
答非所问	dá fēi suǒ wèn	the answer is not what was asked: to give a reply beside the question — Lit: answer-not-what-asked. A reply that fails to address the actual question, whether from evasion or misunderstanding. A near neighbor of 文不对题, but about spoken answers rather than written work.
大吃大喝	dà chī dà hē	to eat and drink lavishly: feast extravagantly — The doubled 大 stresses excess. Modern usage often flags wasteful banqueting or living beyond one's means.
大错特错	dà cuò tè cuò	greatly and especially wrong: utterly, completely mistaken — Lit: big-error-particularly-error. An emphatic doubling that drives home how badly someone has misjudged: 你这样想就大错特错了 "if you think that, you are gravely mistaken."
大大方方	dà dà fāng fāng	generous and unaffected; poised and at ease in manner — The reduplicated form of 大方 dàfang (generous; gracious; not stingy or awkward). Said in praise of someone whose bearing is relaxed and dignified rather than petty or self-conscious.
大哥别说二哥	dà gē bié shuō èr gē	the eldest shouldn't mock the second: pot calling the kettle black — A proverb on shared flaws: 大哥 and 二哥 are equally at fault, so neither has standing to criticize the other. The Mandarin take on glass houses.
大江东去	dà jiāng dōng qù	the great river flows east — time and history flow on; the brilliant figures of the past are gone — From Su Shi's 苏轼 famous ci poem "Niannujiao · Red Cliff Nostalgia" (念奴娇·赤壁怀古): 大江东去，浪淘尽，千古风流人物. "The great river flows east; its waves wash away the brilliant figures of a thousand ages." Invoked for historical grandeur and the irreversible passage of time.
大姐大	dà jiě dà	top sister: a commanding, dominant woman — A colloquial trigram, not a classical idiom, for the female leader of a group or a forceful, authoritative woman. Carries a half-admiring, half-wary tone.
大惊小怪	dà jīng xiǎo guài	to make a big fuss over nothing — overreacting to small things — Lit: big-shock small-wonder. To be shocked and amazed by things that are actually trivial. A mild criticism of someone who overreacts or lacks perspective.
大闹天宫	dà nào tiān gōng	havoc in Heaven — the Monkey King's great rebellion — The celebrated early episode in which Sun Wukong storms the celestial palace, defeats Heaven's armies, and helps himself to the food and treasures of the gods.
登科及第	dēng kē jí dì	to ascend the rolls and place in the rankings: to succeed in the civil-service exam — Lit: mount-the-roster-reach-rank. 登科 is to have one's name posted on the pass list, 及第 to be ranked within it. A set phrase of congratulation for examination success.
滴水穿石	dī shuǐ chuān shí	dripping water bores through stone: persistence wins — The image of a single drip, repeated for years, drilling a hole in rock. The standard idiom for patient, steady effort overcoming a hard obstacle.
颠倒是非	diān dào shì fēi	to invert right and wrong; to call black white and white black — Lit: overturn-invert-right-wrong. A severe accusation: to deliberately misrepresent the facts, to claim something wrong is right and vice versa. The logical extreme of a bad-faith 是…的 , not just correcting a focus, but inverting the entire reality. Common in political rhetoric and moral arguments.
顶天立地	dǐng tiān lì dì	head touching heaven, feet on earth — a person of complete integrity and heroic stature — 顶 dǐng = to stand at the top of; to press against from below. 立 lì = to stand; to be upright. The image is of a person so morally complete that they fill the entire vertical axis of the universe , feet firmly planted on earth, head pressing against heaven.
东奔西走	dōng bēn xī zǒu	"rushing east, running west" — frantically busy everywhere; constantly in motion — Lit: east-rush-west-walk. Describes someone perpetually in motion, always somewhere else, never settled. Often implies exhausting, stressful busyness with little to show for it. 他为了这件事东奔西走了好几年。"He spent years running all over the place trying to make this happen."
东拼西凑	dōng pīn xī còu	"pieced together from east and west" — cobbled together from miscellaneous sources — 东拼 (stitched from the east) + 西凑 (gathered from the west). Something assembled by scraping together bits and pieces from wherever they could be found. Used for a speech that draws from random sources, a meal made from leftovers, or a budget patched together with emergency measures.
东施效颦	Dōng Shī xiào pín	to imitate blindly without understanding, and make things worse — A homely woman copies Xi Shi's famous frown and clears the street: Zhuangzi's parable about form without essence.
洞房花烛	dòng fáng huā zhú	bridal chamber and wedding candles: the wedding night — 洞房 is the bridal chamber, the deep inner 房 of the house; 花烛 are the red wedding candles. The set phrase stands for a marriage celebration, often paired as 洞房花烛夜, the night of the wedding.
独门绝技	dú mén ju é jì	a unique school's matchless skill: a signature technique known to no one else — Lit: sole-school-unmatched-skill. 门 here is a school or lineage of practice. A 独门绝技 is the secret art that belongs to one tradition or master alone.
对牛弹琴	duì niú tán qín	playing the qin to a cow — speaking to someone incapable of understanding — From a Han dynasty text attributed to 牟融 Móu Róng. A musician plays the most refined music for a cow; the cow goes on eating grass. The problem is not the cow's intelligence but the category mismatch: the music is addressed to the wrong kind of listener.
对症下药	duì zhèng xià yào	prescribe according to the symptoms; tailor the remedy to the problem — 对 (match) + 症 (symptoms) + 下药 (administer medicine). From the case lore of the Han physician Hua Tuo, who gave two patients with identical headaches different prescriptions because the causes differed.
多此一举	duō cǐ yī jǔ	"one unnecessary action too many" — to do something superfluous — 多 (extra, unnecessary) + 此 (this) + 一举 (one action). An extra action that wasn't needed , wasted effort that doesn't improve the result. A gentle criticism of overcaution or unnecessary elaboration: 你不用解释，多此一举 ("you don't need to explain , that's unnecessary").
二话不说	èr huà bù shuō	without a second word: at once, without hesitation or argument — Lit: a-second-word-not-said. Said of someone who acts immediately, raising no objection and wasting no breath: 他二话不说就答应了 "he agreed without a moment's hesitation."
房谋杜断	fáng móu dù duàn	Fáng plans, Dù decides: a perfect pairing of complementary talents — From the Tang statesmen Fáng Xuánlíng, famed for strategy, and Dù Rúhuì, famed for decisiveness. Here 房 is the surname Fáng; the idiom praises two people whose strengths complete each other.
非我族类	fēi wǒ zú lèi	those not of our kin; those outside our group
烽火戏诸侯	fēng huǒ xì zhū hóu	lighting the beacon fires to toy with the lords — crying wolf at the cost of a dynasty — From the story of King You and Bao Si: the beacon fires lit for entertainment, the vassal lords who stopped responding, and the Quanrong attack that ended Western Zhou. Used to describe any deception that destroys the credibility needed in a real crisis.
否极泰来	pǐ jí tài lái	after extreme obstruction (yin) comes peace (yang) — when things hit rock bottom, improvement follows — From the Yijing hexagrams 否 pǐ (stagnation; obstruction , a yin hexagram) and 泰 tài (peace; harmony , a yang hexagram). The cosmological law: forces cycle, yin cannot dominate forever, the turn of yang is inevitable. A comforting phrase in hard times: 否极泰来，好日子还在后面。
哥俩好	gē liǎ hǎo	the two of us are tight: a toast and a drinking-game call — A finger-guessing drinking call and a stock phrase for two close male friends. 俩 (liǎ) is "the two of us," so the whole thing means "we two brothers are solid."
割地赔款	gē dì péi kuǎn	cede territory and pay indemnities — the formula of defeat in unequal treaties — Lit: cut-land-compensate-money. The standard shorthand for what unequal treaties required of China: territorial concessions (割地) combined with massive financial reparations (赔款). The phrase encodes the humiliation of the treaty system in four characters.
个个	gè gè	each and every one; all of them — Reduplicating the measure word makes it mean "every single one without exception." 他们个个都很努力 "every one of them works hard." A vivid, emphatic way to say "all."
个中滋味	gè zhōng zī wèi	the flavor within: the inner experience only the one who lived it can know — Lit: the-taste-within-this. 个中 means "within this matter," and 滋味 is flavor. The phrase points to feelings or hardships that an outsider cannot fully grasp, known only to the person who went through them.
各有所能	gè yǒu suǒ néng	each has their own abilities — everyone has something they're good at — Lit: each-has-what-[they]-can[do]. A Confucian-flavored observation about the distribution of talent. Used to affirm that different people excel in different domains , a polite and diplomatic formula. 人各有所能，没什么好比较的 "Everyone has their own abilities; there's nothing to compare."
各种各样	gè zhǒng gè yàng	every kind and every sort: all manner of things — Lit: each-kind-each-sort. The standard everyday phrase for "all kinds of," stressing variety: 各种各样的人 "all sorts of people," 各种各样的问题 "every kind of problem."
关系密切	guān xi mì qiē	"relationship close and tight" — closely connected; intimately related — A descriptive fixed phrase rather than a classical 成语, but widely used as a set expression in formal Chinese writing and speech. 这两家公司的关系密切，共享很多资源。"These two companies are closely connected and share many resources." The closeness can be personal, institutional, or strategic.
哈哈大笑	hā hā dà xiào	to roar with laughter: a full belly laugh — 哈哈 is the sound of laughter itself, 大笑 a big laugh. The everyday, vivid way to say someone burst out laughing. Common in narration and casual speech alike.
邯郸学步	hán dān xu é bù	losing your own way while trying to imitate another's — ending up with neither — From the Zhuangzi (秋水 chapter). The problem: imitation so total that it erases the imitator's original competence. Used for people who pursue a foreign or fashionable style so completely that they lose their own footing.
汗牛充栋	hàn niú chōng dòng	to sweat the oxen and fill the rafters: a vast number of books — Coined by the Tang writer Liǔ Zōngyuán. So many volumes that hauling them makes the oxen sweat and stacking them fills the house to the roofbeams. Praise for a great library or prolific output.
好景不长	hǎo jǐng bù cháng	good times don't last — a beautiful scene cannot endure — Lit: good-scenery-not-long. An expression of Buddhist impermanence and Chinese realism about fortune. Used when pleasant circumstances are ending or were brief.
好了歌	hǎo le gē	the Song of Good and Done — the vanity of worldly craving — The song sung by a tattered Daoist near the start of the novel, teaching that all the things people chase (rank, gold, lovers, children) end in nothing, and that 好 (good) and 了 (finished with, let go of) are ultimately the same.
好事多磨	hǎo shì duō mó	good things take time — good outcomes require much grinding and effort — Lit: good-things-much-grinding. Used to console someone experiencing delays or obstacles on the path to something valuable. The consolation: friction is the price of worth.
何去何从	hé qù hé cóng	which way to turn; what course to take — Literally "where to go, what to follow", the weighing of a hard choice between paths. A more literary cousin of 怎么办, asking not just "what to do" but "which direction to commit to". Here 何 is the classical "how / what" that 怎么 replaced in everyday speech.
和光同尘	hé guāng tóng chén	to soften one's light and share the dust: to blend in and live among ordinary people — From the Daoist classics: to dim one's brilliance and mix with the common crowd rather than stand apart. A vision of how the wise move within society, joining its dust instead of towering over it. The ideal of fitting unobtrusively into the social whole.
鹤立鸡群	hè lì jī qún	a crane standing among chickens: head and shoulders above the rest — The tall white crane towering over a yard of squat fowl. Said of a person whose talent or bearing makes them stand out from an ordinary crowd.
鸿门宴	hóng mén yàn	the Feast at Hong Gate — a banquet set as a trap — The Shiji's most famous set-piece: the banquet at which Xiang Yu's faction plotted to kill the future Han founder Liu Bang, where the sword-dance of 项庄 (项庄舞剑) masked an assassination attempt and Liu Bang escaped by a hair.
后来居上	hòu lái jū shàng	"the latecomer occupies the top" — the newcomer surpasses those who came first — 后来 (later arrival) + 居上 (occupies the upper position). From the Shǐjì (史记), the court historian Sima Qian. A newly promoted official sitting above older ministers in court: the latecomer had outranked his seniors.
虎门销烟	Hǔ mén xiāo yān	the Humen Opium Destruction — Lin Zexu's burning of British opium in 1839 — Commissioner Lin Zexu's destruction of 1,200 tons of British-owned opium at Humen (虎门), Guangdong, in June 1839. The act that triggered the First Opium War. Commemorated as a patriotic symbol in Chinese history education. The date (June 26) is marked as China's National Anti-Drug Day (中国禁毒日).
花好月圆	huā hǎo yu è yuán	flowers in full bloom, moon perfectly round — a moment of perfection and happiness — The classic wedding blessing and New Year wish. Flowers (spring beauty) + full moon (reunion, completeness) = the ideal of a happy, complete life. Often inscribed on wedding gifts and red envelopes.
话不投机	huà bù tóu jī	the talk does not meet: the conversation does not click — Lit: words-not-matching-the-moment. Said when two people simply cannot find common ground in conversation. The fuller saying 话不投机半句多 adds that "when talk does not click, even half a sentence is too much."
话里有话	huà lǐ yǒu huà	there are words within the words: a remark carries a hidden meaning — Lit: inside-the-words-there-are-words. Said when someone speaks with an implied second meaning, hinting at more than the surface remark admits.
坏人坏事	huài rén huài shì	bad people and bad deeds: wrongdoers and their misdeeds — A set four-character phrase pairing 坏人 with 坏事, often in the formula 与坏人坏事作斗争, "to struggle against wrongdoers and wrongdoing." Carries a formal, almost official register.
欢天喜地	huān tiān xǐ dì	wild with joy: overjoyed, beside oneself with delight — Lit: joy-Heaven-glad-Earth, joy that fills the whole sky and ground. Said of someone bursting with happiness at good news, a reunion, or a festival. 一家人欢天喜地过新年 "the family rang in the New Year overjoyed."
欢欣鼓舞	huān xīn gǔ wǔ	elated and inspired: filled with joy and high spirits — Lit: joyful-glad-drum-dance, so cheered that one beats the drum and dances. Used of a group lifted and energized by good news or a great event, joy that rouses people to action.
皇亲国戚	huáng qīn guó qī	imperial relatives and royal in-laws — The emperor's kin and the families connected to the throne by marriage, the privileged inner circle of the imperial house. Now used, often with a sardonic edge, for anyone who enjoys protection or privilege through family connections to power.
火上加油	huǒ shàng jiā yóu	to add oil to the fire — to make a bad situation worse — Lit: fire-above-add-oil. The Chinese equivalent of "adding fuel to the fire." 你别火上加油了 "Don't make things worse." The equivalent English idiom "add fuel to the fire" has exactly the same logic.
鸡飞蛋打	jī fēi dàn dǎ	the hen flies off and the eggs break: to lose everything, ending up empty-handed — A botched scheme where both the chicken and its eggs are lost. Said when an attempt to grab more leaves you with nothing at all.
鸡毛蒜皮	jī máo suàn pí	chicken feathers and garlic skins: trivial, petty matters — The kitchen sweepings nobody bothers with. Used to dismiss a quarrel or complaint as not worth the fuss: 别为这点鸡毛蒜皮的事吵架.
既生瑜，何生亮？	jì shēng yú , hé shēng liàng ?	Since you bore Zhou Yu, why also bear Zhuge Liang? — The dying lament attributed to the Wu commander 周瑜 Zhou Yu, repeatedly outmatched by his rival Zhuge Liang.
家常便饭	jiā cháng biàn fàn	an everyday home meal: a common, unremarkable occurrence — Lit: home-ordinary-simple-meal. Literally plain home cooking, used figuratively for anything that happens so routinely it is no big deal: 加班对他来说是家常便饭 "working late is routine for him."
家徒四壁	jiā tú sì bì	home with only four walls — extreme poverty; owning nothing — Lit: home-merely-four-walls. 徒 = only; merely; bare. The household contains nothing but the four walls themselves. Used in literature to describe destitution. Often contrasted with 家财万贯 jiācái wànguàn (immense family wealth). A stock phrase for total financial ruin.
家喻户晓	jiā yù hù xiǎo	known to every household — a household name; universally known — Lit: family-understood-household-known. 喻 = understood; 晓 = known; aware. Every family understands it; every household knows it. 这首歌已经家喻户晓了。(This song is now known to everyone.) The go-to phrase for "universally famous" in Chinese.
兼爱天下	jiān ài tiān xià	love all under Heaven impartially — the Mohist manifesto in four characters — 兼 jiān (jointly; all together) + 爱 ài (love) + 天下 tiānxià (all under Heaven; the world). The compressed statement of Mozi's central ethical position: love extended equally to every person without gradation by family, state, or social proximity.
见笑	jiàn xiào	to be laughed at: forgive my poor effort — Lit: be-seen laughed-at. A set humble phrase: 让您见笑了 ("I've let you laugh at me") deflects praise for one's own work or skill. Polite, slightly formal register.
将错就错	jiāng cuò jiù cuò	to take the mistake and run with it: to leave a wrong thing as it is — Lit: take-the-error-go-with-the-error. Rather than undo a mistake already made, one accepts it and proceeds on that footing, often because correcting it would cost more than living with it.
皆大欢喜	jiē dà huān xǐ	all are greatly delighted — everyone ends up happy — Originally the closing formula of Buddhist sutras, where the assembly rejoices at the teaching and departs; now the phrase for win-win endings, amicable settlements, and comedies. It is also the standard Chinese title of Shakespeare's As You Like It, a translation with better provenance than most.
巾帼不让须眉	jīn guó bù ràng xū méi	"the woman's headdress yields nothing to the man's beard" — a woman who is the equal of any man — 巾帼 jīnguó , a woman's hair ornament, by metonymy "woman." 须眉 xūméi: beard and brows, by metonymy "man." Used in praise of female generals (Mulan, Hua Mulan; Liang Hongyu), athletes, and accomplished women.
今非昔比	jīn fēi xī bǐ	the present cannot compare with the past — Literally "today is not what the past compared to," meaning things have changed greatly, usually for the better, so that now far surpasses before. 今 (now) versus 昔 (former times). A common way to mark dramatic progress or transformation.
锦上添花	jǐn shàng tiān huā	"adding flowers on top of brocade" — making something excellent even better — Brocade (锦 jǐn) is already the finest silk, already decorated with woven patterns. Adding flowers on top of it is a gift that was not needed but is delightful. Contrasted with 雪中送炭 (sending coal in the snow), helping someone in genuine need. 锦上添花 is the luxury gesture; 雪中送炭 is the necessary one.
惊天动地	jīng tiān dòng dì	"startling heaven, shaking earth" — earth-shaking; of tremendous power or significance — 惊 jīng (to startle; to alarm) + 动 dòng (to move; to shake). When something is so significant that heaven is startled and earth trembles, the hyperbole names genuine magnitude. Used for historic events, monumental achievements, and powerful works. 他们完成了一项惊天动地的壮举。
九九八十一难	jiǔ jiǔ bā shí yī nàn	the eighty-one tribulations — a long sequence of trials that must all be passed — From 西游记: the pilgrims must survive exactly eighty-one ordeals (九九, "nine nines," a number of completeness) before the scriptures can be won.
九牛一毛	jiǔ niú yī máo	"one hair from nine oxen" — a trifling amount; a drop in the bucket — 九牛 (nine oxen) and 一毛 (a single hair). A lone hair plucked from nine whole oxen is utterly negligible against the mass it comes from. The phrase comes from Sima Qian, who wrote that his own death would weigh on the world no more than one hair lost from nine oxen.
就地取材	jiù dì qǔ cái	to use local materials; to make do with what is immediately available — Lit: just-ground-take-materials. 就地 = "right here on the spot" (from 就 = to approach what is near). 取材 = to gather materials. The idiom celebrates resourcefulness , using what is immediately at hand rather than waiting for something better. The 就 captures the "readiness without delay" quality.
君子不器	jūn zǐ bù qì	the exemplary person is not a vessel — Confucius says the junzi is not a utensil: not defined by function, not limited to one use. The Analects' argument against reducing a person to their usefulness.
开天辟地	kāi tiān pì dì	"to split open heaven and cleave earth" — to create the world; a groundbreaking, unprecedented achievement — From the Pangu myth (盘古 Pángǔ). In the beginning, all was 混沌 hùndùn: undifferentiated chaos. Pangu broke it open. The light, clear elements rose and became Heaven; the heavy, turbid elements sank and became Earth. Pangu stood between them for 18,000 years until the separation was permanent.
来得及	lái de jí	to have enough time; to make it in time — A potential complement construction: 来 (come/arrive) + 得 + 及 (reach in time). The negative 来不及 is one of the highest-frequency colloquial expressions. 来不及了！ "There's no time!" Both forms exemplify the potential complement use of 得/不.
来者不拒	lái zhě bù jù	turn no one away; welcome all comers; refuse nothing that comes — 来者 = those who come + 不拒 = do not refuse. Originally a statement of generous hospitality or all-accepting policy. Used in commercial contexts (a shop that accepts all customers), in descriptions of a person with an open-door policy, and ironically for someone who takes on too much work.
六月飞霜	liù yu è fēi shuāng	"frost flies in the sixth month" — a sign of grievous injustice — 六月 (the sixth lunar month, high summer) plus 飞霜 (flying frost). Frost in midsummer is an impossibility that heaven produces only in response to a wrong so great it disturbs the natural order.
乱七八糟	luàn qī bā zāo	"chaotic seven, messy eight" — in complete disorder; a total mess — 乱 (chaotic) + 七八 (seven-eight) + 糟 (rotten, spoiled). One of the most common expressions in spoken Mandarin for utter disorder, whether of a messy room, a tangled situation, or muddled thinking. The embedded 七八 again uses the clashing seven-and-eight to mean jumbled, every-which-way.
马马虎虎	mǎ mǎ hū hū	horse-horse tiger-tiger: so-so, careless, passable — The doubled form of 马虎. Used both for sloppy work and, self-deprecatingly, to answer "how are you?" with a shrugging "just so-so."
满腔热血	mǎn qiāng rè xu è	a chest full of hot blood: brimming with fervor — 满腔 a full chest, 热血 hot blood. Describes youthful idealism and the will to throw oneself into a cause. Warm and slightly literary in tone.
满室生辉	mǎn shì shēng huī	the whole room glows — a radiant, welcoming presence fills the space — Lit: room-full-give-off-radiance. Used for a beautiful person or a remarkable guest whose presence lights up a room. A formal compliment in social and literary contexts.
门庭若市	mén tíng ruò shì	the gateway like a marketplace: thronged with visitors — Lit: gate-courtyard-like-market. Said of a household or office so busy with callers that the entrance resembles a crowded market. Its opposite is 门可罗雀, a gate so deserted you could net sparrows in it.
孟母三迁	Mèng mǔ sān qiān	Mencius's mother moves three times — the importance of environment in education — Not from the Mencius text itself but from the Lienüzhuan 列女传 (Biographies of Exemplary Women), attributed to Liu Xiang 刘向 (77–6 BCE).
莫须有	mò xū yǒu	perhaps he did — a charge without evidence; judicial murder by innuendo — Chancellor Qin Hui's response when asked what evidence existed for Yue Fei's treason charge: 莫须有, variously translated as "perhaps," "maybe there was a reason," or "need it necessarily be?" The phrase captures the essence of political show-trial logic: the accusation is sufficient; evidence is irrel…
内外有别	nèi wài yǒu bié	inside and outside are distinct: insiders and outsiders are treated differently — Lit: inside-outside-have-distinction. The principle that there is a proper line between those within a family, group, or nation and those without, and that the two are not handled the same way.
能者多劳	néng zhě duō láo	the capable bear the heavier burden; the able do more work — 能者 = the capable one; one who can + 多劳 = works more, bears more labor. A maxim that can be used approvingly (the talented should take on more responsibility) or ironically (the competent get punished with extra tasks).
你觉得呢？	nǐ ju é de ne ?	What do you think? — An extremely common conversational turn, throwing the question back to the other person. The particle 呢 ne softens it and invites their view. One of the most natural ways to ask for an opinion in spoken Chinese, and a good phrase to keep a conversation going.
拈花微笑	niān huā wēi xiào	holding up a flower, smiling faintly — wordless understanding — Chan's founding legend, recorded in Song-era lamp histories: the Buddha silently held up a flower before the assembly, and only Mahākāśyapa smiled, receiving the transmission outside words. Cited for any perfect unspoken communication between two people who need no explanation.
牛刀小试	niú dāo xiǎo shì	a small test of the ox-cleaver: great talent on a minor task — From the cook Páo Dīng of the Zhuāngzǐ, whose ox-carving blade glides through every joint. A heavy cleaver lightly tried, said when a capable person handles an easy job and shows the skill in reserve.
朋友遍天下	péng you biàn tiān xià	to have friends all over the world — A warm saying praising someone with a wide circle of friends everywhere. 遍 biàn means "all over," 天下 "under heaven, the whole world." Often paired with the encouraging proverb 在家靠父母，出门靠朋友 ("at home you rely on parents; away from home you rely on friends"), which captures the practical importance of…
披星戴月	pī xīng dài yu è	cloaked in stars, capped with the moon — toiling from before dawn until after dark — To wear the stars as a cape and the moon as a hat is to be outdoors working while they are up. Said admiringly of farmers, delivery riders, and anyone on the early-and-late grind: 他披星戴月地工作，就为了孩子上大学。He works from starlight to moonlight, all so his child can attend university.
普度众生	pǔ dù zhòng shēng	to universally deliver all sentient beings from suffering — A Buddhist phrase (from Guanyin's vow) applied also to Mazu, who is often assimilated with the Goddess of Mercy in folk practice. 普度 pǔdù = universally deliver; 众生 zhòngshēng = all living beings.
七上八下	qī shàng bā xià	"seven up, eight down" — nervous and unsettled; anxious about the outcome — Seven and eight are the neighbors of ten who cannot agree on direction. Seven goes up, eight goes down: the image is of something that cannot find rest, oscillating without resolution. The idiom describes the feeling of anxiety before a result, the inner turbulence of waiting without knowing.
七嘴八舌	qī zuǐ bā shé	"seven mouths, eight tongues" — everyone talking at once; a clamor of voices — 七嘴 (seven mouths) and 八舌 (eight tongues): so many people speaking at the same time that no single voice can be made out. Describes a crowd all offering opinions at once, a noisy discussion where everyone interrupts everyone.
谦谦君子	qiān qiān jūn zǐ	"the humbly modest exemplary person" — the junzi whose modesty is doubled and genuine — From the 易经 (Yijing), first line of hexagram 15 谦 Qiān: 谦谦君子，用涉大川，吉 (The humbly modest exemplary person , it is advantageous to cross the great river. Good fortune). The doubling of 谦谦 marks the quality as thorough and genuine rather than surface-level.
琴瑟和鸣	qín sè hé míng	qin and se playing in harmony — a happy and loving marriage — 琴 qín (seven-string zither) and 瑟 sè (larger twenty-five string zither) are the classical paired instruments. When they sound together in harmony, they are the image of a couple in accord. A literary idiom for marital happiness, used in wedding toasts and poetry.
轻举妄动	qīng jǔ wàng dòng	to act rashly and recklessly: to move without due thought — Lit: light-lifting-reckless-moving. A warning against hasty, ill-considered action, often 不要轻举妄动 "do not act rashly," said when caution and patience are called for.
求同存异	qiú tóng cún yì	seek common ground while keeping differences: agree where you can, set the rest aside — Lit: seek-sameness-preserve-difference. A principle of diplomacy and negotiation: build on what is shared without forcing every disagreement to be resolved first.
热泪盈眶	rè lèi yíng kuàng	hot tears filling the eye sockets: moved to tears — 热泪 hot tears, 盈眶 brimming the eye rims. For the moment emotion wells up and the eyes flood. Used for reunions, awards, and stirring speeches.
任人宰割	rèn rén zǎi gē	to be at the mercy of others; to be slaughtered at will by whoever wants to — Lit: let-people-slaughter-carve. The extreme end of the 被 spectrum , not just being acted upon but being vulnerable to anyone who chooses to exploit you. 宰割 = to slaughter and carve up (animals, and by extension, the weak).
塞翁失马	sài wēng shī mǎ	The Old Man at the Frontier Loses His Horse — His horse ran off. His neighbours grieved. He shrugged. The Daoist parable that became China's word for the unknowability of fortune.
三顾茅庐	sān gù máo lú	"three visits to the thatched cottage" — to seek out a person of talent with great persistence and sincerity — The most celebrated episode from Romance of the Three Kingdoms. Liu Bei, ruler of Shu, visits the hermit Zhuge Liang three times at his remote thatched cottage before Zhuge Liang agrees to leave seclusion and serve as his strategist.
僧多粥少	sēng duō zhōu shǎo	many monks, little porridge — too many claimants for scarce resources — The monastery breakfast as economic model. Used for job markets (百个人抢一个岗位，僧多粥少), housing, research funding, and concert tickets. The pictorial economy of the phrase, one queue and one pot, is why it beats the abstract word for scarcity in daily speech.
杀鸡儆猴	shā jī jǐng hóu	kill the chicken to warn the monkey: punish one to frighten the rest — Make an example of a minor offender so the watching crowd falls into line. Common in talk of office discipline and political crackdowns.
山穷水尽	shān qióng shuǐ jìn	"the mountains are exhausted, the waters are at their end" — at the end of one's resources — From Lu You (陆游, Song dynasty), 游山西村: 山穷水尽疑无路，柳暗花明又一村 ("when the mountains are spent and the waters are done and there seems to be no road , there, beyond the dim willows and bright flowers, is another village").
山重水复	shān chóng shuǐ fù	mountains piled on mountains, waters winding through waters — a seemingly hopeless impasse — From a poem by Lu You 陆游. Full line: 山重水复疑无路，柳暗花明又一村, "Mountain on mountain, water on water, seemingly no path; then through dark willows and bright flowers, a village appears." The proverb for finding hope when all seems lost.
少见多怪	shǎo jiàn duō guài	"seen little, amazed by much" — making a fuss about ordinary things — 少见 (having seen few things) + 多怪 (finding much to be strange/surprising). When you haven't seen much of the world, ordinary things seem remarkable. A mild criticism of provinciality or naïvety: someone who marvels at things a more experienced person takes for granted.
生生不息	shēng shēng bù xī	life generating life without ceasing — the endless renewal of existence — A Daoist cosmological phrase: life perpetually generating more life. Used in nature writing, philosophical prose, and speeches about cultural continuity.
十年寒窗	shí nián hán chuāng	ten years at a cold window — long, austere years of study — The image of the exam candidate reading through winters without heat. The full couplet adds the payoff: ten years at the cold window unknown, one exam success and the world knows your name (十年寒窗无人问，一举成名天下知). Modern parents invoke it during gaokao season with full sincerity.
十年树木，百年树人	shí nián shù mù , bǎi nián shù rén	"ten years to grow a tree, a hundred years to cultivate a person" — education takes far longer than any other cultivation — From the classical text 管子 Guǎnzi. Growing a tree requires ten years of patience: planting, watering, waiting. Cultivating a human being , educating, civilizing, forming character , requires ten times longer. The century-scale horizon is a deliberate rebuke to impatience.
时不我待	shí bù wǒ dài	time will not wait for me — seize the moment; there is no time to lose — Lit: time-not-me-wait. A classical inversion , the subject (时, time) is placed before the negation, with "me" as object, producing the archaic feel of a proverb. The phrase frames time as an agent with no personal loyalty: it moves whether you are ready or not.
时过境迁	shí guò jìng qiān	time passes, circumstances change — things are different now; that was then — 时过 (time has passed) + 境迁 (the situation has shifted , 境 jìng: circumstances, context; 迁 qiān: to move, to change). Used to acknowledge that what was true, appropriate, or possible in the past no longer applies , without judgment, simply as a statement of temporal fact.
时候不早	shí hou bù zǎo	the hour is not early: it is getting late — A common set expression rather than a classical chengyu. Used to signal politely that time has slipped on, often as a cue to wrap up or take one's leave: 时候不早了，我该走了 "it is getting late, I should go."
时来运转	shí lái yùn zhuǎn	when the time comes, fortune turns — one's luck changes for the better — Lit: time-comes fortune-turns. Fortune is not fixed; it turns like a wheel. Used when someone's luck dramatically improves after a period of misfortune. The Daoist wheel of fortune encoded in a four-character phrase.
时势造英雄	shí shì zào yīng xióng	the times produce their heroes — Great figures are made by the conditions of their age, not by innate destiny alone, the circumstances create the opportunity for greatness.
事不过三	shì bù guò sān	"nothing happens more than three times" — the third time is the last; do not push beyond three — A folk saying rather than a classical text quotation: three is the outer limit of tolerance or recurrence.
事过境迁	shì guò jìng qiān	the affair has passed and the circumstances have changed — 事 (the matter) 过 (has passed) 境 (circumstances) 迁 (have shifted). Note 过 in its full verbal sense, the same morpheme that grammaticalized into the experiential particle. Used to wave off old grievances: 事过境迁，别再提了 "It's all in the past, don't bring it up again."
事与愿违	shì yǔ yuàn wéi	things go contrary to ones wishes — Traced to Ruan Ji of the Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove: a literary formulation of the gap between intention and outcome, resigned rather than accusatory, with no blame assigned.
是非颠倒	shì fēi diān dǎo	right and wrong turned upside down — moral inversion — Lit: right-wrong-overturned-upended. Used when injustice prevails, when blame is assigned to the innocent, or when someone deliberately distorts moral reality. 他把是非颠倒，让人无法接受。Strong condemnation in formal and classical register.
是非对错	shì fēi duì cuò	right and wrong, correct and incorrect — the full moral spectrum of a situation — Lit: right-wrong-correct-incorrect. A compound of two pairs: 是非 (right/wrong as a classical pair) and 对错 (correct/incorrect as a modern pair). Used to describe careful moral judgment of a complex situation. 分清是非对错 = "to clearly distinguish right from wrong in the situation."
是非曲直	shì fēi qū zhí	right and wrong, crooked and straight: the true merits of a case — Lit: right-wrong-crooked-straight. The four poles by which a dispute is judged. To 分清是非曲直 is to sort out who is truly in the right, free of bias.
守候多时	shǒu hòu duō shí	to have kept watch for a long while: to wait faithfully and at length — Lit: keep-watch-much-time. Said of someone who has been standing by patiently for a long stretch, often awaiting a person or an awaited moment. 守候 pairs the vigil of 守 with the waiting of 候.
水滴石穿	shuǐ dī shí chuān	water drips and the stone is bored through: small force, long time — The same image reordered, traced to the Han text Hàn Shū. Stresses the result, the worn-through stone, as proof that small efforts accumulate.
水中捞月	shuǐ zhōng lāo yu è	fishing the moon out of water — chasing an illusion — Lit: water-inside-fish-moon. The moon's reflection in a pond looks real but cannot be grasped. Used for futile efforts chasing something impossible. A Buddhist parable: mistaking the reflection for reality.
说来话长	shuō lái huà cháng	to start telling it makes a long story: it is a long story — Lit: to-speak-of-it-the-words-grow-long. The standard preface to a complicated explanation, the Chinese equivalent of "well, it's a long story."
说一不二	shuō yī bù èr	say one, not two — a person whose word is final; absolute authority — Lit: say-one-not-two. To speak once and have it stand; not to be contradicted. Describes a figure of absolute authority within their domain. Can be used admiringly (a decisive leader) or critically (an autocrat). 他在公司里说一不二。Tā zài gōngsī lǐ shuō yī bù èr. "In the company, what he says goes."
四面八方	sì miàn bā fāng	"four faces, eight directions" — from every direction; from everywhere — The most common chengyu using 方. 四面 (the four faces of a square; the four sides) + 八方 (the eight directions). Used for crowds, news, demands, attacks , anything converging from every direction. 来自四面八方的客人 (guests from all over).
四面楚歌	sì miàn chǔ gē	"Chu songs on all four sides" — besieged on every side; utterly isolated — From the fall of Xiang Yu, the Hegemon-King of Chu, at the battle of Gaixia (202 BCE). Surrounded by Han forces, Xiang Yu heard his enemies singing the songs of his own homeland of Chu from every direction, and concluded that Chu had already fallen and his men had deserted to the enemy.
送往迎来	sòng wǎng yíng lái	to send off those leaving and welcome those arriving: the endless round of social hospitality — Traces to the Lǐjì (Book of Rites). Describes the constant ceremony of receiving guests and seeing them off, often with a faint note of how exhausting such social duty becomes.
岁月不饶人	suì yu è bù ráo rén	the years spare no one — time is merciless; age catches up with everyone — 岁月 suìyuè (years and months , a classical compound for the flow of time) + 不饶 bù ráo (shows no mercy, does not spare). A cousin-phrase to 时不我待, but the mood is different: less urgent, more resigned. Where 时不我待 urges action, 岁月不饶人 acknowledges what cannot be undone.
所谓伊人	suǒ wèi yī rén	"the one of whom I speak" — the beloved, the one I yearn for — From the Shijing (诗经) poem 蒹葭 ("Reeds"). One of the most famous lines of classical poetry, and the locus classicus for 伊 as feminine third person. The poem itself is gender-ambiguous in the classical text but read across two millennia as referring to a beloved woman across the river.
昙花一现	tán huā yí xiàn	to flower briefly and vanish — Literally "the night-blooming cereus shows itself once," for something splendid that lasts only a moment and is gone. The flower opens for a single short while in the night, then withers. A poignant image of fleeting brilliance, the shadow side of "a little while."
堂堂正正	táng táng zhèng zhèng	dignified and upright: open, honorable, with nothing to hide — Reduplicated from 堂正. Said of someone who acts squarely and aboveboard, facing things head-on rather than scheming in the shadows. 堂堂正正做人 "to live as an upright person."
桃李满天下	táo lǐ mǎn tiān xià	peaches and plums fill the world: a teacher's students are everywhere — Lit: peach-plum-fill-the-world. 桃李 (peach and plum trees) stand for a teacher's 弟子, the disciples cultivated over a lifetime. To say someone's 桃李满天下 is high praise for a great educator.
啼笑皆非	tí xiào jiē fēi	unable to laugh or cry: caught between amusement and dismay — Lit: crying-laughing-both-wrong. Neither weeping nor laughing fits the situation, the awkward state of finding something at once absurd and exasperating. 令人啼笑皆非 "leaves one not knowing whether to laugh or cry."
天赐良缘	tiān cì liáng yuán	"heaven-bestowed good fate" — a heaven-sent destined match; a blessed union — Lit: heaven-bestow-good-fate. The most auspicious framing for a destined romantic match , one that heaven itself has arranged and blessed. Used in wedding toasts, blessings, and romantic contexts when the match seems particularly right. 他们的婚姻真是天赐良缘，两个人都幸福极了。
天翻地覆	tiān fān dì fù	"heaven overturned, earth capsized" — a total upheaval; world-reversing change — 翻 fān (to overturn; to flip) + 覆 fù (to capsize). When the cosmos itself is described as flipped, the image captures change so total that the existing order has been inverted. Used for dramatic historical upheavals or situations where everything has changed beyond recognition. 经历了这场变革，国家发生了天翻地覆的变化。
天后圣母	Tiān hòu Shèng mǔ	Heavenly Empress, Holy Mother — Mazu's full honorific — The full formal title combines 天后 (Heavenly Empress, the highest imperial designation) with 圣母 Shèngmǔ (Holy Mother, a term shared with the Blessed Virgin in Catholic contexts, creating interesting resonance in Fujian where Portuguese missionaries arrived in the 16th century).
天人一体	tiān rén yī tǐ	"Heaven and humanity as one body" — the complete unity of cosmic and human orders — A variant formulation of 天人合一, with 体 (body; substance) replacing 合一 (united as one). 一体 emphasizes organic embodied unity , Heaven and humanity are not merely in accord but constitute a single body, a single substance.
天时地利人和	tiān shí dì lì rén hé	"Heaven's timing, Earth's advantages, human harmony" — the three conditions for success — From Mencius (2A.1): the convergence of the right moment from Heaven, favorable terrain from Earth, and human solidarity. A practical expression of 天人合一 in strategic thought: success requires alignment with all three orders simultaneously.
铁哥们儿	tiě gē men r	iron buddies: rock-solid male friends — 铁 (iron) intensifies 哥们儿. The metaphor is metal: a friendship that won't bend or rust. Reserved for the few friends you would drop everything for.
同舟共济	tóng zhōu gòng jì	in the same boat, crossing together: united by a common difficulty — Lit: same-boat-jointly-cross. People who, sharing one boat, must row together to reach the far shore. The classical image for setting aside differences in the face of a shared crisis.
突飞猛进	tū fēi měng jìn	to advance by leaps and bounds: to progress at great speed — Lit: sudden-fly-fierce-advance. Said of rapid, dramatic progress in study, technology, or an economy. 科技突飞猛进 "technology advances by leaps and bounds."
徒有虚名	tú yǒu xū míng	nothing but an empty name — a title with no substance behind it — 徒 (merely, only) + 有 (have) + 虚名 (empty name). The sharpest everyday formulation of what 正名 opposes. A famous general who wins no battles is 徒有虚名. A top university whose graduates learn nothing is 徒有虚名.
万里长征	wàn lǐ cháng zhēng	"the ten-thousand-li long march" — the 1934–1935 retreat of the Red Army; any prolonged and arduous undertaking — 万里 (ten thousand li , vast) + 长征 (long expedition, long march). The Chinese Communist Party's Long March (1934–1935) covered approximately 12,000 kilometers as the Red Army retreated from Nationalist forces. 万里 again functions as rhetorical superlative rather than measurement.
亡羊得牛	wáng yáng dé niú	lose a sheep, gain an ox — A related phrase (not a classical chengyu in the same sense) used to describe a small loss that leads to an unexpected larger gain: the silver lining formulation. Where 亡羊补牢 is about repair, 亡羊得牛 is about serendipitous compensation. The shared 亡羊 makes the pair memorable.
往来不绝	wǎng lái bù ju é	a constant stream of coming and going — uninterrupted traffic — 往 wǎng (to go toward) + 来 (to come) + 不绝 (without cessation). Describes a place or relationship with continuous back-and-forth movement: traffic, commerce, correspondence, social connection. 街上人来人往，好不热闹 "People coming and going on the street , what a lively scene!"
