韦编三绝	wéi biān sān ju é	the leather cord worn through three times — the archetype of devoted scholarship — 韦 wéi = leather; 编 biān = cord, binding; 三绝 sān jué = worn through three times. Classical books were written on bamboo strips bound together with cords of leather or silk.
闻鸡起舞	wén jī qǐ wǔ	hear the cock and rise to practice swordplay: diligent self-discipline — From the Jin Shu account of Zǔ Tì (祖逖), who woke at the rooster's first crow to drill with his sword. A praise word for someone who studies or trains relentlessly.
问鼎中原	wèn dǐng Zhōng yuán	to aspire to rule China — lit. "to inquire about the cauldrons of the Central Plains" — From a Spring and Autumn anecdote: the king of Chu asked Zhou envoys about the weight of the Nine Cauldrons (九鼎), signaling his ambition to displace the Zhou. 问鼎 became permanently associated with political ambition at the highest level.
五体投地	wǔ tǐ tóu dì	"five limbs to the ground" — to prostrate fully; to admire with complete humility — 五体 (the five parts of the body: the two knees, the two elbows or hands, and the head) plus 投地 (cast to the ground). It names the Buddhist full prostration in which all five touch the floor, the deepest bodily gesture of reverence.
雾里看花	wù lǐ kàn huā	"looking at flowers through mist" — seeing something obscurely, without clarity — 雾 (mist, fog) + 里 (inside, within) + 看花 (looking at flowers). The object of perception is real, but the medium prevents clear sight. Used for situations where the truth is obscured , murky political signals, ambiguous intentions, confusing circumstances.
喜欢就好	xǐ huan jiù hǎo	as long as you like it, that's enough — A warm everyday phrase, not a classical chengyu, used when giving a gift or making a choice for someone: if you like it, that's all that matters. It captures a generous, easygoing attitude and is one of the most common natural uses of 喜欢 in spoken Chinese.
先小人后君子	xiān xiǎo rén hòu jūn zǐ	petty men first, gentlemen after — settle terms up front — The contract-signing proverb: state the money, the split, and the deadlines with un-gentlemanly explicitness now, so that everyone can afford to be a gentleman later. Spoken at the start of partnerships between friends precisely because friendship is what it protects.
小菜一碟	xiǎo cài yī dié	a small dish on a plate: a piece of cake, an easy matter — Lit: small-dish-one-saucer. A 小菜 is a minor side dish, trivial next to the main course, so 小菜一碟 means a task so easy it is hardly worth mentioning. 这对我来说是小菜一碟 "this is a piece of cake for me."
小孩没娘，说来话长	xiǎo hái méi niáng , shuō lái huà cháng	the child has no mother: it's a long story — A 歇后语 (xiēhòuyǔ) two-part saying. The literal image, a motherless child, sets up the punchline 说来话长, "it's a long story." Spoken as a preface when the backstory is tangled and the speaker hardly knows where to begin.
小孩子家	xiǎo hái zi jiā	just a kid, only a child — Colloquial set phrase. The trailing 家 marks a category of person; said dismissively or fondly of someone too young to know better. "你一个小孩子家懂什么。"
小人得志	xiǎo rén dé zhì	the petty man gets his moment — power in unworthy hands — 得志 is achieving one's ambitions; attached to 小人 it names the special insufferability of the schemer promoted. The stock description of the new supervisor swaggering through a department, and of every court favorite in the historical novels.
笑里藏刀	xiào lǐ cáng dāo	a dagger hidden in a smile: friendly on the surface, treacherous beneath — Lit: laugh-inside hide-knife. Traced to the Tang minister Lǐ Yìfǔ, whose mild smile masked a ruthless heart. Names a person whose warmth is a weapon, the dark twin of 微笑.
星罗棋布	xīng luó qí bù	arrayed like stars, spread like chess pieces — dotted everywhere — 星罗 (netted like stars) + 棋布 (laid out like go stones). The standard descriptor for anything scattered densely across a landscape: lakes on a delta, islands in an archipelago, convenience stores in Shanghai. The two images, night sky and go board, are both grids the classical eye knew intimately.
兴亡教训	xīng wáng jiào xùn	the lessons of rise and fall — the standard analytical frame for the Taiping in modern Chinese historiography — 兴 (rise, flourish) + 亡 (fall, perish) + 教训 (lesson, warning). Not a fixed classical chengyu but a recurring analytical phrase in modern Chinese historical writing, particularly in discussions of the Taiping.
凶多吉少	xiōng duō jí shǎo	much ill fortune, little good — the outlook is grim — 多 and 少 as a probability report: on the scale of outcomes, the bad end is heavy. The stock phrase of thriller narration and worried families when someone has gone missing; expect it in every wuxia novel's darkest chapter.
兄弟姐妹	xiōng dì jiě mèi	brothers and sisters; siblings — The full set of siblings named in birth-order pairs: 兄 (elder brother), 弟 (younger brother), 姐 (elder sister), 妹 (younger sister). A staple four-character phrase, and one of the few common ones to carry 姐 directly.
削尖脑袋往里钻	xu ē jiān nǎo dai wǎng lǐ zuān	to sharpen one's head and bore in: to push to the front of the queue — A colloquial saying, not a classical 成语, paired here with 加塞儿 (jiāsāir, to cut the line). Mentioned to contrast the orderly 排队 ideal with the elbows-out reality of a crowded queue.
循序渐进	xún xù jiàn jìn	to advance step by step in order — Literally "following the sequence, advancing gradually", to progress in proper order without rushing. It is the principle behind any 先…然后…最后 plan: each step in its turn, the next only after the last. A watchword in learning and training.
一唱百和	yì chàng bǎi hè	one sings, a hundred answer: a single voice draws a crowd of agreement — One person 唱 starts the note and a hundred others 和 (hè) chime in. Used for an idea or call that instantly wins broad, eager support.
一唱一和	yì chàng yì hè	one sings, the other echoes: to chime in and back each other up — Originally one voice leads and another harmonizes (和 hè, to respond in tune). Now usually mildly negative: two people egging each other on or colluding to push the same line.
一点一滴	yì diǎn yì dī	a dot and a drop: bit by bit, little by little — Lit: one-dot-one-drop. The small increments by which patient effort accumulates. 一点一滴地积累 "to build up bit by bit."
一动不动	yí dòng bú dòng	not moving a muscle: perfectly still, motionless — Lit: one-move-not-move. The everyday phrase for total stillness, a person frozen in place, an animal holding its breath. The doubled 动 frames the stillness as the complete absence of motion.
一分钱一分货	yī fēn qián yī fēn huò	you get what you pay for — one fen of money, one fen of goods — The Chinese equivalent of "you get what you pay for." 分 fēn is both a fraction of a yuan and a measure of fairness. A practical consumer proverb that endorses quality over cheapness.
一个个	yí ge gè	one by one; one after another — The triple form stresses that things happen or are dealt with individually, in sequence. 学生一个个走进教室 "the students filed into the classroom one by one."
一毛不拔	yī máo bù bá	Not Give Even a Hair — Refuse to give even a single hair from your body. Mencius's phrase for the Yangist philosophy of radical self-preservation, and the word for a miser that it became.
一模一样	yì mú yí yàng	cast from one mould: exactly alike, identical — Lit: one-mould-one-shape. Said of two things so alike they might have come from a single cast: 这对双胞胎长得一模一样 "the twins look exactly alike."
一票否决	yí piào fǒu ju é	a single vote vetoes it: one negative vote overrides the rest; absolute veto power — 一票 yí piào = a single vote. 否决 fǒujué = to vote down; to veto. A decision rule in which one opposing vote is enough to block a proposal outright, the power a permanent member holds on the UN Security Council.
一石二鸟	yī shí èr niǎo	one stone, two birds — accomplish two goals with a single action — The Chinese and English idioms are structurally identical. The image of a single stone bringing down two birds appears to have developed independently in both traditions, though the Chinese form likely circulated in literary Chinese before modern contact. In use: 这个方案真是一石二鸟。
一时半会儿	yì shí bàn huì r	in a short time; for a little while — A colloquial set phrase, "one moment, half a spell," meaning a brief span of time, most often in the negative: 这事一时半会儿说不清 ("this can't be explained in a moment"). It builds directly on the 会儿 of 一会儿, stretching it into a fixed expression for "any time soon."
一字不漏	yī zì bù lòu	not missing a single character — word-for-word; complete and verbatim — 漏 lòu = to leak; to omit; to miss. 他把合同一字不漏地读了一遍。(He read the contract verbatim, not missing a single character.) Used to emphasize thoroughness, total quotation, or painstaking completeness. Related: 一字不差 yī zì bù chā (not one character wrong, absolutely accurate).
驿寄梅花	yì jì méi huā	sending plum blossoms by postal relay — a token of remembrance sent across great distance — From a Tang poem, but the Yuan postal relay system (驿站 yìzhàn) was the most elaborate in East Asian history, running thousands of stations across the empire with fresh horses. The phrase evokes distance, longing, and the slender thread of connection maintained across vast space.
有来有往	yǒu lái yǒu wǎng	"there is coming and there is going" — mutual exchange; reciprocity — 往 wǎng = to go; toward. 来 and 往 together make up the full back-and-forth of social exchange: gifts, visits, words, favors. A relationship characterized by 有来有往 is balanced and healthy , neither party gives without receiving. Often used to encourage reciprocity in social or diplomatic contexts.
有始有终	yǒu shǐ yǒu zhōng	to see a thing through to the end — Literally "to have a beginning and have an end", to finish what one starts rather than abandon it midway. The 终 of completion again, valued as a virtue of persistence. The opposite is 有始无终, all start and no finish.
鱼肉百姓	yú ròu bǎi xìng	to make fish-meat of the common people: to oppress and exploit them — Here 鱼肉 is a verb: to treat people as meat to be carved up. A classical condemnation of tyrannical officials, traceable to the Shǐjì. Still used to denounce abuse of power.
与时俱进	yǔ shí jù jìn	to advance together with the times — A classical phrase that became a political slogan in 2001, used to justify the Communist Party accommodating private enterprise, and criticized for justifying whatever the party currently does.
欲速则不达	yù sù zé bù dá	Haste Does Not Bring Success — Speed desired, arrival missed: Confucius on why rushing produces the opposite of what you intend.
原原本本	yuán yuán běn běn	from source and root: in full and exact detail, from start to finish — The doubled 原 (source) and 本 (root). To tell something 原原本本 is to give the whole account faithfully, leaving nothing out and adding nothing.
缘木求鱼	yuán mù qiú yú	"climb a tree to look for fish" — to seek something by completely wrong means; a futile approach — Lit: climb-tree-seek-fish. Here 缘 means "to climb along" rather than "karmic connection" , a completely different sense of the character (the edge-clinging, tree-climbing meaning). A cautionary idiom from Mencius. Used when someone's method is fundamentally unsuited to their goal.
载歌载舞	zài gē zài wǔ	both singing and dancing: to celebrate with song and dance — 载…载… is a classical frame meaning doing two things at once. Standard description of festive crowds at a 春节 (chūnjié) gala or village celebration.
振兴中华	zhèn xīng Zhōng huá	revitalize China — the rallying cry of national renewal — Attributed to Sun Yat-sen and the Revive China Society (兴中会). One of the most durable slogans in modern Chinese political history, used by the Nationalist government, the Communist government, and by Xi Jinping's "China Dream" (中国梦 Zhōngguó Mèng) rhetoric.
正中下怀	zhèng zhòng xià huái	hitting exactly what I hoped for — exactly what I wanted — Lit: exactly-hit-one's-heart/intention. Note: 中 here is zhòng (tone 4, to hit). The expression describes something that lands exactly where you hoped (a proposal, a result, a turn of events) as if it aimed directly at your wishes. 这个方案真是正中下怀 "This plan is exactly what I had in mind."
郑和下西洋	Zhèng Hé xià Xī yáng	Zheng He's expeditions to the Western Oceans — shorthand for Chinese maritime greatness — Used as a historical reference point in modern discussions of Chinese global engagement, maritime strategy, and the "peaceful rise" narrative. The voyages are cited as evidence that Chinese power projection has historically been tributary rather than colonial.
纸上谈兵	zhǐ shàng tán bīng	strategize on paper — armchair theorizing without practical ability — From the Battle of Changping (260 BCE): Zhao Kuo (赵括) could discuss military theory fluently but had no battlefield experience. His appointment as Zhao's commander, reversing the caution of the veteran Lian Po, led to the encirclement and massacre of 400,000 Zhao troops.
指指点点	zhǐ zhǐ diǎn diǎn	to point and gesture: to criticize or gossip about others behind their backs — The reduplicated 指点 zhǐdiǎn (to point out, to instruct) turns sour here: to keep pointing fingers and passing judgment on what is not one's business.
治国安邦	zhì guó ān bāng	"govern the state, pacify the realm" — the task of statecraft — 治国 (govern the state) + 安邦 (stabilize the realm; 邦 bāng, polity, domain). The paired formulation for the ruler's task: governing the state and maintaining peace across the whole domain. A classical political phrase that appears in pre-Qin texts.
置身事外	zhì shēn shì wài	to place oneself outside the matter: to stay aloof, keep clear of it — Lit: place-body-affair-outside. To keep oneself out of an affair, neither helping nor interfering. Often mildly critical, of someone who stands apart when they ought to be involved.
中流砥柱	zhōng liú dǐ zhù	a pillar standing firm in the middle of the current — a mainstay; steadfast support — Lit: mid-current-firm-pillar. The image of a rocky pillar in the middle of a fast-moving river, standing unmoved while the water rushes past. Used to describe a person, organization, or principle that holds firm and provides stability amid turmoil.
重蹈覆辙	chóng dǎo fù zhé	to follow the overturned cart's tracks again: to repeat a past disaster — Lit: again-tread-overturned-ruts. The chóng reading. To make the same ruinous mistake that wrecked others before, having failed to learn from it.
装模作样	zhuāng mú zuò yàng	to fake a shape and put on a show: to playact, to put on airs — Lit: feign-mould-make-shape. To strike a false pose, going through the motions of sincerity or effort while meaning none of it. A pointed way to call out pretense.
追本溯源	zhuī běn sù yuán	trace back to the root and seek the source — 追 (pursue) + 本 (root) + 溯 (go upstream) + 源 (source). Used for rigorous investigation of origins: of a word, a practice, a family line, a historical claim. The metaphor of going upstream to find the source of a river runs through classical Chinese thought about genealogy and textual scholarship.
的话	de huà	if; in the case that — The soft conditional: how 的话 marks a premise without committing to it.
多少 / 几	duō shao / jǐ	how many, how much — The two question words for quantity: 几 for small countable numbers and 多少 for larger or unknown amounts, why 几 needs a measure word and 多少 may skip it, and how to ask prices and ages.
还是 / 或者	hái shi / huò zhě	"or" in questions vs. statements — Chinese splits English "or" in two: 还是 offers a choice in a question, 或者 offers an alternative in a statement. Using the wrong one is a classic error.
来	lái / qù	去 · directional complements — 上来, 下去, 出去, 进来: the motion complement system mapped.
没有	méi yǒu	negation of possession and past action — The two most important negation characters, 不 and 没, and why they cannot be swapped.
起来	qǐ lái	directional complement: upward, inward, beginning — The most versatile directional complement in Mandarin: three distinct grammatical lives.
时态	shí tài	past, present, future — Mandarin verbs never change form: time words, 了, 过, 着, 在, and 会 do the work English does with conjugation.
是…的	shì … de	the cleft construction — Focus and past-detail marking: the construction that trips every learner.
太…了	tài le	too, excessively — The emphatic degree structure: 太…了 expresses excess and strong feeling, from complaints to enthusiastic praise.
一边…一边	yī biān yī biān	while doing; at the same time — Simultaneous actions: the frame for describing two things happening at once.
一点儿 / 有点儿	yì diǎn r / yǒu diǎn r	a little — The classic confusion pair: 有点儿 comes before an adjective and leans negative ("a bit too…"), while 一点儿 comes after and is neutral ("a little more").
一下	yí xià	the verb-softener — The verbal-quantity word that makes an action brief and casual, how verb + 一下 softens a request, and how it differs from verb reduplication and from the time-word 一会儿.
一样 / 像	yí yàng / xiàng	same and similar — How to say two things are the same (A 跟 B 一样) or alike (A 像 B), and how this differs from the unequal comparison with 比.
在	zài	location, existence, and the progressive — One of the busiest words in Mandarin: the verb "to be at," the location coverb "at/in," and the progressive marker for ongoing action.
正反问	zhèng fǎn wèn	A-not-A questions — Chinese asks yes-no questions by offering both answers: 去不去 is "going, not going?", and the pattern replaces 吗 with built-in neutrality.
*把他喜欢	* bǎ tā xǐ huān	WRONG — stative/emotional verbs cannot take 把 — Stative verbs (是, 有, 像), mental-state verbs (喜欢, 爱, 恨, 讨厌, 知道, 认识, 觉得), and perception verbs (看见, 听见, 闻到) cannot be used in the 把 construction. The 把 construction requires an agent who deliberately acts on a concrete object with a physical or tangible result.
*我把他看见了	* wǒ bǎ tā kàn jian le	WRONG — unaffected objects cannot take 把 — The object of the 把 construction must be affected by the action , it must undergo a change, be moved, be consumed, be transformed, or be disposed of. Perception and cognition verbs (看见 see, 听见 hear, 闻到 smell) involve objects that are registered but not physically affected.
阿是穴	ā shì xu é	Ashi points — Ashi points: tender spots with no fixed location, needled wherever the patient says "Ah, yes, that is it!" when pressed. Not on any meridian map, but recognized in classical texts as clinically valid.
爱国	ài guó	patriotic; to love one's country — 爱 ài (love) + 国 guó (country). Patriotism. A deeply freighted term in Chinese political culture: 爱国主义 àiguó zhǔyì is patriotism as ideology, 爱国人士 patriotic personages. In everyday use, simply "to love one's country."
爱好	ài hào	hobby; interest; pastime — 爱 ài (to love) + 好 hào (to be fond of). A thing one loves doing , a hobby or passion. One of the first personal vocabulary items learners need: 你有什么爱好？(What are your hobbies?)
爱护	ài hù	to cherish and protect; to take loving care of — 爱 ài + 护 hù (to protect; to guard). To love something by protecting it. Used for public property (爱护环境), younger people (爱护学生), and cultural heritage. A civic and ethical register.
爱面子	ài miàn zi	to be very concerned with face; to care about appearances — 爱 (to love; to value; to be attached to). 爱面子 describes someone who places unusually high value on face , so much so that their behavior is shaped by the desire to maintain appearances even when it conflicts with practical sense.
鹌鹑蛋	ān chún dàn	quail egg
八大菜系	bā dà cài xì	the Eight Great Cuisines — every regional banquet runs on baijiu
八卦	bā guà	the eight trigrams; gossip — 八 bā + 卦 guà (trigram; divinatory figure). The eight three-line figures of yin and yang at the foundation of the Yijing, used in divination, feng shui, and cosmology. In colloquial modern usage the same word means gossip: 八卦新闻 (tabloid news), 别八卦了 (stop gossiping).
八卦镜	bā guà jìng	The ba-gua mirror , an octagonal mirror surrounded by the eight trigrams (八卦) of the I Ching, hung above doorways to deflect negative energy or bad feng shui directed at the entrance. The mirror reflects harmful influences back toward their source.
八旗	bā qí	Eight Banners (Manchu military system)
八仙	bā xiān	the Eight Immortals — 八 bā + 仙 xiān (immortal; transcendent). The Eight Immortals of Daoist legend, a band of figures each with a distinctive emblem and power, including Lü Dongbin, He Xiangu, and Iron-Crutch Li.
八月	bā yu è	August; the eighth month — 八 bā + 月 yuè (month). August in the solar calendar. The fifteenth day of the eighth lunar month is the Mid-Autumn Festival (中秋节), one of the most important holidays of the year, when families gather to admire the full moon and eat mooncakes.
八字	bā zì	birth horoscope (Four Pillars)
把包放下	bǎ bāo fàng xià	put the bag down — 放 fàng (to place; put) + 下 xià (down; into a lower position). A simple directional 把 sentence , the object (bag) is moved to a lower location. 下 here signals downward movement or lowering into position. The 把 foregrounds the deliberate, targeted nature of the placing.
把窗户打开	bǎ chuāng hu dǎ kāi	to open the window (strike it into an open state) — 打 dǎ (to hit; to strike , used as a general action verb with many objects) + 开 kāi (open; apart; into an open state). The combination 打开 means "open" , the object is moved into an open or separated state. 开 as a resultative complement implies separation, opening, or dispersal.
把门关上	bǎ mén guān shàng	to close the door (shut it into the closed position) — 关 guān (to close) + 上 shàng (up; into a closed/attached state). Here 上 is a resultative complement indicating that the door has been moved into the closed, shut position.
把椅子搬进来	bǎ yǐ zi bān jìn lái	bring the chair inside (move it in-coming direction) — 搬 bān (to move heavy things; to transport) + 进来 jìnlái (in-coming; into this space). The directional complement 进来 specifies both the direction (in) and the orientation toward the speaker (来). The 把 construction signals the chair is a definite, specific object being moved as a deliberate act.
白白	bái bái	in vain; for nothing
白菜	bái cài	Chinese cabbage; bok choy family — 白 bái (white) + 菜 cài (vegetable). The pale-stalked cabbage that anchors northern winter cooking. So cheap and plentiful that 白菜价 "cabbage price" means dirt cheap.
白吃	bái chī	to eat for free; to freeload
白毫	bái háo	white down — the fine hairs on young buds
白话	bái huà	vernacular Chinese; plain speech
白酒	bái jiǔ	baijiu — the spirit most often paired with a hotpot meal
白米	bái mǐ	white rice; polished rice
白切鸡	bái qiē jī	White-Cut Chicken
白天	bái tiān	daytime
白衣	bái yī	white-robed (form of Guanyin)
百花	bǎi huā	a hundred flowers; the 1956 campaign
百货	bǎi huò	all kinds of goods; department store — 百 (all kinds of) + 货 huò (goods, merchandise). 百货公司 (bǎihuò gōngsī, "all-goods company") is the standard Chinese term for a department store.
百家	bǎi jiā	a hundred schools; all schools of thought
百科	bǎi kē	encyclopedia; all branches of knowledge — 百 (all) + 科 kē (branch of study, department, subject). The term for encyclopedia: 百科全书 (bǎikē quánshū, "complete book of all branches of knowledge"). 百度百科 is the major Chinese-language wiki encyclopedia.
百年	bǎi nián	a hundred years; a full generation; a lifetime — 百 (hundred) + 年 nián (year). A century, but also a metonym for a full human span or a generational unit of history. 百年大计 (bǎinián dàjì, "a great plan for a hundred years") means a long-term fundamental strategy.
百年屈辱	Bǎi nián Qū rǔ	Century of Humiliation
百姓	bǎi xìng	the common people; ordinary citizens — 百 (hundred; all) + 姓 xìng (surname). The full range of surnames = everyone who is not nobility. The warm, slightly literary word for "the people." 老百姓 (lǎobǎixìng) adds 老 (old, familiar) for a more affectionate, colloquial tone. Political speeches say 人民; everyday speech says 老百姓.
败坏	bài huài	to corrupt, ruin
斑马	bān mǎ	zebra
帮我翻译一下	bāng wǒ fān yì yí xià	please translate this for me — For your interpreter, or for the English-speaking salesperson when the engineer goes too fast. Using an interpreter for one hard passage and Chinese for the rest is common and costs no face.
帮我一下	bāng wǒ yí xià	give me a hand; help me out — A model of 一下 as a politeness softener. The bare 帮我 ("help me") is blunt; adding 一下 frames it as a small, quick favor and sounds far friendlier. Note that 一下 can follow the object here (帮我一下) because 帮 takes a person directly. The same softening works across requests: 看一下, 等一下, 问一下.
榜样	bǎng yàng	role model; good example to follow — 榜 bǎng (a posted list, a board) + 样 yàng (model). A person held up as a pattern for others: 树立榜样 "to set an example," 学习的榜样 "a model to learn from."
棒喝	bàng hè	the shout-and-blow — spontaneous awakening techniques — 棒 bàng (staff, blow) + 喝 hè (shout, roar). Two of the most distinctive Chan teaching methods: a sudden shout (associated especially with the master Linji 临济, whose shout 喝 became the name of the Rinzai school) and a strike with a staff (associated with Deshan 德山).
包间	bāo jiān	private dining room
背后	bèi hòu	behind someone's back; in secret — 背 bèi (back of the body; to turn one's back on) + 后 (behind). The space behind a person's back , the zone they cannot see. In figurative use, 背后 means to act covertly, without someone's knowledge or consent. 在背后说人坏话 (to speak ill of someone behind their back) is a strong social criticism.
被打了	bèi dǎ le	got hit; was beaten (by an unspecified agent) — When the agent is unknown, irrelevant, or deliberately omitted, the 被 construction works without naming who did the action. The 被 alone signals passive and adversarial meaning.
被动	bèi dòng	passive
本纪	běn jì	Basic Annals — rulers and dynasties — Twelve chapters chronicling the central line of power: the legendary sage-kings, the Xia, Shang, and Zhou, the Qin, and the Han emperors down to Wu.
本来	běn lái	originally; at first; (it goes without saying that) — 本 běn (root; origin) + 来 lái (come). What was the case from the start, before things changed: 我本来想去 "I originally meant to go (but didn't)." It often carries a quiet sense that the original state was the natural or proper one.
本领	běn lǐng	skill; capability
本人	běn rén	oneself; the person in question; I (formal) — 本 běn (this; one's own) + 人 rén (person). The very person concerned, as opposed to a representative or a rumor. On forms and in formal speech it means "I myself": 本人同意 "I personally agree."
本事	běn shi	ability; skill; what one is capable of — 本 běn (root; one's own) + 事 shì (matter). The capacities a person carries in themselves, their real skill rather than their luck or connections: 有本事 "to have what it takes." A close synonym is 本领 běnlǐng.
本质	běn zhì	essence
本子	běn zi	notebook; exercise book; pad — 本 běn (volume) + 子 zi (suffix). A blank or ruled book to write in. Here 本 takes the toneless noun-suffix 子, exactly as in 桌子 and 房子.
笨蛋	bèn dàn	idiot, fool — 笨 (bèn, stupid, clumsy) + 蛋: a fool or dimwit. Among close friends it softens into teasing, even affection, but aimed at a stranger it lands as a real insult.
鼻子	bí zi	nose
边关	biāng uān	border pass; frontier checkpoint — 边 (biān, border; edge) + 关 (guān, pass; strategic gate). The fortified checkpoints through the wall where military control, taxation, and passage were managed.
便宜一点	pián yi yì diǎn	a bit cheaper — The basic request, best softened. 价格有点高 (the price is a bit high) states the problem without accusing; 还有空间吗 (is there still room?) invites a counteroffer without naming one.
辫子	biàn zi	the Manchu queue hairstyle
标点	biāo diǎn	punctuation
标记	biāo jì	a marker; a label; to mark — 标 biāo (to label, to mark out) + 记 jì (a mark). A marker or tag that identifies a thing, and the act of placing it: 在地图上标记位置 "to mark a location on a map."
别人	bié rén	other people; someone else (colloquial) — 别 bié (other; different; another) + 人 rén. The default, everyday word for "other people" in spoken Mandarin. Neutral register, very high frequency. Used in advice, comparisons, complaints, and social commentary; the most natural choice whenever you mean "other people" in conversation.
宾馆	bīng uǎn	hotel, guesthouse — 宾 bīn (guest) + 馆. A house for guests, that is, a hotel. The word keeps the old hospitality sense of 馆 right on its sleeve.
并非	bìng fēi	is not in fact; really is not — 并 bìng (actually; emphatic) + 非 fēi (not). A firm, slightly formal "is not at all," used to push back against an assumption: 并非如此 "that is not the case," 并非不可能 "not in fact impossible."
病毒	bìng dú	virus — 病 + 毒 (dú, poison): a "sickness poison," the standard word for a biological virus and, by extension, a computer virus. The same term covers the flu and the malware on your hard drive.
病房	bìng fáng	hospital ward, sickroom — 病 + 房 (fáng, room): the room where patients are kept, a hospital ward. It pairs naturally with 病床 (bìngchuáng, hospital bed), bringing the original sickbed of the radical 疒 full circle.
病假	bìng jià	sick leave
病菌	bìng jūn	germ, pathogenic bacterium — 病 + 菌 (jūn, bacterium, fungus): a disease-causing germ. Where 病毒 names a virus, 病菌 names the bacterial culprit, the everyday word a parent uses when telling a child to wash their hands.
病人	bìng rén	patient, sick person — 病 + 人 (rén, person): a sick person, the general word for a patient in a hospital or clinic. It names the person by their condition rather than their role, unlike 患者 (huànzhě), which is the more clinical register used in medical writing.
玻璃被打破了	bō lí bèi dǎ pò le	the glass was broken — 打 dǎ (to hit) + 破 pò (broken; shattered , resultative complement). The 破 is the result that makes the sentence grammatical. Breakage is typically undesirable , a window, a vase, a screen. The 被 construction here makes the glass the focus: it is the thing that suffered the breaking.
不错	bú cuò	pretty good, not bad; correct, right — 不 bù (not) + 错 cuò (wrong). Two readings from one shape: as praise, "quite good, not bad" (成绩不错 "good grades"); as confirmation, "right, correct" (一点不错 "exactly right"). Note the tone sandhi: 不 shifts to bú before the falling 错.
不但	bú dàn	not only
不得不	bù dé bù	cannot but; have no choice but to — 不得不 (not-obtain-not) = double negation creates strong obligation: "cannot not do" = must do, compelled by circumstances. Stronger than 必须 bìxū in the sense of reluctant necessity. The speaker has no choice.
不得了	bù dé liǎo	extremely; terrible; amazing (intensifier) — 不 + 得了 déliǎo (can be concluded; can be over). "Cannot be concluded" → extreme beyond measure. Used after adjectives as a strong intensifier: 好得不得了 = incredibly good. Also standalone: 不得了！= This is unbelievable / It's a disaster! (depending on context).
不都	bù dōu	not all; not every one (partial negation) — 不 precedes 都. The negation falls on the universal claim: "it's not the case that all of them do X." Some do, some don't. This is logically weaker than 都不 and much more common in careful, qualified speech.
不断	bú duàn	continuously; without stopping
不妨	bù fáng	might as well; no harm in
不过	bù guò	"However" or "but" (softer) — "However" or "but" (softer). The default conversational pivot. Lighter than 但是, which can feel direct or even adversarial in spoken contexts. 不过 carries a gentler "that said…" tone, ideal for adding a qualification.
不会吧	bù huì ba	"No way!" or "you can't be serious — "No way!" or "you can't be serious." Expresses disbelief, often at bad news or an unwelcome outcome. The 吧 softens it from an outright denial into an incredulous reaction.
不见了	bú jiàn le	to be missing, to have disappeared
不仅	bù jǐn	not merely (formal pair for 而且)
不来了	bù lái le	not coming anymore; decided not to come — 不…了 is the negation of sentence-final 了 , it signals a reversal of an expected or prior state. The 了 here paradoxically signals a new situation: the new situation is "not doing X anymore." Extremely common for announcing changed plans, habits that have been given up, or states that have ended.
不良率	bù liáng lǜ	defect rate — Percentage of defective units; 良品率 or 良率 (liánglǜ) is the yield, its complement. 不良品 (bùliángpǐn) and 次品 (cìpǐn) are defective goods. Agree an acceptable rate in the contract and what happens above it.
不食人间烟火	bù shí rén jiān yān huǒ	"not eating the smoke and fire of the human world" — otherworldly; too aloof to be of this world — Literally: "does not eat the smoke and fire of 人间." Originally said of immortals (仙 xiān) and deities, who subsist on pure air rather than cooked food and are therefore free of the desires and needs that tie mortals to this world.
不是	bú shì	is not; to not be; it's not that… — The negation of 是 , always 不是, never 没是 (unlike verbs of action, which can be negated with 没 for past tense).
不是我说的	bú shì wǒ shuō de	it wasn't me who said it (correcting the agent) — The classic defensive use of 不是…的: denying agency for a known past statement or action. Both parties know something was said , the 不是…的 corrects the attribution. This is one of the most practically useful forms of the construction in daily social life.
不是昨天买的	bú shì zuó tiān mǎi de	it wasn't bought yesterday (correcting the time) — Time correction: both speakers know the item was bought , the correction is about when. 不是昨天 denies the specific time; the speaker may then supply the correct time with 是…的.
不舒服	bù shū fu	Unwell — Unwell: "not comfortable." The all-purpose symptom, the polite sick-day excuse, and the doctor's opening question in negative form: 哪里不舒服?
不太	bú tài	not very; not too; not quite — 不 bù (not) + 太 tài (very). The gentle negative, softer than a bare 不: 不太喜欢 "don't really like it," 不太方便 "not very convenient." The standard polite way to decline or downplay.
不太会喝酒	bú tài huì hē jiǔ	not much of a drinker — Decide before the dinner whether you drink, and say it early with a reason that leaves no one at fault: health, medication, an early meeting, or driving. Once you have drunk one toast, refusing the next is harder.
不同	bù tóng	different; not the same; difference — 不 bù (not) + 同 tóng (same). The standard word for "different": 不同的意见 "differing opinions." As a noun it names the difference itself: 两者的不同 "the difference between the two."
不退转	bù tuì zhuǎn	non-regression
不喜欢	bù xǐ huan	to not like; to dislike — Negate with 不 before the verb. To soften, insert 太: 不太喜欢 ("don't really like") is gentler and more polite than a flat 不喜欢. For a stronger feeling, 讨厌 tǎoyàn means "to find annoying, to dislike intensely."
不孝	bù xiào	unfilial
不锈钢	bù xiù gāng	stainless steel — Grades by number: 201 is cheaper and less corrosion-resistant, 304 is the common food-contact grade, 316 adds molybdenum for better resistance to acids and salt. 材质 (cáizhì) is "material" in the sense of grade and composition.
不要	bú yào	don't (prohibition)
不一样	bù yí yàng	different
菜单	cài dān	menu; (computing) a menu — 菜 cài (dish) + 单 dān (list; slip). The list of dishes a restaurant offers, and by extension the drop-down 菜单 of a computer program. 看菜单 "to look at the menu."
菜鸟	cài niǎo	(slang) novice; newbie; noob — 菜 cài (here: weak, lousy) + 鸟 niǎo (bird). A fledgling, hence a beginner who is still bad at something, especially in gaming and the workplace. Self-deprecating or teasing: 我是个菜鸟 "I'm a total newbie."
菜系	cài xì	cuisine school; culinary lineage — Cuisine system , the term for a recognized regional cooking tradition. The 八大菜系 (eight great cuisine systems) are the canonical eight, though culinary scholars recognize anywhere from four to dozens of regional traditions.
苍白	cāng bái	pallid, ashen, sickly white
曾国藩	Zēng Guó fān	Zeng Guofan, scholar-general
差不多	chà bu duō	almost; about the same
差错	chā cuò	a slip, an error; a mishap going wrong — 差 chā (off, deviating) + 错 cuò (error). A small slip or accidental error, and by extension an unforeseen mishap: 万一出了差错 "in case something goes wrong." Both halves mean "off the mark," doubling the sense of deviation.
拆开	chāi kāi	to tear open; to unwrap
常常	cháng cháng	often; frequently; time and again — The reduplicated 常. A common, conversational adverb for frequent action. Its negative is usually 不常 bùcháng "not often," since 不常常 is avoided.
常识	cháng shí	common sense; general knowledge — 常 cháng (constant; common) + 识 shí (knowledge). The basic knowledge that holds across a community, what one is expected to know: 生活常识 "everyday know-how," 缺乏常识 "to lack common sense."
厂房	chǎng fáng	factory building
厂家	chǎng jiā	manufacturer
唱歌	chàng gē	to sing — 唱 (sing) + 歌 (song). The social institution attached is KTV (卡拉OK), where 唱歌 is what colleagues, classmates, and business contacts do together; declining every song is borderline antisocial, and one rehearsed number is sound survival strategy.
唱片	chàng piàn	record; disc (vinyl, album) — 唱 chàng (to sing) + 片 piàn (a thin flat piece). A singing-disc: the physical record that holds recorded sound. 唱片公司 chàngpiàn gōngsī is a record label. The word predates and survives digital formats.
唱腔	chàng qiāng	Vocal style , the melodic and tonal patterns of a particular role type. Different roles use different vocal registers and melodic conventions.
钞票	chāo piào	banknote: paper money; cash — 钞 chāo (paper money) + 票 piào (slip; note). A banknote, paper currency, cash in hand. This compound shows 票 at its purest: a printed slip of paper that stands for money. Closely related is 支票 zhīpiào (a cheque), where 支 zhī (to pay out) marks the slip as an instrument of payment.
嘲笑	cháo xiào	to mock; to ridicule; to jeer at — 嘲 cháo (to taunt; to deride) + 笑 xiào (laugh). To laugh at someone with scorn. This is the cruel edge of 笑: the same sound aimed downward at a target. Takes a direct object, the person mocked.
炒蛋	chǎo dàn	scrambled eggs — 炒 (chǎo, to stir-fry) + 蛋: eggs broken into a hot wok and pushed around until they set in soft curds. The same 炒 powers most Chinese cooking, so 炒蛋 reads as plainly as English "scrambled eggs."
车间	chē jiān	workshop, shop floor — Named by process: 冲压车间 stamping, 焊接车间 welding, 注塑车间 injection molding, 喷涂车间 painting, 组装车间 assembly, 包装车间 packing. 无尘车间 (wúchén chējiān) is a clean room.
车票	chē piào	travel ticket: a ticket for a bus, train, or coach — 车 chē (vehicle; car; train) + 票 piào (slip; ticket). A ticket for ground transport: a bus ticket, a train ticket, a coach ticket. The slip that proves you have paid your fare and may board. By the same pattern, 机票 jīpiào is a plane ticket and 船票 chuánpiào is a boat ticket.
车同轨	chē tóng guǐ	carts made to the same track — Qin standardized the gauge of cart axles so that all wheeled vehicles fit the same ruts. The empire-wide road system, including the 直道 (zhídào, "straight roads") radiating from the capital Xianyang, depended on this.
车站	chē zhàn	station; bus or train stop — 车 (chē, vehicle) + 站 (stop): the place where buses or trains halt and passengers board. The base word for any ground-transit station; specify with 火车站 huǒchēzhàn (train station) or 汽车站 qìchēzhàn (bus station).
吃醋	chī cù	to be jealous (in love); to eat vinegar — 吃 chī + 醋 cù (vinegar). Eating vinegar: romantic jealousy. The origin story: a Tang dynasty concubine had to drink vinegar as punishment when she defied orders, giving rise to this vivid metaphor. Exclusively romantic jealousy (not professional envy, which is 嫉妒 jídù).
吃饭	chī fàn	To eat a meal , literally "eat cooked grain — To eat a meal , literally "eat cooked grain." Used as a general term for eating any meal regardless of whether rice is served. 吃 means "to eat"; 饭 means cooked grain or, by extension, any meal.
吃亏	chī kuī	to suffer a loss; to be at a disadvantage; to get the short end — 吃 chī + 亏 kuī (deficit; shortfall). To eat a shortfall , to come out worse, to be cheated or disadvantaged. 吃亏是福 chī kuī shì fú (suffering loss is a blessing) is a common proverb: the Daoist wisdom that accepting disadvantage builds character.
吃了	chī le	ate; has eaten (the action is complete) — The most basic post-verb 了 construction. The 了 immediately follows the verb and signals that the action was completed. With a bare monosyllabic object, the 了 sits between verb and object. With a quantified object, this placement is even clearer and more natural.
吃了吗	chī le ma	have you eaten
吃人	chī rén	Eating people, man-eating , Lu Xun term for the cannibalistic logic he claimed to find underlying Confucian social morality.
持有	chí yǒu	to hold (a position, certificate)
充分	chōng fèn	ample; full; sufficient — 充 chōng (to fill) + 分 fèn (measure; share). Filled to the proper measure, fully sufficient: 充分的准备 "ample preparation," 充分利用 "to make full use of." Note the fèn reading.
重庆小面	Chóng qìng xiǎo miàn	Chongqing Spicy Noodles
除非	chú fēi	unless; only if — 除 chú (except) + 非 fēi (not). Literally "except if not," hence "unless." Often paired with 否则 fǒuzé or 才 cái: 除非下雨，否则我们就去 "we'll go unless it rains."
厨房	chú fáng	kitchen — 厨 (chú, cooking, the cook) plus 房. The room where food is prepared. One of the most frequent 房 compounds in daily life, and usually among the first room words a learner needs.
触动	chù dòng	to touch; to stir
川菜	chuān cài	Sichuan cuisine — 川 chuān (Sichuan) + 菜 cài (cuisine). The cooking of Sichuan, famous for the numbing-spicy 麻辣 málà of Sichuan peppercorn and chili. 麻婆豆腐 and 回锅肉 are classic 川菜 dishes.
穿过	chuāng uò	to pass through; to cross — 穿 (to pass through) + 过 (guò, across, a directional complement). The verb of moving through and out the other side, a crowd, a forest, a tunnel. 过 marks the full traverse, in one side and out the far side.
穿上	chuān shàng	to put on (and have it on) — 穿 (to thread on) + 上 (shàng, up/onto, here a result complement). The complement 上 marks the garment as now in place. 穿上 stresses the completed act of getting it on, against bare 穿 which can describe the habit of wearing.
穿梭	chuān suō	to shuttle back and forth
穿透	chuān tòu	to penetrate; to pierce through — 穿 (to bore through) + 透 (tòu, thoroughly/through). Two synonyms stacked for force, used for light, sound, water, or radiation breaking all the way through a barrier. The technical word in physics and medicine for penetration.
穿衣	chuān yī	to put on clothes; to dress — 穿 (to thread through) + 衣 (yī, clothing). A verb-object pair, the plainest way to say "get dressed." 衣 is the general word for what covers the upper body, so 穿衣 frames dressing as passing the body into a garment.
穿着	chuān zhe	wearing; dressed in (current state) — 穿 (to put on clothing) + 着 = the dressed state is in force. This is among the most natural uses of durative 着, because clothing occupies a position continuously. The same pattern applies to 戴着 (wearing a hat/glasses), 拿着 (holding), 带着 (carrying/bringing).
创作	chuàng zuò	to create (art)
吹牛	chuī niú	to brag, to talk big — 吹 (chuī, to blow) + 牛 (ox). From the full phrase 吹牛皮 (chuī niúpí, blow up an ox-hide), once said of herders inflating a hide skin-raft by lung power: big puff, little substance.
慈禧太后	Cí xī Tài hòu	Empress Dowager Cixi
此外	cǐ wài	apart from this; besides; furthermore — 此 cǐ (this) + 外 wài (outside). A formal written connector, "outside of this," used to add a further point: 此外，还有一点需要注意 "besides, there is one more point to note."
次要	cì yào	secondary; minor
从来	cóng lái	always; never (with negation); at all times up to now — 从 cóng (from; since) + 来 lái (coming; up to now). "Coming from the past all the way here," from some starting point up to the present moment. Almost always paired with negation: 从来不 (never; has never) / 从来没有 (never; has never happened).
从现在起	cóng xiàn zài qǐ	from now on — A fixed frame meaning "starting from now." 从 (from) plus 现在 plus 起 (to start). It marks a turning point or a resolution: 从现在起，我要早睡 ("from now on, I'll go to bed early"). A close variant is 从现在开始, with 开始 (to begin) in place of 起.
存在	cún zài	to exist; existence
错过	cuò guò	to miss (a chance, a train, a person) — 错 cuò (crosswise) + 过 guò (to pass). To pass by crosswise rather than meet: two things slip past each other and the moment is lost. Used for chances, trains, and people: 错过机会 "to miss a chance," 错过末班车 "to miss the last bus."
错误	cuò wù	mistake, error; wrong, mistaken — 错 cuò (wrong) + 误 wù (to err, mistake). Both a noun, the mistake itself, and an adjective, mistaken or incorrect: 犯了一个错误 "made a mistake," 错误的决定 "a wrong decision." The standard, slightly formal word for an error of fact or judgment.
错字	cuò zì	a wrongly written character; a typo — 错 cuò (wrong) + 字 zì (character). A character written incorrectly, whether mis-formed by hand or mistyped: 这篇作文有很多错字 "this essay has many wrong characters." A 错别字 cuòbiézì folds in 别字, the right-looking but wrong character.
打江山	dǎ jiāng shān	to conquer the realm; to win power through struggle — 打 dǎ (to strike; to fight; to build through effort) + 江山 (the realm). Always implies hard-won, usually military achievement , you do not inherit 江山, you take it. The character 打 carries physical force: the same verb used for punching, striking, and waging war.
打开	dǎ kāi	to open up (a closed object)
打扫	dǎ sǎo	to clean; to sweep
打算	dǎ suàn	to plan; to intend
打样	dǎ yàng	to make a sample; sampling — The verb for producing samples before an order. 打样费 (dǎyàngfèi) is the sampling fee, often refunded against the first order; 打样周期 is how long sampling takes.
打折	dǎ zhé	to give a discount — 折 counts tenths of the price you pay, not the amount taken off. 八折 means you pay eight tenths, a 20% discount. Learners hear "eight" and think "80% off." In a negotiation 点 (diǎn, a percentage point) is more common than 折 for small moves.
打字	dǎ zì	to type; to type characters — 打 dǎ (to hit; to strike) + 字 zì. You are "striking" the characters into existence: the same 打 used in 打球 (play ball by striking it), 打电话 (make a phone call by striking a connection), 打鼓 (beat a drum).
大悲咒	Dà bēi Zhòu	Great Compassion Mantra
大乘	dà chéng	Mahayana — the Great Vehicle
大慈大悲	dà cí dà bēi	great loving-kindness, great compassion — the full bodhisattva quality — 大 (great) + 慈 (loving-kindness) + 大 (great) + 悲 (compassion). The epithet most closely associated with Guanyin: 大慈大悲观世音菩萨. The reduplication of 大 before each component gives it the character of a liturgical formula.
大地	dà dì	the great earth; the land
大都	Dà dū	Dadu (Yuan capital, modern Beijing)
大多数 · 少数	dà duō shù shǎo shù	the majority · the minority — 大多数 (the great majority) and 少数 (the few) partition a set by proportion: 大多数人都同意 (most people agree), with the majority phrase taking 都 like any near-universal. 少数 appears in the fixed civic vocabulary 少数民族 (minority nationalities, China's official term for its 55 recognized non-Han groups).
大方	dà fang	generous; natural; poised; not stingy — 大 dà + 方 fāng (square; proper; generous). Having the big-square manner: poised, natural, generous, not petty. Used for both personality (generous with money) and bearing (natural and composed, not awkward). A high compliment in Chinese social contexts.
大概	dà gài	approximately; probably; roughly — 大 dà (large, rough) + 概 gài (general outline; to level off). A rough outline: approximately, probably. One of the most natural hedges in spoken Mandarin: 大概三点 "around three o'clock."
大哥	dà gē	eldest brother; big bro — 大 (big) + 哥. Literally the eldest brother in the family. By extension it addresses the leader of a group, the boss of a gang, or any older man you want to flatter into helping you.
大哥大	dà gē dà	brick phone; top boss — 大哥 (big brother) + 大 again. First a nickname for the 1980s brick mobile phone, the status symbol of bosses; by extension the kingpin or undisputed number one in any field.
大货	dà huò	bulk goods, the production run — Floor slang for the main order as opposed to samples. 大货生产 is bulk production, 大货样 a unit pulled from it. The word appears in nearly every merchandiser message: 大货已经上线了 means your order is on the line.
大家	dà jiā	everyone; everybody (warm, inclusive) — 大 dà (big) + 家 jiā (family; home). Literally "the big family," used for "everyone present" or "all of us." Much warmer and more conversational than 人们. The go-to word for addressing or including a present group: 大家好！(Hello everyone!).
大甲妈祖绕境	Dà jiǎ Mā zǔ Rào jìng	the Dajia Pilgrimage — one of the world's largest religious events — The annual Dajia Mazu Pilgrimage (大甲妈祖绕境进香) is a nine-day, eight-night procession covering approximately 340 kilometers through central and southern Taiwan, beginning from the Zhenglong Temple (镇澜宫 Zhènlán Gōng) in Dajia, Taichung.
大姐	dà jiě	eldest sister; ma'am — 大 (big) plus 姐 marks the eldest of several sisters at home. On the street it doubles as a friendly, respectful way to address a woman somewhat older than the speaker.
大人	dà rén	adult, grown-up
大师	dà shī	master; grandmaster
大使馆	dà shǐ guǎn	embassy — 大使 dàshǐ (ambassador, lit. great envoy) + 馆. The ambassador's official house abroad. A smaller mission led by a consul is a 领事馆 lǐngshìguǎn, a consulate.
大势至	Dà shì zhì	Mahāsthāmaprāpta bodhisattva
大侠	dà xiá	great knight-errant; heroic wanderer; the wuxia protagonist archetype — 大 dà (great) + 侠 xiá (chivalrous fighter; knight-errant). The archetypal wuxia protagonist: skilled, wandering, honorable, righting wrongs without official sanction. The 大侠 has no fixed home , the entire 江湖 is their domain.
大小	dà xiǎo	size — Big-small: the size scale, the template for the whole antonym-compound family. 这双鞋大小正好 means the shoes fit exactly. Its siblings 长短 (length), 轻重 (weight), and 高矮 (height) work identically, and 轻重 doubles as "relative importance," what a tactless person 不知轻重 fails to weigh.
大笑	dà xiào	to laugh loudly
大兴安岭	Dà Xīng ān lǐng	Greater Khingan Mountains
大学	dà xu é	university; college — and the Confucian Great Learning — 大 dà (great; large) + 学 xué (learning). The modern term for university. But 大学 is also the title of one of the Four Books of Confucianism (四书 Sìshū) , the 大学 (Great Learning), a short text that outlines the program of self-cultivation leading from individual virtue to social order to universal peace…
大学 · 中庸	Dà xu é Zhōng yōng	Great Learning · Doctrine of the Mean — Originally chapters of the 礼记, elevated to independent classic status by Zhu Xi. 大学 Dàxué outlines the nested self-cultivation program (修身齐家治国平天下).
大篆	dà zhuàn	Greater Seal Script , the broad category encompassing the varied script forms of the Zhou dynasty bronze inscriptions, Stone Drum text, and oracle bone script.
单身汉	dān shēn hàn	a bachelor; a single man — 单身 dānshēn (single, unmarried) + 汉 hàn (man). An unmarried man living on his own. The 汉 here is purely the colloquial "man," with none of the heroic ring of 好汉.
担担面	dān dān miàn	Dan Dan Noodles
但是	dàn shì	but; however
诞生	dàn shēng	to be born (grand things)
蛋白	dàn bái	egg white; protein — 蛋 (egg) + 白 (bái, white): literally the white of the egg. In science it extends to protein in general, and 蛋白质 (dànbáizhì) is the standard word for dietary protein.
蛋糕	dàng āo	cake — 蛋 (egg) + 糕 (gāo, a steamed or baked flour cake): a cake gets its name from the eggs whipped into the batter. The word covers birthday cakes, sponge cakes, and the slice you buy at a café.
蛋壳	dàn ké	eggshell
蛋挞	dàn tà	egg tart
刀削面	dāo xiāo miàn	Knife-Shaved Noodles
捣蛋	dǎo dàn	to make trouble, to be naughty
地藏	Dì zàng	Kṣitigarbha — guardian of hell and savior of the dead — Sanskrit Kṣitigarbha ("Earth Womb/Treasury"). The bodhisattva who specifically vowed to save beings in hell realms and not enter nirvāṇa until all hells are empty. Associated with Mount Jiuhua 九华山 in Anhui. His vow: 地狱不空，誓不成佛 (If the hells are not empty, I vow not to become a Buddha).
地点	dì diǎn	place; location; site; venue — 地 dì (ground) + 点 diǎn (point). A specific point on the ground, the precise spot where something happens: a meeting place, an event venue, the scene of an incident. More exact than 地方 dìfang.
地点焦点	dì diǎn jiāo diǎn	place focus — "it was [there] that…" — When the focused element is a place, the construction answers "where?" — again, about a past event both speakers know happened. The place phrase typically includes 在 and appears immediately after 是.
地方	dì fang	place; spot; (also) local, regional — 地 dì (earth; ground) + 方 fang. The all-purpose word for "place," from a physical spot to an abstract point in an argument (这个地方我不同意 "on this point I disagree"). With full tone, 地方 dìfāng means "local" as opposed to "central": 地方政府 local government.
地府	dì fǔ	the underworld; the realm of the dead — 地 (earth; ground) + 府 (government office; official residence). The underground realm of the dead, governed by 阎王 Yánwáng (Yama, the king of the underworld) and his bureaucracy.
地名	dì míng	place names
地球	dì qiú	the Earth; the globe — 地 dì (earth) + 球 qiú (ball; sphere). Lit. "earth ball." The modern scientific term for the planet , a coinage that dates to the late 19th century, when Chinese translators had to name the heliocentric model.
地铁	dì tiě	subway
地下	dì xià	underground; beneath the earth; covert; subterranean — 地 dì (earth; ground) + 下 xià (below; under). Literally: beneath the earth. Extended to mean "covert" or "underground" in the political sense , 地下组织 (underground organization), 地下工作 (underground/clandestine work). 地铁 dìtiě (subway; underground railway , lit. "underground iron") uses the same 地.
地支	dì zhī	Earthly Branches
的 · 得 · 地	de	the three de's
登山	dēng shān	mountaineering; to climb
等候	děng hòu	to wait; to await — 等 děng (to wait) + 候 hòu (to await). A more formal, written-leaning word for waiting than plain 等. Common on signs and announcements: 请耐心等候 "please wait patiently."
敌人	dí rén	enemy
弟子	dì zǐ	disciple; student of a master — 弟 dì (younger brother) + 子 zǐ. The pupil who studies under a teacher and carries the lineage forward, used of philosophical, martial, and religious schools alike.
第二	dì èr	second; number two — 第 dì (ordinal) + 二 èr (two). The ordinal "second." The same pattern extends without limit: 第三 third, 第四 fourth, 第十 tenth. The number after 第 is just the ordinary cardinal, unchanged.
第二人称	dì èr rén chēng	second person
第几	dì jǐ	which (in order); what number in a series — 第 dì (ordinal) + 几 jǐ (how many; which). The question form that asks for a position rather than a quantity. The expected answer is always another 第 form: 第三 (the third), 第七 (the seventh).
第三人称	dì sān rén chēng	third person
第一	dì yī	first; number one — 第 dì (ordinal) + 一 yī (one). The ordinal "first." Note the live tone-sandhi: 一 yī shifts to second tone before a fourth-tone syllable, so it is read dì-yí in 第一个 but stays dì-yī before a pause or another tone.
第一流	dì yī liú	first-rate; first-class; top-tier — 第一 dì-yī (first) + 流 liú (stream; class). Of the highest grade or quality: 第一流的大学 "a first-rate university." A close synonym of 一流 yīliú, with 第 adding a touch of formality.
第一人称	dì yī rén chēng	first person
点菜	diǎn cài	to order dishes (at a restaurant) — 点 diǎn (to choose; to dot) + 菜 cài (dish). To pick dishes off a menu and order them. The host usually does the 点菜 for the table. For the many senses of 点 see the 点 entry.
点火	diǎn huǒ	to ignite; to light a fire
点赞	diǎn zàn	To like (a post) — To like (a post): tap-approve. 赞 alone means to praise; 点赞之交 ("a likes-only friendship") names the acquaintance you never see but always like.
丢面子	diū miàn zi	to lose face; to be publicly embarrassed — 丢 diū (to lose; to throw away , the losing of something you once had). To lose face is not merely to feel embarrassed privately; it is to have one's standing visibly diminished in the eyes of others. The public, witnessed nature of face-loss is what makes it significant.
东方	dōng fāng	the east; the Orient — 东 dōng (east) + 方 fāng (direction). The eastern direction, and by extension "the East" as a cultural sphere. Its pair is 西方 xīfāng, the West. The four directions all take 方: 南方 the south, 北方 the north.
东林党	Dōng lín Dǎng	Donglin Partisans (reform faction)
东周	Dōng Zhōu	Eastern Zhou
冬天	dōng tiān	winter
动机	dòng jī	motivation; driving motive
动作	dòng zuò	movement; action; gesture; motion (of the body) — 动 dòng (to move) + 作 zuò (to do; to make). A single physical movement or gesture: 这个动作很难 "this move is hard," 动作快一点 "move faster." Also "action" in film: 动作片 dòngzuòpiàn (action movie).
独唱	dú chàng	solo (vocal)
读者	dú zhě	reader (of a publication); readership — 读 dú + 者 zhě (one-who). The standard term for the reader of a book, magazine, or newspaper. 亲爱的读者 "Dear reader" is the conventional opening for a letter from the editor.
肚子	dù zi	belly, stomach
度众生	dù zhòng shēng	to liberate sentient beings
端正	duān zhèng	upright; proper
对不对	duì bu duì	right? correct? — The teacher's tag and the explainer's checkpoint, punctuating reasoning the way English "right?" does. The listener's murmured 对对对 in reply is the sound of a Chinese conversation agreeing with itself.
对不起	duì bu qǐ	I'm sorry — lit. "I cannot face you" — 对 duì (to face; toward) + 不起 buqǐ (cannot rise to; cannot face). Literally "I cannot face you," an expression of guilt and apology for having let someone down. The response is 没关系 méi guānxi (it doesn't matter) or 没事 méishì (no problem).
对方	duì fāng	the other party; the opposite side — 对 duì (facing; opposite) + 方 fāng (party). The other side in a conversation, negotiation, contest, or relationship. Its pairs are 双方 shuāngfāng (both parties) and 我方 wǒfāng (our side).
对话	duì huà	dialogue; conversation; to hold talks — 对 duì (facing; mutual) + 话 huà (speech). Speech exchanged face to face. Concrete dialogue in a textbook or film, and figurative dialogue between sides: 和平对话 "peace talks."
对我来说	duì wǒ lái shuō	"For me" or "as far as I'm concerned — "For me" or "as far as I'm concerned." Frames a statement as true from your perspective without claiming it is universally true. Often paired with adjectives of preference or difficulty: 对我来说, 这个太难了 ("for me, this is too hard").
多大	duō dà	how old; how big
多多少少	duō duō shǎo shǎo	more or less; to some extent — The question word reduplicated into a hedge: some amount on the scale, unspecified but nonzero. It softens claims politely, which makes it a favorite of careful speakers: everyone at the meeting 多多少少 had objections, though few voiced them.
多亏	duō kuī	thanks to; fortunately (because of) — 多 (much) + 亏 (kuī, to be indebted to, to owe). "Much-owed-to" , fortunately thanks to X. Expresses relief that something good or fortunate happened because of another person or circumstance: 多亏你帮忙，要不然就麻烦了 (thanks to your help, otherwise it would have been a problem). Colloquial and warm in register.
多么	duō me	how; what a (exclamatory) — 多 (how much) + 么 (question/exclamation suffix). An exclamatory intensifier: 多么美啊 (how beautiful!); 多么幸运 (how lucky). More literary than colloquial 多 alone. Used in song lyrics, poetry, and formal speeches to express admiration or strong feeling.
多少钱	duō shao qián	how much (money); what's the price — The universal way to ask a price. Because cost can be any amount, you use 多少, never 几: 这件衣服多少钱？("how much is this piece of clothing?"). The answer uses money measure words: 块 kuài (yuan, colloquial), 毛 máo (ten cents), 分 fēn (cent). 一共多少钱 ("how much altogether") totals a bill.
多元	duō yuán	diverse; pluralistic; multi-element — 多 (multi-) + 元 (yuan, element, source, unit). Multi-source, diverse in origin. 多元文化 (multiculturalism); 多元社会 (pluralistic society); 多元经济 (diversified economy).
儿歌	ér gē	nursery rhyme — 儿 (child) plus 歌 (song). A children's song, the first 歌 most native speakers ever learn.
儿子	ér zi	son — 儿 ér (child, son) + 子 zi (suffix). The standard word for a son. Its pair is 女儿 nǚ'ér (daughter), which keeps 儿 but not 子.
二哥	èr gē	second eldest brother — 二 (two) + 哥. The second of the brothers, ranked after 大哥. The numbering system is literal: each older brother is addressed by his birth order, never by name within the family.
二胡	èr hú	erhu; two-stringed bowed fiddle — 二 èr (two) + 胡 hú (a category of instruments of non-Han origin, literally "barbarian"). The erhu has exactly two strings, which defines both its name and its voice: the two strings produce the characteristic close-interval resonance that makes the instrument sound like a human throat.
二话	èr huà	a second word; objection; demurral — 二 èr (two; a second) + 话 huà (speech; words). Always appears in the negative: 没有二话 or 不说二话 means to comply without objection or complaint, without a second word.
二十八宿	èr shí bā xiù	The 28 Lunar Mansions — The 28 Lunar Mansions: the primary coordinate system of Chinese astronomy, dividing the ecliptic and celestial equator into 28 unequal stations corresponding to the moon's daily progress around the sky.
二维码	èr wéi mǎ	QR code
二月	È r yu è	February; the second month — 二 èr (two) + 月 yuè (moon; month). Chinese months are purely positional: the first month is 一月, the second is 二月, through 十二月 (December). There are no separate month names.
凡间	fán jiān	the mortal realm; the ordinary world (as seen from above) — 凡 fán (mortal; ordinary; not immortal) + 间 (space; realm). The mortal world as defined by its contrast with the celestial.
繁体	fán tǐ	traditional (character form)
繁体字	fán tǐ zì	traditional Chinese characters — 繁 fán (complex; numerous) + 体 tǐ (form; body; style) + 字 zì. The original, more structurally complex character forms that preserve more visual information about component etymology. Used in Taiwan, Hong Kong, Macau, and many overseas Chinese communities.
反常	fǎn cháng	abnormal; unusual
反正	fǎn zhèng	anyway; in any case; regardless — 反 fǎn (reverse) + 正 zhèng (right). Literally "reverse-or-right," whichever way it turns out: the all-purpose "anyway, the outcome is the same." 反正我不去 "anyway, I'm not going."
返回	fǎn huí	to return; go back
饭馆	fàng uǎn	restaurant, eatery — 饭 fàn (cooked rice, a meal) + 馆. A modest, sit-down place to eat, the neighborhood diner rather than a banquet hall. Often said 小饭馆 for the cheap, friendly kind.
方便	fāng biàn	convenient; to make things easy for; (euphemism) to use the toilet — 方 fāng + 便 biàn (convenient; suitable). Easy and convenient, of a time, a place, or an arrangement. 方便面 fāngbiànmiàn is instant noodles, literally "convenient noodles." As a polite euphemism it also means to relieve oneself.
方程	fāng chéng	Equation — Equation. The term is two thousand years old, taken from the chapter of the Nine Chapters that solved systems of linear equations with counting rods. 解方程 (jiě fāngchéng) is to solve an equation.
方面	fāng miàn	aspect; side; area; regard — 方 (direction; side) + 面 (face; surface). A particular "side" or "face" of a topic , one of the aspects of a multi-sided issue. Extremely common in academic, journalistic, and analytical Chinese. 在这方面 (in this regard), 各个方面 (all aspects), and 经济方面 (in terms of economics) are standard expressions.
方式	fāng shì	way; manner; mode; style — 方 fāng (way) + 式 shì (form; pattern). The manner or mode in which something is done, broader and more abstract than 方法: 生活方式 lifestyle, 付款方式 payment method.
方式焦点	fāng shì jiāo diǎn	manner focus — "it was [by X] that…" — When the focused element is a manner, means, or instrument, 是…的 answers "how?" The manner phrase sits after 是 and before the main verb. This is a natural way to specify or contrast the means of travel, communication, or action.
方位	fāng wèi	cardinal position; bearing; orientation — 方 (region) + 位 (position). More technical than 方向: a fixed cardinal bearing rather than a heading. Used in geography, military, navigation, and feng shui contexts. 方位词 (fāngwèicí) is the grammatical category of "position words" , 上 above, 下 below, 前 front, 后 back, 左 left, 右 right.
方位禁忌	fāng wèi jìn jì	Directional Taboos — feng shui and orientation — Spatial orientation is governed by 风水 fēngshuǐ (feng shui, lit. "wind-water"), the art of placing buildings, graves, and furniture to harmonize with the flow of qi 气. The main door of a house should not directly face a staircase, a toilet, or a knife-like corner of another building.
房东	fáng dōng	landlord — 房 (house) plus 东 (dōng, east; by old convention the host stands on the east side). The person who owns the dwelling and rents it out. The 东 element marks the host or owner, the same 东 found in 股东 (gǔdōng, shareholder).
房间	fáng jiān	room (inside a building) — 房 fáng (room; building-section) + 间 jiān (space between; a measure word for rooms). The standard neutral word for a room in a house, hotel, or office. 三室两厅 = three bedrooms two living rooms (standard real-estate description).
房客	fáng kè	tenant, lodger
房子	fáng zi	house, building — 房 (building) plus the neutral-tone noun suffix 子 (zi). The default everyday word for a house or any standalone building you live in. Concrete and countable: you buy one, rent one, or build one.
房租	fáng zū	rent — 房 (house) plus 租 (zū, to rent, rent money). The money paid to occupy a dwelling. Pair it with 交 (jiāo, to hand over): 交房租 means to pay the rent.
访问	fǎng wèn	to visit; to call on; to interview; (computing) to access — 访 fǎng (to call on) + 问 wèn (to ask after). A formal visit made to a person, place, or, by modern extension, a website or file. 国事访问 "a state visit," 访问网站 "to access a website."
飞白	fēi bái	Flying white — Flying white: a dry-brush effect where the hairs of the brush separate, leaving streaks of paper showing through the ink. A controlled accident, difficult to achieve and highly prized.
飞虫	fēi chóng	flying insect
飞机	fēi jī	airplane, aircraft — 飞 (to fly) plus 机 (machine): the flying machine. This is the everyday word for any fixed-wing aircraft, from a jetliner to a small propeller plane.
飞鸟	fēi niǎo	a bird in flight, flying bird — 飞 (flying) plus 鸟 (bird): a bird on the wing. The word keeps the character close to its pictographic root, naming the very creature the glyph was drawn from.
飞翔	fēi xiáng	to soar, to circle in flight
飞跃	fēi yu è	a leap, a great advance
飞涨	fēi zhǎng	to rise sharply (prices) — 飞 (flying) plus 涨 (to rise, of water or prices): prices climbing as if in flight. The standard word for a steep, fast jump in cost.
非常	fēi cháng	extremely; very; unusual; extraordinary — 非 fēi (not) + 常 cháng (ordinary). As an adverb before an adjective it means "extremely" (非常高兴 "extremely happy"); as an adjective before a noun it keeps the older sense "extraordinary, exceptional" (非常时期 "extraordinary times").
非攻	fēi gōng	opposition to aggressive war — Mozi distinguished between defensive warfare (permitted) and aggressive conquest (condemned as categorically unjust).
非遗	fēi yí	intangible cultural heritage (abbrev.)
非议	fēi yì	to criticize; to reproach; censure — 非 fēi (to fault) + 议 yì (to discuss; opinion). To talk of something as wrong, to find fault with it: 无可非议 "beyond reproach, nothing to criticize." Here 非 acts as a verb, "to deem wrong."
分担	fēn dān	to share (a cost) — The compromise line. Offering it yourself, once fault is clear, buys goodwill for the next order.
分开	fēn kāi	to separate; to part; to divide up — 分 fēn (divide) + 开 kāi (open; apart). To pull two things apart or keep them distinct: 把垃圾分开 "sort the rubbish," 我们分开走 "let's go separately."
分类	fēn lèi	to classify; to sort into categories — 分 fēn (divide) + 类 lèi (kind; category). To divide things into kinds: 垃圾分类 "waste sorting," 图书分类 "cataloguing books."
分配	fēn pèi	to allot; distribute
分数	fēn shù	score; mark; (math) fraction — 分 fēn (point) + 数 shù (number). A test score, and in mathematics a fraction. Both senses share the idea of a number standing below or against a whole.
分钟	fēn zhōng	minute (of time) — 分 fēn (a divided unit) + 钟 zhōng (clock). A minute as a span of time: 等五分钟 "wait five minutes." To tell the time on the clock face, 分 alone is enough: 两点十五分 "two fifteen."
分子	fēn zǐ	Molecule — Molecule: divided unit. The same word, differently stressed, means "member of a group" in political vocabulary (知识分子, intellectuals), a coincidence of compounding worth knowing before it confuses.
份子钱	fèn zi qián	share money — the obligatory wedding gift
烽火台	fēng huǒ tái	beacon tower; signal-fire platform
奉天承运	fèng tiān chéng yùn	receiving heaven's mandate and carrying forward its fortune — the opening formula of imperial edicts — 奉 fèng (to receive; to hold reverently) + 天 tiān (heaven) + 承 chéng (to carry on; to inherit) + 运 yùn (fortune; cycle; destiny). The four characters that opened every imperial edict from the early Ming dynasty onward.
敷衍了事	fū yǎn liǎo shì	to dispose of perfunctorily
府第	fǔ dì	a mansion; an official's grand residence — 府 fǔ (official residence; manor) + 第 dì (ranked dwelling). The stately home of a high official or noble family. Literary and historical; you meet it in novels and on heritage plaques rather than in conversation.
附体	fù tǐ	spirit possession — the spirit descending into the shaman — 附体 (spirit possession , lit. "spirit attaches to body") is one of two modes of shamanic trance in Tungusic tradition. In the other mode (魂游 húnyóu , soul-flight), the shaman's own soul leaves the body and travels to the spirit world while the body remains passive.
搞笑	gǎo xiào	funny; hilarious
哥儿俩	gēr liǎ	the two brothers; the pair of bros
哥哥	gē ge	elder brother — The base word: 哥 doubled, the second syllable toned down to neutral. Standard everyday term for one's own older brother, and a child can also use it for an unrelated older boy.
哥们儿	gē men r	buddy, bro, mate — 哥 + 们 (plural) + the Beijing 儿 ending. Close male friends, sworn-brother style. Singular or plural, it marks loyalty between guys; northern and casual, common in Beijing speech.
歌唱	gē chàng	to sing (in praise); singing as an art — 歌 gē (song) + 唱 chàng (to sing). More formal and elevated than 唱歌. Used for singing as a discipline and for singing in celebration: 歌唱家 gēchàngjiā is a professional vocalist, and 歌唱祖国 means to sing the praises of one's homeland.
歌剧	gē jù	opera — 歌 (song) plus 剧 (drama). A sung drama; the term used for Western-style opera, as distinct from 京剧 (jīngjù) Peking opera.
歌曲	gē qǔ	song (as a work) — 歌 (song) plus 曲 (tune, melody). 歌 leans on the lyrics, 曲 on the music; together they name a song as a finished piece. Slightly more formal than a bare 歌.
歌星	gē xīng	singing star
个别	gè bié	individual; a small number of; exceptional — 个 gè (single) + 别 bié (separate; other). Either "one by one, individually" (个别谈话 "to talk to people one at a time") or "only a few, exceptional" (个别情况 "a few isolated cases").
个人	gèr én	individual; personal; in one's own view — 个 gè (single) + 人 rén (person). The single person set against the group, and by extension "personal, private": 个人观点 "a personal opinion," 个人信息 "personal information." Its foil is 集体 jítǐ (the collective).
个体	gè tǐ	an individual (entity); a single member — 个 gè (single) + 体 tǐ (body; entity). A single unit within a larger whole, used in biology and economics alike: 个体差异 "individual differences," 个体户 "a self-employed business owner."
个子	gè zi	height; stature; build (of a person) — 个 gè + 子 zi (noun-suffix). A person's height or physical build: 高个子 "tall build," 矮个子 "short build," 个子很高 "very tall." Used of the whole frame of a person, not a measured number.
给面子	gěi miàn zi	to give face; to show respect; to honor someone's standing — 给 (to give) + 面子. To actively enhance or protect someone else's face. Can mean: attending their event (your presence honors them), publicly praising them, deferring to them in front of others, or not challenging them when they are wrong , to spare their embarrassment.
根本	gēn běn	fundamental; basic; (with a negative) at all — 根 gēn (root) + 本 běn (root). The two words for "root" doubled. As an adjective, fundamental or essential: 根本问题 "the fundamental issue." As an adverb with a negative, it means "not at all": 我根本不知道 "I had no idea whatsoever."
攻读	gōng dú	to pursue (a degree, a field of study); to read-for — 攻 gōng (to attack; to apply oneself to) + 读. The verb for actively pursuing advanced study , a master's, a doctorate, a specialty. The 攻 conveys the sense of going at it, attacking the subject rather than passively absorbing.
宫保鸡丁	gōng bǎo jī dīng	Kung Pao Chicken
恭喜	gōng xǐ	to congratulate; congratulations — 恭 gōng (respectful) + 喜 xǐ (joy). To respectfully share in someone's happiness. Doubled as 恭喜恭喜 it is the warm spoken "congratulations," and 恭喜发财 gōngxǐ fācái is the standard New Year greeting wishing wealth.
共和	gòng hé	republic; shared harmony
共同	gòng tóng	common; shared; jointly; together — 共 gòng (together; shared) + 同 tóng (same). Held in common by all parties: 共同的目标 "a shared goal," 共同努力 "to strive together." Stronger and more formal than 一起.
顾面子	gù miàn zi	to consider face; to save face for someone; to protect dignity — 顾 gù (to consider; to look after; to take into account). 顾面子 means to take face into consideration when making decisions or acting , whether one's own face or someone else's. More deliberate and conscious than simply 给面子. Suggests a thoughtful navigation of face dynamics.
顾问	gù wèn	adviser; consultant — 顾 gù (to look after; to consider) + 问 wèn (to ask). The person one turns to and asks for counsel: 法律顾问 "legal adviser," 技术顾问 "technical consultant." Literally the one whom you regard and question.
怪不得	guài bu de	"That explains it!" Synonym for 难怪 with the same function — "That explains it!" Synonym for 难怪 with the same function. Slightly more drawn out and emphatic. 怪不得他不来 ("that's why he didn't come").
关闭	guān bì	to close (formal)
关帝	Guān Dì	Emperor Guan — deified general, god of war and righteousness — Guan Yu 关羽 (d. 220 CE), the historical Three Kingdoms general celebrated for his loyalty and righteousness 义 yì, was progressively deified over the Tang, Song, and Ming dynasties, eventually receiving the title 关圣帝君 (Holy Emperor Guan).
关汉卿	Guān Hàn qīng	greatest Yuan playwright
关怀	guān huái	to show concern and care for (warm, often institutional)
关机	guān jī	to power off (a device); to switch off a phone
关键	guān jiàn	key, crucial, the decisive hinge
关联	guān lián	correlation; connection; to be associated with — 关 (connection) + 联 (to join; to link). More formal and analytical than 关系 , used for logical correlations, data relationships, and systematic connections. Common in academic, research, and analytical writing. 相互关联 xiānghù guānlián = mutually correlated; interconnected.
关门	guān mén	to close the door; (of a shop) to close for the day or for good
关起来	guān qǐ lái	to lock up; to shut in; to imprison
关系网	guān xi wǎng	network of connections; relationship network — 网 wǎng = net; network. 关系网 is the spider's web of personal connections , who you know, who they know, and the overlapping obligations and trust that run through the web.
关系重大	guān xi zhòng dà	of great importance; high-stakes; consequential — A common fixed expression used to characterize something as having major consequences or importance. 重大 zhòngdà = major, significant. Used in formal registers to signal that a decision, situation, or event carries real weight and should be treated with corresponding seriousness.
关羽	guān yǔ	Guan Yu — the archetype of loyalty and honour — The red-faced sworn brother of Liu Bei, towering in loyalty and martial valour, who refuses every bribe and honour Cao Cao offers to keep him.
关注	guān zhù	to pay attention to; to follow closely; attention; concern — 关 (to concern; to connect) + 注 (to pour; to focus , water pouring into a single point). Focused, sustained attention toward something important. In digital contexts, 关注 means to follow someone's account (social media).
官方	guān fāng	official; governmental
贯穿	guàn chuān	to run through, to permeate
滚蛋	gǔn dàn	get lost, scram — 滚 (gǔn, to roll) + 蛋: a blunt order to get out. The image is of telling someone to roll away like a dropped egg. It is rude and forceful, used only in anger.
国歌	guó gē	national anthem — 国 (nation) plus 歌 (song). The official song of a country, sung standing at ceremonies and medal podiums.
国际	guó jì	international — 国 guó + 际 jì (boundary; border; between). "Between countries." The standard word for anything international: 国际机场 international airport · 国际关系 international relations · 国际化 internationalization.
国外	guó wài	abroad
果肉	guǒ ròu	fruit pulp, flesh of fruit — 果 (guǒ, fruit) + 肉 (flesh). The soft edible part between the skin and the pit, the meat of the fruit.
过错	guò cuò	fault, wrongdoing
过分	guò fèn	excessive; going too far
过关	guò guān	to pass the pass; to clear a hurdle, exam, or test
过来	guò lái	come over, come here
过年	guò nián	to celebrate New Year
过去	guò qù	the past; to pass; to go over to — 过 guò (to pass; to cross; to go beyond) + 去 qù (away). As a noun: the past , what has passed away from the present. As a verb: to go over to (move across and away to a location). Both meanings share the same root image: time or a person crossing and moving beyond the current point.
过一会儿	guò yí huì r	after a while; in a little while — "After a spell passes," used to push something a short way into the future: 过一会儿再打给你 ("I'll call you back in a little while"). 过 (to pass) marks the elapsing of the short time. A close, even softer relative is 等会儿 / 待会儿 ("in a bit, later"), the ready phrase for "hold on / later."
还好	hái hǎo	not bad; passable; fortunately (context-dependent) — The most versatile of the three hedges. As a standalone response to "how was it?" or "how are you?", 还好 reads as "fine, okay, not bad." As an opener to a clause, it shifts to "fortunately, luckily" , the speaker is noting that things didn't go as badly as they might have.
还是	hái shi	or (in a choice question) — Used in questions that offer the listener a choice between two (or more) options: A 还是 B？It signals "which one?" and asks for a pick. A choice question with 还是 does not also take 吗, since 还是 already makes it a question. The options can be nouns, verbs, or whole clauses.
还是算了	hái shì suàn le	forget it; never mind; on second thought, no
还有一点	hái yǒu yī diǎn	"One more thing" or "and another point — "One more thing" or "and another point." Marks the addition of a further item to a list of points you are making. Common in explanations and arguments where you are building up to a conclusion.
孩童	hái tóng	child; young children (literary) — 孩 paired with 童 (child, youngster). A bookish, slightly elevated word for children, common in writing and formal speech rather than casual talk.
孩子	hái zi	child; kid; one's offspring — 孩 (child) plus the neutral-tone suffix 子, which turns many one-syllable nouns into stable everyday words. 孩子 is the default word for a child and also the word a parent uses for a son or daughter of any age.
含有	hán yǒu	to contain; to include
寒热	hán rè	cold and heat (TCM diagnosis)
寒山	Hán shān	Cold Mountain (Tang Chan poet)
韩非	Hán Fēi	Han Fei — greatest Legalist theorist, student of Xunzi — 韩非 (280–233 BCE): a prince of the state of Han who studied under Xunzi and became the most systematic theorist of Legalism 法家. His text, the 韩非子 Hánfēizǐ, synthesizes earlier Legalist thought and provides the fullest account of governance through law 法, technique 术, and power 势.
韩非子	Hán Fēi zǐ	Han Feizi; the Legalist theorist
汉服	Hàn fú	Traditional Han Dress
汉人	Hàn rén	Northern Chinese — third class — Former subjects of the Jin dynasty, including Han, Jurchen, Khitan, and Koreans. Conquered earlier, treated with somewhat less suspicion than southerners. Had somewhat greater access to lower official positions.
汉学	hàn xu é	Sinology; the study of Chinese language and civilization — 汉 Hàn (Chinese) + 学 xué (study, learning). The academic study of Chinese language, texts, and culture, especially as pursued by scholars outside China. A 汉学家 hànxuéjiā is a sinologist.
汉元素	hàn yuán sù	"Han elements" — commercially inspired garments that aren't structurally hànfú
汉族	Hàn zú	the Han ethnicity; the Han people — 汉 Hàn (of Han) + 族 zú (clan, ethnic group). The majority ethnic group of China, named after the Han dynasty. Set against the 少数民族 shǎoshù mínzú, the country's many minority peoples.
航天	háng tiān	Spaceflight — Spaceflight: "sailing the heavens," on the model of 航海 (seafaring). 航天员 is the official word for China's astronauts; the industry is 航天工程, space engineering.
毫不	háo bù	not at all, not in the least
好不好	hǎo bu hǎo	okay? — the agreement-seeking tag — Appended to a proposal, it softens a request into a negotiation: 我们明天再说，好不好? Its siblings 行不行 and 可不可以 climb the same ladder of politeness; children deploy 好不好 serially until granted.
好吃	hǎo chī	delicious
好处	hǎo chù	benefit; advantage; good point — 好 hǎo (good) + 处 chù (place; aspect; point). The good points of something: its advantages. Contrast: 坏处 huàichù (bad points; disadvantages).
好汉	hǎo hàn	a brave fellow; a stout-hearted hero — 好 hǎo (good) + 汉 hàn (fellow). A bold, admirable man, the heroic outlaw or stalwart of old novels. The saying 好汉不吃眼前亏 "a wise fellow does not take a loss he can avoid" shows its everyday use.
好喝	hǎo hē	tasty (to drink) — 好 (good) + 喝 (drink): pleasant to drink. Mandarin splits "tasty" by mouth-action, so liquids are 好喝 while solid food is 好吃 (hǎo chī), good to eat.
