好看	hǎo kàn	good-looking; interesting to read
好了	hǎo le	done; ready; okay; alright (change of state) — 好 + 了 (change-of-state marker) = has become ready/done/okay. One of the most frequent two-syllable expressions in spoken Mandarin. Announces completion, signals readiness, and in reduplicated form (好了好了) means "alright, enough, okay."
好朋友	hǎo péng you	good friend; close friend — A close friend, the most common way to mark closeness. 好 hǎo (good) stacks directly in front. For an even closer bond there is 知己 zhījǐ, "one who knows me," a soulmate-friend who truly understands you, a notion with deep roots in classical Chinese friendship.
好奇	hào qí	curious; inquisitive — 好 hào (fond of) + 奇 qí (the strange, the unusual). Fond of the unusual , curious. 好奇心 hàoqíxīn = curiosity (the heart of fondness for strangeness).
好听	hǎo tīng	pleasant-sounding; beautiful (of music, voice, speech) — 好 hǎo (good; well) + 听 (to listen). The auditory parallel of 好看 hǎokàn (nice-looking). Applied to music, voices, songs, and speech.
好闻	hǎo wén	nice to smell
好像	hǎo xiàng	seems like; as if
好兄弟	hǎo xiōng dì	good brothers — polite euphemism for wandering hungry ghosts — 好 hǎo (good) + 兄弟 xiōngdì (brothers). The Taiwanese term of respect used in place of 鬼 during Ghost Month. Addressing spirits as "good brothers" acknowledges their presence without the provocation of calling them ghosts; it is also an appeal to fraternal feeling.
喝酒	hē jiǔ	to drink alcohol — 喝 (drink) + 酒 (liquor). The default verb for drinking alcohol of any kind, from beer to baijiu. Central to banquet culture, where 喝酒 carries social obligation.
喝咖啡	hē kā fēi	to drink coffee — 喝 (drink) + 咖啡 (coffee). Like 喝茶, the phrase doubles as a soft social invitation: "改天一起喝咖啡" means let's meet up sometime.
喝水	hē shuǐ	to drink water — 喝 (drink) + 水 (water). The first sentence most learners build with this character, and the everyday reminder a Chinese host or parent will repeat constantly.
喝汤	hē tāng	to drink soup
喝醉	hē zuì	to get drunk — 喝 (drink) + 醉 (drunk), a resultative compound: the 醉 is the result of the 喝. Often expanded to 喝醉了 (hē zuì le) for the completed state, "got drunk".
和氏璧	Hé Shì Bì	the legendary jade disc
和谐	hé xié	harmony; harmonious
荷包蛋	hé bāo dàn	fried egg, poached egg
很多	hěn duō	a lot; many
很多 · 不少	hěn duō bù shǎo	many, a lot: plain and understated — 很多 is the everyday "many, a lot": 很多人 (many people), 很多问题 (many questions). 不少, literally "not few", says the same thing through understatement, and often carries a note of mild surprise: 他认识不少人 (he knows quite a few people).
后方	hòu fāng	the rear; behind the front line — 后 (rear) + 方 (direction; side; region). The military and formal register word for the rear area behind a front line or forward position. Contrasted with 前方 qiánfāng (the front, the forward area). Also used metaphorically for the support structure behind any endeavor: family, logistics, home base.
后宫	hòu gōng	the imperial harem; the rear palace — 后 (rear; and the empress sense) + 宫 (palace). The residential complex at the back of the imperial enclosure housing the emperor's wives and concubines. The term plays on both senses of 后: the rear palace (spatially, at the back) and the palace of the 后 (the empress and her female hierarchy).
后来	hòu lái	afterward; later; subsequently — 后 (after) + 来 (to come; to arrive). "What came after" , subsequent events in a narrative or sequence. 后来 always refers to the past relative to a reference point already established: you set the scene, then 后来 picks up the story.
后门	hòu mén	back door; backdoor; unofficial channel — 后 (rear) + 门 (door; gate). Literally the door at the back of a building. Figuratively: an unofficial, informal, or improper channel for getting something done.
后面	hòu miàn	behind; at the back; in the rear — 后 (behind) + 面 (face; side; direction). The most common spatial word for "behind" in everyday Mandarin. Used for physical position relative to a reference point. Interchangeable with 后边 hòubian in many contexts; 后面 is slightly more formal.
后天	hòu tiān	the day after tomorrow — 后 hòu (after; behind; later) + 天 tiān (day). Chinese has dedicated words for two days in either direction of today: 前天 qiántiān / 昨天 zuótiān / 今天 jīntiān / 明天 míngtiān / 后天 hòutiān. Beyond that, you count: 三天后 sān tiān hòu (three days later).
后者	hòu zhě	the latter
候车	hòu chē	to wait for a bus or train — 候 hòu (to wait) + 车 chē (vehicle). To wait for transport. Seen everywhere on station signage: 候车室 hòuchēshì "waiting room," 候车厅 "waiting hall."
候鸟	hòu niǎo	migratory bird — 候 hòu (season) + 鸟 niǎo (bird). The bird that moves with the season, contrasted with 留鸟 liúniǎo, the resident bird that stays year-round.
候诊	hòu zhěn	to wait to see a doctor — 候 hòu (to wait) + 诊 zhěn (to examine medically). To wait for a medical consultation. The room where patients sit is the 候诊室 hòuzhěnshì.
胡同	hú tòng	hutong, alley
户外	hù wài	outdoors; in the open air — 户 hù (door; household) + 外 wài (outside). Literally "outside the door," the open air beyond a building. Common in 户外运动 "outdoor sports" and 户外活动 "outdoor activities."
花白	huā bái	grizzled; salt-and-pepper (of hair) — 花 huā (mixed-color) + 白 bái (white). Of hair or a beard, mixed black and white, grizzled: 花白的头发 "grizzled hair." The "many-colored" sense of 花 applied to the heads of the middle-aged.
花旦	huā dàn	lively female role
花朵	huā duǒ	flower; bloom (as a discrete blossom) — 花 huā (flower) + 朵 duǒ (the measure word for blooms). A flower viewed as an individual blossom, slightly more literary than bare 花. Children are often called 祖国的花朵, "the flowers of the motherland."
花费	huā fèi	to spend; expense; cost — 花 huā (to spend) + 费 fèi (fee, to expend). A more formal word for spending, and also a noun meaning the expense itself: 生活花费 "living expenses." Heavier and more written than bare 花.
花甲	huā jiǎ	60th birthday; one full cycle
花椒	huā jiāo	Sichuan pepper; prickly ash berry — Sichuan pepper , the dried husk of the prickly ash berry, not related to black pepper or chili pepper. Contains hydroxy-alpha sanshool, which activates the same receptor as light touch and low-frequency vibration, producing the characteristic tingling numbness on the tongue and lips.
花轿	huā jiào	The decorated red sedan chair in which the bride was traditionally carried to the wedding. The chair was ornately painted and curtained, surrounded by musicians and attendants. The tradition has been revived in some contemporary weddings as a heritage element.
花开	huā kāi	flowers bloom
花钱	huā qián	to spend money — 花 huā (to spend) + 钱 qián (money). The basic verb-object phrase for spending money. Often split by an amount: 花了不少钱 "spent quite a lot of money," 花很多钱 "spend a lot."
花时间	huā shí jiān	to spend time; to take time — 花 huā (to spend) + 时间 shíjiān (time). To spend or take time on something. The same verb 花 covers time as well as money, where English needs a different word for each.
花样	huā yàng	pattern; variety; fancy trick — 花 huā (patterned) + 样 yàng (shape, kind). A decorative pattern or a fancy variation, and by extension a trick or gimmick: 玩花样 "to play tricks," 花样滑冰 "figure skating."
花园	huā yuán	garden — 花 huā (flower) + 园 yuán (garden, plot). A garden, a cultivated plot of flowers and plants. 植物园 zhíwùyuán is a botanical garden; 动物园 dòngwùyuán, a zoo, keeps the same 园.
华人	huá rén	ethnic Chinese (esp. diaspora)
划分	huà fēn	to demarcate; to divide
话说回来	huà shuō huí lái	"Coming back to what we were saying" or "on the other hand — "Coming back to what we were saying" or "on the other hand." Marks a return to a previous thread, often with a contrasting take. Useful for acknowledging a counterpoint to your own argument: "but coming back to it, you do have a point that…"
淮扬菜	Huái yáng Cài	Huaiyang cuisine — Huaiyang cuisine: the specific tradition of the Yangzhou-Huai'an axis, considered the most technically refined of Chinese culinary traditions and the foundation of state banquet cooking.
坏处	huài chu	harm, drawback, downside — 坏 (bad) + 处 (point, aspect). The disadvantage or harm of something, the standard counterpart to 好处 (hǎochu, benefit). Weighed up when deciding whether something is worth doing.
坏蛋	huài dàn	scoundrel, bad egg — 坏 (bad) + 蛋 (egg). A literal rotten egg, used figuratively for a scoundrel. Colloquial and elastic: a genuine insult when angry, a flirtatious tease between a couple when said with a smile.
坏了	huài le	broken; out of order — 坏 (to break down) + 了 (change-of-state particle). The everyday way to say a device or appliance has stopped working, or that food has gone off. Also a spoken exclamation: 坏了！ "Uh-oh, this is bad!"
坏人	huài rén	bad person, villain — 坏 (bad) + 人 (person). The default word a child learns for the villain in a story or the stranger to avoid. Broad and moral, covering everyone from the cartoon antagonist to a genuine criminal.
欢呼	huān hū	to cheer; to hail with joy — 欢 huān (joy) + 呼 hū (to shout; to call). To shout for joy, the sound a crowd makes in celebration: 欢呼声 "cheers," 人们欢呼雀跃 "people cheered and jumped for joy."
欢送	huān sòng	to give a warm send-off — 欢 (joyful) plus 送 (see off). To send someone off with celebration: a farewell party for a retiring colleague or a graduating class. The opposite number of 欢迎 (huānyíng), to welcome.
欢腾	huān téng	to be jubilant; to seethe with joy — 欢 huān (joy) + 腾 téng (to leap; to surge). A crowd boiling over with joy, leaping and shouting. Said of stadiums, squares, and whole towns at a great moment: 全场欢腾 "the whole place erupted in jubilation."
欢喜	huān xǐ	happy; joyful; delighted — 欢 huān (merry) + 喜 xǐ (glad). A warm, somewhat colloquial word for being happy and pleased, common in the south and in the phrase 欢欢喜喜 "all merry and glad," and in 皆大欢喜 "everyone happy."
欢迎	huān yíng	to welcome; welcome! — 欢 huān (joy) + 迎 yíng (to greet; to meet). To receive someone gladly. Also the set greeting 欢迎 "welcome!" and the shop sign 欢迎光临 "welcome, please come in." 受欢迎 shòu huānyíng means "popular; well-liked."
皇后	huáng hòu	empress; empress consort — 皇 huáng (august; emperor) + 后 (the commanding consort). The principal wife of a reigning emperor in the Chinese imperial system. A formal title with specific ritual privileges and a defined role in the imperial hierarchy.
回答	huí dá	to answer; to reply; an answer — 回 huí (back) + 答 dá (to respond). To send a response back to a question: 回答问题 "to answer a question." As a noun, the answer itself: 一个满意的回答 "a satisfactory answer."
回复	huí fù	to reply
回甘	huí gān	returning sweetness — the lingering aftertaste
回锅肉	huí guō ròu	Twice-Cooked Pork
回家	huí jiā	to go home; to return home — 回 huí (to return) + 家 jiā (home). One of the most common V+location phrases in the language. The directional verb 回 indicates movement back toward an origin point: home as the natural destination of return, not merely a place you happen to go.
回来	huí lái	to come back; to return — 回 huí (to return; to turn back) + 来 lái (toward here). To turn and come back toward the speaker's reference point, usually home, a shared location, or where you were before leaving. The counterpart is 回去 huíqù (to go back, returning away from the speaker).
回去	huí qù	to go back
回忆	huí yì	to recall; to look back on; a memory — 回 huí (turn back) + 忆 yì (to remember). To turn the mind back to the past. As a noun, a remembered scene: 美好的回忆 "fond memories." The eddy here loops through time rather than space.
会议纪要	huì yì jì yào	meeting minutes — A short written summary of decisions, owners, and dates, sent the same day. Offering to write it yourself means your version becomes the record. 白纸黑字 (báizhǐ hēizì, "black ink on white paper") is the idiom for having it in writing.
混蛋	hún dàn	bastard, scoundrel
火车	huǒ chē	train (lit. "fire cart") — 火 huǒ + 车 chē (vehicle; cart). Named when steam locomotives first arrived in China , "fire-powered cart." The word has outlasted the steam era. 高铁 gāotiě (high-speed rail) is now preferred for bullet trains, but 火车 remains in universal use.
火车站	huǒ chē zhàn	railway station
火锅	huǒ guō	hotpot; steamboat — 火 huǒ + 锅 guō (pot; wok). A simmering communal pot at the table , the quintessential Chinese social meal. 吃火锅 = to eat hotpot, one of the most common social dining activities. Regional varieties: 四川火锅 Sichuan hotpot (numbing-spicy 麻辣), 北京涮羊肉 Beijing lamb hotpot (clear broth).
火候	huǒ hou	the right heat and timing; (by extension) the right moment or level of skill — 火 huǒ (fire) + 候 hou (the timing of). In cooking, the exact control of heat and duration that makes or ruins a dish. Figuratively, the ripeness of a moment or the maturity of a skill: 火候到了 "the moment is ripe."
火山	huǒ shān	volcano — 火 huǒ (fire) + 山 shān (mountain). A fire-mountain. Strikingly direct. Related: 火山爆发 volcanic eruption · 活火山 active volcano · 死火山 extinct volcano · 休眠火山 dormant volcano.
火星	huǒ xīng	Mars; a spark — The fire star twice over: the red planet of the five-phase planet names, and the everyday spark from a fire or a grinder's wheel. Internet slang added a third life: a 火星人 (Martian) is someone hopelessly behind on the news.
火焰	huǒ yàn	flame; blaze — 火 huǒ + 焰 yàn (flame , itself contains the fire radical 火). Used for actual flames and for intensity: 熊熊火焰 "roaring flames." Also in compound idioms: 火焰般的热情 "flame-like passion."
肌肉	jī ròu	muscle — 肌 (jī, muscle tissue) + 肉 (flesh). The fibrous flesh that moves the body; both characters carry the 月 flesh-radical.
鸡蛋	jī dàn	chicken egg — 鸡 (jī, chicken) + 蛋 (egg): the default egg in Chinese kitchens. When a recipe or menu just says egg, it means a hen's egg, so 鸡蛋 is one of the first nouns most learners cook with.
鸡肉	jī ròu	chicken (meat) — 鸡 (chicken) + 肉 (ròu, meat). The flesh on the menu, as opposed to the live bird. Pair it with 牛肉 (niúròu, beef) and 猪肉 (zhūròu, pork) to name the everyday meats.
基本	jī běn	basic; fundamental; (基本上) basically, on the whole — 基 jī (foundation) + 本 běn (root). The base layer of something: 基本功 jīběngōng (basic skills), 基本工资 (base salary). The adverb 基本上 jīběnshàng means "basically, by and large."
基督教	Jī dū jiào	Christianity
激动	jī dòng	excited; agitated; stirred up; worked up — 激 jī (to rouse; to surge) + 动 dòng (to stir). A fast, strong emotional stirring, joy, anger, or agitation that one can barely sit still through: 太激动了 "so excited," 别激动 "calm down."
及第	jí dì	to pass the imperial examination; to place in the rankings — 及 jí (to reach; to attain) + 第 dì (rank). To succeed in the civil-service exam and be placed in the official order. A historical and literary term; the modern equivalent is 考上 kǎoshàng (to test into).
疾病	jí bìng	disease, illness (formal) — 疾 (jí) and 病 are near-synonyms paired into one formal noun. In classical usage 疾 was the slight ailment and 病 the serious one; fused together, 疾病 is the neutral cover term for disease in medical and official language.
集体	jí tǐ	collective; group; the community — 集 jí (to gather) + 体 tǐ (body). A gathered body of people acting as one: 集体活动 "a group activity," 集体主义 "collectivism." Set against the individual, 个人 gèrén.
几个	jǐ ge	a few; several; how many — 几 jǐ (how many; a few) + 个 ge. Either a question ("how many?") or a vague small number ("a few"), depending on context: 几个人 "how many people?" or "a few people."
记得	jì de	to remember; to recall — 记 jì (to keep in mind) + 得 de (a resultative marker). To have something still held in memory, the everyday word for remembering: 我记得他 "I remember him." Its opposite is 忘记 wàngjì (to forget).
记号	jì hào	a mark; a sign (made to remember by) — 记 jì (to mark) + 号 hào (sign, number). A mark made deliberately so you can find or recall a spot: 做记号 "to make a mark." Often a tick beside a line or a fold in a page.
记录	jì lù	to record; a record; minutes — 记 jì (to write down) + 录 lù (to copy, to register). To set something down in writing, and the written result itself. Also the "record" that is broken in sport: 世界记录 "world record."
记忆	jì yì	memory; to remember (formal) — 记 jì (to remember) + 忆 yì (to recall, recollect). The faculty of memory and its contents, more formal and abstract than 记得: 童年的记忆 "childhood memories," 记忆力 jìyìlì "the power of memory."
记者	jì zhě	reporter; journalist — 记 jì (to record) + 者 zhě (one who). Literally "one who records," the reporter whose work is to set down and report events: 新闻记者 "a news reporter."
记住	jì zhù	to remember firmly; to commit to memory — 记 jì (to keep in mind) + 住 zhù (a resultative meaning "to stay fast"). To make something stick so it will not slip away: 记住这个号码 "memorize this number." The 住 stresses that the memory has caught and held.
祭天	jì tiān	offering to Heaven
稷下学宫	Jì xià Xu é gōng	Jixia Academy — the Warring States research university — Established by the state of Qi near its capital Linzi (临淄 Líncí) in the early 4th century BCE, operating for about a century. Scholars from all schools received state stipends and official titles without bureaucratic duties.
加热	jiā rè	to heat up — 加 (jiā, to add) plus 热 (heat): to add heat, to warm something up. The verb on the microwave button and the recipe step.
加上	jiā shàng	in addition; plus; adding to this
加油站	jiā yóu zhàn	petrol station — 加油 (jiāyóu, to add fuel) + 站 (station): the place a car stops to refuel. Note that 加油 doubles as the cheer "let's go! keep it up!", literally "add oil," but here it is strictly the fuel sense.
夹菜	jiā cài	To pick food for someone else , placing food from a shared dish into another person's bowl as a gesture of care and hospitality. The action is performed with chopsticks and directed toward honored guests, elders, or someone whose preferences you are attending to.
家常	jiā cháng	everyday home life; homestyle; ordinary — 家 jiā (home) + 常 cháng (ordinary; often; constant). The routine of domestic life: meals, small talk, everyday rhythms. A warmly neutral word that positions the homely and ordinary as valuable rather than inferior.
家规	jiā guī	family rules; household rules — 家 jiā (family) + 规 guī (rules; regulations; norms). The explicit code of conduct of a household, the spoken version of what 家风 transmits implicitly. Distinguished families in imperial China often had written 家规 codifying expectations for children's conduct, study, and marriage.
家祭	jiā jì	domestic ancestral offering
家谱	jiā pǔ	genealogy book
家人	jiā rén	family members; one's family — 家 jiā + 人 rén (person/people). The people of the home. More intimate and emotionally warm than the formal compound 家庭成员 (family members, bureaucratic). 家人 feels like "my people": those who share your roof and your life.
家庭	jiā tíng	Family; household — Family; household. The nuclear unit of parents and children who share a home. 家庭作业 (jiātíng zuòyè, homework , lit. "family work") and 家庭医生 (jiātíng yīshēng, family doctor) show the compound in everyday use. The term carries a warmth and domesticity that the more formal 家族 lacks.
家乡	jiā xiāng	hometown; native place — 家 jiā + 乡 xiāng (countryside; native place, the rural origin point). The place where 家 was first made. A place of origin rather than merely an address: family roots, ancestral connections, formative landscape. Central to Chinese identity, deeply tied to belonging and longing.
家训	jiā xùn	family precepts; ancestral rules
家园	jiā yuán	home; homeland; one's native place
家长	jiā zhǎng	head of household; guardian
家族	jiā zú	Clan; extended family network; lineage group sharing a surname. 家族企业 (jiāzú qǐyè) is a family business , often meaning a business controlled across generations by a single clan. The word implies shared ancestry, shared surname, and historically shared clan property and ancestral hall (祠堂 cítáng).
嘉峪关	Jiā yù guān	Jiayu Pass — western terminus of the Ming wall
甲子	jiǎ zǐ	the 60-year cycle; first combination
假花	jiǎ huā	artificial flower
兼爱	jiān ài	universal, impartial love — 兼 jiān (jointly; all together; simultaneously) + 爱 ài (love; care). The central Mohist doctrine: love that makes no distinction by relationship, proximity, or social standing. Contrasted directly with the Confucian 爱有差等 ài yǒu chā děng (love is graded by degree of relationship).
减少	jiǎn shǎo	to reduce; to decrease; to diminish — 减 jiǎn (to subtract, to reduce) + 少 (few, less). To make less: to reduce a quantity, frequency, or intensity. 减少污染 (reduce pollution); 减少开支 (cut expenditure); 人数减少了 (the number of people decreased). The standard verb for any reduction in quantity.
减字谱	jiǎn zì pǔ	abbreviated-character tablature — the guqin's notational system — 减 jiǎn (reduce; abbreviate) + 字 zì (character) + 谱 pǔ (notation; score). Developed in the Tang dynasty, used continuously since. Each composite character encodes string number, finger, position, and technique in a single glyph. Over 3,000 pieces survive in this notation.
碱水面	jiǎn shuǐ miàn	alkaline noodles; lye-water noodles
见面	jiàn miàn	to meet; to see one another (in person) — 见 jiàn (to see) + 面 miàn (face). Literally "to see faces," the everyday verb for meeting someone in person. It is a separable verb: 见了一面 "met once," 见过面 "have met before."
见外	jiàn wài	to treat as an outsider
建国	jiàng uó	to found a nation; nation-building — 建 jiàn (to build, to establish) + 国 (country, state). The founding of a state. 建国以来 (since the founding of the country) is a common phrase that, depending on speaker, refers to 1912 (Republic) or 1949 (People's Republic). 国庆节 (National Day, October 1) marks the 1949 建国.
将来	jiāng lái	the future; in the future; what is coming — 将 jiāng (about to; will; the near future) + 来 lái (coming; arriving). What is about to arrive, the future approaching. 将来 refers to the general future (not an immediate event). Slightly more formal than 以后 yǐhòu. Contrast: 未来 wèilái (the future, lit.
交错	jiāo cuò	to interlace, crisscross, intersect — 交 jiāo (to cross) + 错 cuò (crosswise). The original "crossing" sense, with no fault implied: lines, paths, or branches weaving across one another. 纵横交错 describes a network running every which way.
交朋友	jiāo péng you	to make friends — A verb-object phrase: 交 jiāo (to associate with, to form bonds) + 朋友. To make a single friend is 交个朋友 (with 个 splitting the phrase). The same 交 appears in 交流 (to exchange, communicate) and 社交 (social interaction), all built on the sense of mutual exchange.
焦点	jiāo diǎn	focus; focal point
饺子	jiǎo zi	dumplings — the northern New Year food — The quintessential northern Chinese New Year food, eaten at midnight on New Year's Eve in many northern households. The shape resembles the 元宝 yuánbǎo (ancient sycee gold ingots), making them symbols of wealth.
饺子皮	jiǎo zi pí	Dumpling wrapper, literally "dumpling skin — Dumpling wrapper, literally "dumpling skin." Sold pre-made in supermarkets, but making them by hand is considered superior.
教材	jiào cái	teaching material
教师	jiào shī	teacher (formal); educator — 教 jiào (to teach) + 师 shī (master; expert). The formal word for a teacher as a profession, common in writing and titles. In direct address the warmer 老师 lǎoshī is used instead.
教室	jiào shì	classroom — 教 jiào (to teach) + 室 shì (room). The room where teaching happens. Watch the tone of 室 (shì), and do not confuse it with 教师 jiàoshī (teacher); the two words differ only in the second syllable.
教授	jiào shòu	professor
教堂	jiào táng	church (the building) — 教 jiào (religion) + 堂 táng (hall). The hall of worship, a church building. The community of believers, by contrast, is 教会 jiàohuì.
教训	jiào xun	a lesson (learned the hard way); to reprimand — 教 jiào (to teach) + 训 xùn (to admonish). As a noun, the lesson drawn from a mistake or setback: 吸取教训 "to learn the lesson." As a verb, to scold or take someone to task.
教育	jiào yù	education; to educate — 教 jiào (to teach) + 育 yù (to raise; to nurture). The whole enterprise of teaching and bringing up, as both a noun and a verb: 高等教育 "higher education," 教育孩子 "to bring up a child." This is the formal jiào reading.
姐姐	jiě jie	elder sister — The doubled character is the everyday word for one's older sister. The second 姐 takes a neutral tone, giving the warm, soft cadence Chinese kinship terms favor.
姐妹	jiě mèi	sisters; female friends — 姐 (elder sister) plus 妹 (younger sister) yokes the two into a single word for "sisters" as a pair or group, and by extension a tight circle of close female friends.
界面	jiè miàn	interface; the surface between two systems — 界 (boundary; boundary marker) + 面 (surface; face). The surface at the boundary between two things. In technology and computing: the user interface (用户界面 yònghù jièmiàn), screen interface. In science: the interface between two phases or systems.
今天	jīn tiān	today — 今 jīn (now; the present moment) + 天 tiān (day). The word for "today" encodes "the now-day." Chinese time words are nouns that typically occupy the topic position at the start of a sentence, before the verb; they work differently from English adverbs like "today."
进步	jìn bù	progress; to improve; progressive — 进 jìn (to advance) + 步 bù (a step). To move forward step by step, hence to improve. Works as a verb (你进步了 "you've improved") and as a noun (很大的进步 "great progress").
进度	jìn dù	progress — 跟进 (gēnjìn) is to follow up, 催 (cuī) to chase. Ask for photos as well as words; a picture of goods on the line settles more than 快了 (kuài le, "nearly there").
进来	jìn lái	to come in (toward the speaker) — 进 jìn (to enter) + 来 lái (to come). To enter moving toward where the speaker is. 请进来 "please come in" is what you say from inside a room to someone at the door.
进去	jìn qù	to go in (away from the speaker) — 进 jìn (to enter) + 去 qù (to go). To enter moving away from where the speaker stands. From outside a building you tell someone 你先进去 "you go in first."
进入	jìn rù	to enter; to go into (a place or stage) — 进 jìn (to enter) + 入 rù (to go in). A more formal "to enter," used of physical spaces and of abstract stages alike: 进入房间 "enter the room," 进入新阶段 "enter a new phase."
进士	jìn shì	palace graduate — the highest examination degree — The degree conferred on all who passed the palace examination (殿试). Literally "advanced to scholar" or "presented scholar." Receiving the 进士 degree placed a candidate in the imperial bureaucracy; specific posting and rank depended on one's place in the final ranking.
进展	jìn zhǎn	to make progress; headway; development — 进 jìn (to advance) + 展 zhǎn (to unfold). The headway a task, plan, or situation makes over time: 工作有了进展 "the work has made progress." More about an undertaking than a person.
惊喜	jīng xǐ	a pleasant surprise; to be pleasantly surprised — 惊 jīng (startled) + 喜 xǐ (joy). The happy jolt of an unexpected good thing: 一个惊喜 "a surprise (gift or event)," 又惊又喜 "both startled and delighted."
镜子	jìng zi	mirror
九歌	jiǔ gē	Nine Songs — Nine Songs: the series of eleven shamanic hymns attributed to Qu Yuan, addressing the gods of the Chu pantheon. Among the most directly religious texts in the 楚辞, they preserve the ritual language of Chu shamanism within a literary frame.
九九	jiǔ jiǔ	double nine — colloquial shorthand; homophonic longevity wish — 九 jiǔ (nine) + 九 jiǔ (nine). The colloquial compressed name for the festival, and a sound-based wish: 九九 is homophonous with 久久 (jiǔ jiǔ, for a long time; lasting), turning the calendar date into an expression of the wish for long life.
九霄	jiǔ xiāo	the highest heavens; the ninth sky — 九 jiǔ + 霄 xiāo (the upper sky; the clouds). The ninefold heaven, the very top of the sky, as high as the cosmos reaches.
九月	jiǔ yu è	September; the ninth month — 九 jiǔ + 月 yuè (month). September in the solar calendar. The ninth day of the ninth lunar month is the Double Ninth Festival (重阳节 Chóngyáng Jié), when the doubled nine (the highest yang) is celebrated by climbing heights and honoring the elderly, the day's name 重阳 meaning "double yang." So 九月初九 (the…
九州	jiǔ zhōu	the Nine Provinces; all of China — 九 jiǔ + 州 zhōu (province; region). The nine ancient provinces into which the legendary sage-king Yu was said to have divided the land after taming the floods. 九州 became a poetic name for the whole of China, the entire civilized realm: 九州大地 (the great land of the nine provinces).
九转大肠	jiǔ zhuǎn dà cháng	Nine-Turn Intestine
就是	jiù shì	precisely; exactly; even if; it's just that — 就 jiù (then; precisely; at once) + 是. Multiple functions make 就是 one of the most versatile particles in spoken Mandarin.
就是说	jiù shì shuō	that is to say / in other words
就要	jiù yào	about to; soon (imminent)
菊花	jú huā	chrysanthemum — the flower of the ninth month — 菊 jú (chrysanthemum) + 花 huā (flower; blossom). The chrysanthemum blooms in the ninth and tenth lunar months, when most flowering plants have finished. This timing made it a literary symbol of resilience, late-life achievement, and the refusal to conform to expected seasonal rhythms.
巨大	jù dà	enormous; gigantic
巨子	jù zǐ	Grand Master — the head of the Mohist organization — 巨 jù (great; chief) + 子 zǐ (master). The elected or designated leader of the Mohist order, holding authority over all members. The 巨子 could direct members to travel, take on defense contracts for states, and enforce the school's internal law.
具体	jù tǐ	concrete; specific; particular — 具 jù (to possess, to furnish) + 体 tǐ (body, form). Having a definite body, hence concrete and specific as against vague or abstract. Its opposite is 抽象 chōuxiàng (abstract): 具体的例子 "a concrete example."
具有	jù yǒu	to possess (a quality, characteristic, or abstract attribute) — 具 jù (to possess; equipped with; concrete tool) + 有. Formal written register. Used specifically to assert that a subject possesses an abstract quality, characteristic, feature, or capacity , not physical objects. 具有能力 (to possess ability), 具有价值 (to have value), 具有代表性 (to be representative).
军事	jūn shì	military affairs
君子协议	jūn zǐ xié yì	gentleman's agreement — A binding agreement resting on personal honor and the parties' characters, without legal documentation. The term assumes both sides are junzi , people whose word is their commitment. Widely used in modern business and political contexts where formal contracts are either inappropriate or impossible.
咖啡馆	kā fēi guǎn	café — 咖啡 kāfēi (coffee) + 馆. The modern counterpart to the 茶馆, a place to sit with a laptop or a friend. The 馆 suffix quietly marks it as a public establishment.
开车	kāi chē	to drive (a car)
开放	kāi fàng	to open up; open (in attitude or policy)
开关	kāi guān	a switch
开花	kāi huā	to bloom; to blossom; to flower — 开 kāi (to open) + 花 huā (flower). To come into bloom, said of a plant opening its flowers. Figuratively, of an effort that bears fruit: 遍地开花 "blooming everywhere," spreading and flourishing.
开会	kāi huì	to hold a meeting; to attend a meeting
开机	kāi jī	to power on (a device); to start filming
开门	kāi mén	to open the door; (of a shop) to open for business — 开 kāi (to open) + 门 mén (door). To open a door, and idiomatically for a shop to open: 商店几点开门？ "What time does the shop open?" Its pair is 关门 guānmén, to close.
开模	kāi mú	to make a mold; to commission tooling — The step that commits real money. 开新模 is new tooling; 共模 (gòngmú) or 公模 (gōngmú) is a shared or public mold the factory already owns, cheaper but not exclusive to you. 私模 (sīmú) is a private mold for one customer.
开始	kāi shǐ	to begin; the beginning
开学	kāi xu é	school starts; the start of term
看病	kàn bìng	to see a doctor; to seek medical treatment — 看 (to look at, to examine) + 病 (illness). The verb covers both sides of the clinical encounter: the patient "looks at" the illness by going to be treated, and the doctor "looks at" the illness to diagnose it. Context distinguishes who is doing the looking. 我去看病 (I'm going to see a doctor).
看不见	kàn bù jiàn	cannot see; invisible
看不起	kàn bu qǐ	to look down on; to disdain; to have contempt for — 看 (to look) + 不起 (cannot rise up to). The potential complement 不起 indicates inability to raise the gaze high enough , to look up to. Contempt as a failure of elevation. Its opposite is 看得起 kàndéqǐ (to respect, to think well of, to regard highly). 他看不起任何人 , He has contempt for everyone.
看穿	kàn chuān	to see through; to figure out — 看 (kàn, to look) + 穿 (to pierce, as a result complement). The gaze bores all the way through the surface, you see through a lie, a disguise, a scheme. 穿 here is the original drilling sense turned to the mind: the look penetrates and reaches the truth behind.
看好	kàn hǎo	to favor; to back; to be bullish on — 看 (to see, to judge) + 好 (as good/positive). To evaluate something as likely to succeed or worth supporting. Widely used in investment contexts (看好这只股票 , bullish on this stock), sports prediction (我看好他们赢 , I favor them to win), and interpersonal support (我很看好你 , I have high hopes for you).
看见	kàn jiàn	to see, to spot
看来是	kàn lái shì	it seems to be
看朋友	kàn péng you	to visit a friend; to go see someone — 看 extends naturally from visual attention to social attention: going to see someone implies caring, checking in, tending to the relationship. 看朋友 (visit a friend), 看望 kànwàng (to visit and care for, especially an elder or patient), 探望 tànwàng (to pay a visit, more formal).
看起来	kàn qǐ lái	looks like; visually appears
康熙字典	Kāng xī Zì diǎn	the Kangxi Dictionary — Commissioned by the Kangxi Emperor 康熙帝, completed 1716. 47,035 characters organized under 214 radicals. The definitive classical reference work, still consulted by scholars of classical Chinese today.
科学	kē xu é	science; scientific — 科 kē (branch; category; department) + 学 xué (learning; -ology). Lit. "categorized learning" , the systematic division of knowledge into disciplines. Coined as a translation for Western "science" in the late 19th century.
科学家	kē xu é jiā	scientist — 科学 kēxué (science, lit. "the study of branches/categories") + 家 jiā. One who has made science their home.
客家人	Kè jiā rén	The Hakka people, literally "guest family people," so named because they arrived in the regions where they settled as migrants, after earlier inhabitants.
客人	kèr én	guest
客星	kè xīng	Guest star — Guest star: a star that appears briefly and then fades, i.e., a nova or supernova. Chinese court astronomers maintained careful records of guest stars for astrological-political interpretation: a guest star in a particular mansion was a portent for specific ministers, regions, or dynasties.
课本	kè běn	textbook — 课 kè (lesson) + 本 běn (volume). The book for a course of study. A close synonym is 教科书 jiàokēshū, slightly more formal.
孔子	Kǒng zǐ	Confucius
裤子	kù zi	trousers
块 · 毛 · 分	kuài máo fēn	units of currency, spoken — Spoken prices use 块 for the main unit, 毛 for tenths, 分 for hundredths; written prices use 元, 角, 分. The trailing unit is often dropped: 四块一 is 4.10, 三块八五 is 3.85. Factories apply the same words to dollars, so add 美金 (měijīn) or 人民币 (rénmínbì) when it could be either.
筷子	kuài zi	chopsticks
筷子架	kuài zi jià	Chopstick rest — Chopstick rest: a small ceramic or metal rest placed beside the bowl to lay chopsticks on between bites. Used in formal settings; absent in casual ones.
狂欢	kuáng huān	to revel; carnival; wild celebration — 狂 kuáng (wild; unrestrained) + 欢 huān (joy). Joy turned up to its loudest: unrestrained revelry. 狂欢节 kuánghuānjié is the standard word for Carnival, and 狂欢 names any wild, all-out celebration.
来不及	lái bu jí	there's not enough time; too late to do something — 来 lái (to come; to reach) + 不 bù (not) + 及 jí (to reach; to catch up to). The time has not come far enough to reach this action, so there is not enough time. Opposite: 来得及 lái de jí (there is still time; still possible).
来了	lái le	(he/she/it) has come; has arrived; here it is — 来 lái (to come) + 了 le (completion/change-of-state particle). The arrival has been completed: the thing or person is now here. One of the most basic and high-frequency phrases in Mandarin.
来去	lái qù	directional complements
来源	lái yuán	source; origin; where something comes from — 来 lái (to come) + 源 yuán (source; origin; spring, where water comes from). "The place from which it comes." Used for the source of funding (资金来源), information (信息来源), authority (权力来源), and anything whose origin needs to be named.
兰州拉面	Lán zhōu lā miàn	Lanzhou Hand-Pulled Noodles
朗读	lǎng dú	to read aloud (clearly, with expression) — 朗 lǎng (clear; bright; resonant) + 读. The school-room and literary register for reading aloud , the kind of reading where pronunciation, rhythm, and feeling matter. Standard exercise in Chinese language classes.
劳动	láo dòng	labor; to work
了不起	liǎo bu qǐ	remarkable; extraordinary; amazing — 了 liǎo (to conclude; to surpass) + 不 + 起 (unable to rise above; potential complement marker). Literally "cannot be surpassed or concluded" , what cannot be surpassed is extraordinary. A high-praise adjective in everyday speech.
冷门	lěng mén	unpopular, obscure
李白	Lǐ Bái	Li Bai — Tang dynasty poet
里程	lǐ chéng	mileage, distance covered
里面	lǐ miàn	inside; interior; within — 里 (inside) + 面 (face, surface, side). The most direct spatial interior term in Mandarin. Used for concrete physical interiors (inside a box, inside a building) and abstract interiors (inside a system, inside a relationship).
里外	lǐ wài	inside-outside
里子	lǐ zi	The lining; the private reality beneath the public presentation. The counterpart to 面子. 里子不足，面子撑着 ("the lining is insufficient, face holds it up") describes a situation where the private reality does not support the public claim.
利人	lì rén	to benefit others
例外	lì wài	exception
莲花	lián huā	lotus flower
联系	lián xì	to contact; to get in touch; to link; connection — 联 lián (to join; to unite) + 系 xì (tie). Both the act of making contact and the link itself. As a verb: 联系我 "get in touch with me." As a noun: 保持联系 "keep in contact."
良朋	liáng péng	a good friend: a worthy companion — 良 liáng (good, fine) + 朋 péng (friend). A good and worthy friend, the term carrying the older weight of 朋. Literary in tone; often paired as 良朋益友 "good friends who do you good." You would write it, not say it over coffee.
聊天	liáo tiān	to chat
临写	lín xiě	to copy a calligraphic model
聆听	líng tīng	to listen attentively; to give careful ear to — 聆 (to listen carefully , 耳 radical) + 听 (to hear). A formal compound for respectful, focused listening. Used for listening to elders, teachers, and music: 聆听老师的教诲 (listen attentively to the teacher's instruction); 聆听音乐 (to truly listen to music, not just hear it).
领事馆	lǐng shì guǎn	consulate
另外	lìng wài	"Besides" or "in addition — "Besides" or "in addition." Marks a related but separate point. Useful when you want to add information that is not strictly part of the same argument: 另外, 我想问你一件事 ("also, I want to ask you something").
留白	liú bái	留白 — leaving white; the empty space in composition — 留 liú (to leave; to keep) + 白 bái (white; blank). The deliberate empty space left unpainted in Chinese composition: 留白 is an active compositional element, not a failure to fill space. Empty space creates breathing room, implies what is unseen, and activates the imagination.
六波罗蜜	liù bō luó mì	the six paramitas; the six perfections of the bodhisattva path — 波罗蜜 bōluómì from Sanskrit pāramitā: "crossing to the other shore" , from suffering to liberation, from delusion to insight.
六度	liù dù	the six perfections; the sixth is 般若
六六大顺	liù liù dà shùn	"six-six, all goes smoothly" — a wish for things to go well — 六六 (six-six) + 大顺 (great smoothness). A set blessing built on the homophony of 六 liù with 流 liú (to flow) and the auspicious 顺 shùn (smooth, unobstructed). Said at weddings, openings, and new beginnings to wish that everything proceed without obstacle.
六器	liù qì	six ritual jades
六亲	liù qīn	the six relations; close kin — 六 liù + 亲 qīn (relative; kin). The six categories of close relatives (variously listed: father, mother, elder and younger brothers, wife, children, in some reckonings). In practice 六亲 means one's close family as a whole.
六十甲子	liù shí jiǎ zǐ	The sixty-year cycle — The sixty-year cycle: the combination of twelve animals with ten Heavenly Stems, the basic unit of traditional Chinese time reckoning.
六艺	liù yì	the six arts of the Confucian gentleman — 六 liù + 艺 yì (art; skill). Rites (礼), music (乐), archery (射), charioteering (御), writing (书), and mathematics (数). The complete curriculum of the Zhou-dynasty gentleman, balancing civil and martial accomplishments.
六月	liù yu è	June; the sixth month — 六 liù + 月 yuè (month). June in the Gregorian calendar. Like all Chinese month names, it is simply the number plus 月, with no separate words for the months.
六祖	Liù zǔ	the Sixth Patriarch (Huineng)
楼房	lóu fáng	multi-storey building — 楼 (lóu, a building of multiple floors) plus 房. A building with stories stacked above ground, contrasted with the single-level 平房 (píngfáng). The everyday word for the apartment blocks of a modern Chinese city.
楼上	lóu shàng	upstairs; the floor above — 楼 lóu (building; storied structure; floor) + 上 shàng (above; up). The floor above; upstairs. 楼下 is the natural pair. These two form one of the most common directional pairs in everyday speech, used for apartments, offices, restaurants, and any multi-story space.
鲁菜	Lǔ cài	Shandong Cuisine
鲁国	Lǔ Guó	the state of Lu — Confucius's home state
乱八糟	bā	eight as "every kind / all over" — In a large family of four-character expressions, 八 pairs with another number (most often 七) to mean "of all sorts, every which way," not a literal count: 七嘴八舌 (everyone talking at once), 四面八方 (from all directions), 七上八下 (agitated).
罗汉	luó hàn	arhat — Theravada ideal of personal liberation
马车	mǎ chē	horse-drawn carriage, cart — 马 (horse) + 车 (cart, vehicle). The literal horse-and-cart, the carriage that ran on the 马路 long before engines.
马虎	mǎ hu	careless, sloppy — 马 (horse) + 虎 (tiger). A folk tale tells of a painter who drew a creature half horse, half tiger and could not say which it was; the slapdash beast gave its name to careless, half-attentive work.
马拉松	mǎ lā sōng	marathon
马年	mǎ nián	Year of the Horse
马上	mǎ shàng	immediately; right away; at once — 马 mǎ (horse) + 上 shàng (on top of; mounted on). Literally "on horseback," from the military image of an order being carried out while still mounted, without dismounting. One of the most common adverbs in spoken Mandarin.
马戏	mǎ xì	circus
买不起	mǎi bu qǐ	cannot afford — Buy + not + shoulder. Distinct from 买不到 (can't get one: sold out) , the two negatives diagnose different tragedies, wallet versus supply chain.
麦西热甫	mài xī rè fǔ	meshrep — the traditional Uyghur community gathering — 麦西热甫 màixīrèfǔ (meshrep, from Persian/Turkic mäshrip meaning "gathering place") is the traditional Uyghur social institution: a community assembly that brings people together for music, dance, storytelling, comedy, and the resolution of social disputes through a combination of festivity and collecti…
满月	mǎn yu è	the one-month celebration for a newborn — 满 mǎn (full; complete) + 月 yuè (month). When a baby has survived its first full month , historically the most dangerous period , the family celebrates with red eggs (红鸡蛋 hóng jīdàn), gifting of gold jewelry, and a feast. The custom signals that the child is likely to thrive.
毛病	máo bìng	defect, flaw, bad habit; malfunction — 毛 (máo) means fine hair or fur, here the trivial detail; 毛病 is a small fault that keeps recurring. It stretches across a machine that breaks down, a person's annoying habit, and a minor recurring ailment, all the everyday senses of "something is wrong with it."
毛泽东	Máo Zé dōng	Mao Zedong — predecessor, contrast
没吃	méi chī	didn't eat; have not eaten — 没(有) is the standard negator for completed actions. It replaces 了 entirely , you never say 没吃了, because 没 already carries the information that no completion occurred. The 了 is superfluous and wrong. This is a near-universal rule: 没 and post-verb 了 are mutually exclusive.
没错	méi cuò	that's right; exactly; no mistake — 没 méi (not have) + 错 cuò (error). "No error here," used to agree firmly: 没错，我就是这个意思 "exactly, that's just what I mean." Often softened to 没错儿 in northern speech.
没关系	méi guān xi	it doesn't matter; never mind; that's okay — One of the most indispensable phrases in Mandarin. The standard response to 对不起 (sorry). Literally "no relationship" , meaning "it has no effect on us; it carries no weight." Learn this before your first week of speaking Chinese.
没面子	méi miàn zi	to lose face; to be embarrassed; without prestige — To be without face , whether because one has lost it through embarrassing behavior or because one's status is insufficient to command it. 没面子 is the social embarrassment register, distinct from the moral shame of 丢脸.
没事	méi shì	it's nothing; no problem; never mind; to be free (not busy) — 没 méi (not have) + 事 shì. "No matter." The everyday reassurance: it's fine, don't worry, no harm done. Also literally "to have nothing on," to be free.
每天 · 天天	měi tiān tiān tiān	every day · day after day (emphatic) — 每天 měitiān: 每 měi (every; each) + 天 tiān (day). The neutral distributive "every day." 每 attaches to any time unit: 每年 (every year), 每月 (every month), 每周 (every week).
门槛	mén kǎn	threshold (canonical object of 跨过)
门派	mén pài	martial sect; faction
门票	mén piào	entrance ticket: an admission ticket; the entry fee — 门 mén (door; gate; entrance) + 票 piào (slip; ticket). The ticket that admits you through the gate: into a park, a museum, a temple, a concert. By extension it also names the price of admission, what you pay at the door.
孟子	Mèng zǐ	Mencius — the second great Confucian philosopher — Mengzi (372–289 BCE) developed Confucian thought in two crucial directions: the argument for the innate goodness of human nature (性善论 xìngshàn lùn , humans are naturally oriented toward virtue), and a robust theory of political legitimacy based on the welfare of the people.
梦见	mèng jiàn	to see in a dream, to dream of
面包	miàn bāo	bread — 面 miàn (flour) + 包 bāo (wrapped, bun). Literally "flour-bun." The general word for bread of the Western loaf type, as distinct from steamed 馒头 mántou.
面对	miàn duì	to face; to confront; to deal with — 面 (face) + 对 (to face; to be opposite to). Lit. "face-to-face with." To confront a reality, problem, or situation squarely rather than avoiding it. Widely used in motivational, psychological, and journalistic writing.
面条	miàn tiáo	noodles — The same 面 character. In northern China, 面 = wheat-flour dough → noodles. The flat, surface-like sheet of worked dough becomes strips (条 tiáo = strip). A vivid reminder that 面 means "surface/face" in the most general sense , the flat plane.
描写	miáo xiě	to describe; to depict; to portray
渺小	miǎo xiǎo	insignificant; tiny before the vast
灭火	miè huǒ	to extinguish fire
名辩	míng biàn	Mohist name-disputation
名次	míng cì	ranking; place
名分	míng fèn	name and duty — the obligation that comes with a title — 名 míng (name) + 分 fèn (portion, allotted duty). One's station and the obligations that station carries.
名家	Míng jiā	School of Names
名片	míng piàn	business card — 名 míng (name) + 片 piàn (flat slice; card). A "name slice" , the physical object that carries your name into another person's hand. Business card exchange in China carries ritual weight: present and receive with both hands, read the card before putting it away, never write on it.
名人	míng rén	famous person; celebrity; notable — 名 míng (name; fame) + 人 rén (person). A person whose name is widely known. Covers historical greats, contemporary celebrities, and anyone of notable standing. Context determines whether the register is elevated (historical figures) or casual (entertainers).
名誉	míng yù	reputation
名字	míng zi	name (personal name, general term) — 名 míng (name) + 字 zì (character; in classical usage, the courtesy name given at adulthood). In modern Mandarin, 名字 is the everyday word for a person's name , first name, full name, or nickname depending on context. Neutral register, universally usable.
模样	mú yàng	looks; appearance
陌生	mò shēng	unfamiliar; strange; unknown — 陌 mò (a stranger; a path between rice fields, where you encounter people you don't know) + 生 shēng (unprocessed; unfamiliar). For unfamiliar places, strangers, and new experiences. 陌生人 = stranger. 对我来说很陌生 = "It's unfamiliar to me."
陌生人	mò shēng rén	stranger
墨家	Mò jiā	Mohism — universal love, opposition to aggressive war — Founded by 墨子 Mòzǐ (c. 470–391 BCE). Distinguished by: 兼爱 jiān'ài (impartial concern for all, not just one's own family and state), 非攻 fēi gōng (opposition to offensive war), and a utilitarian standard of moral judgment (actions are good if they benefit the people materially).
墨子	Mò zǐ	Master Mo; the person and the text — The name refers both to the historical philosopher (c. 470–391 BCE) and to the text compiled by his followers. The 墨子 consists of 71 chapters (of which 53 survive), covering ethics, political philosophy, logic, and the 墨经 (the scientific and logical canons).
默读	mò dú	to read silently — 默 mò (silent) + 读. The marked term for silent reading , marked precisely because the unmarked sense of 读 is voiced. A reading classroom may instruct students 先默读，再朗读 "first read silently, then aloud."
母鸡	mǔ jī	hen — 母 (female) + 鸡 (chicken). The egg-laying hen; a 母鸡下蛋 (the hen lays eggs) is the first farmyard fact every child learns.
木卡姆	Mù kǎ mǔ	The Uyghur Muqam , twelve suites of music incorporating song, instrumental performance, and dance, each built on a distinct modal system. A living art form and UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage, the Muqam is considered the highest expression of Uyghur musical culture.
木马	mù mǎ	wooden horse; vaulting horse; trojan — 木 (wood) + 马 (horse). A horse made of wood: the rocking toy, the gymnastics vaulting horse, and the computer 木马 (a trojan), all named for the most famous wooden horse of all, the one at Troy (特洛伊木马).
墓地	mù dì	cemetery; grave site
哪里	nǎ lǐ	where; (modest deflection) — 哪 (nǎ, which) + 里 = 哪里 (which interior zone, where). Beyond literal location questions, 哪里 doubles as a stock modest deflection: when someone praises you, responding 哪里哪里 (nǎlǐ nǎlǐ, literally "where, where?") means "oh, not at all, you're too kind." The deflection works because 哪里 implies the prais…
哪里哪里	nǎ lǐ nǎ lǐ	"where, where?" — the classic deflection of a compliment; "you flatter me" — The quintessential 谦虚 response to a compliment , a rhetorical question that denies the existence of the praised quality. Literally "where is this quality you're seeing?" The doubling intensifies the deflection.
那个	nà ge	that one — "Uh…" or "that…" The single most common verbal filler in Mandarin, used to stall for half a second while the next word arrives. Stretched out as 那个… 那个… it functions almost identically to English "um." Overusing it sounds hesitant; sprinkling it in sounds native.
那就这样吧	nà jiù zhè yàng ba	"Okay, let's leave it at that — "Okay, let's leave it at that." The standard wrap-up marker. Signals that the conversation has reached a natural stopping point and you are about to close. The 吧 invites the other party to agree to wind down.
那里	nà lǐ	there
那时候	nà shí hou	at that time
那样	nà yàng	that way
奶牛	nǎi niú	dairy cow
男孩	nán hái	boy — 男 (male) plus 孩 (child). Often expanded to 男孩子 (nánháizi) with no change in meaning. Used for boys and, loosely, for young men.
男朋友	nán péng you	boyfriend: a male romantic partner — 男 nán (male) + 朋友 péngyou (friend). The standard word for a boyfriend; 女朋友 nǚ péngyou is the matching girlfriend. The 朋友 base shows how the modern romantic term grew out of the plain word for companion.
男子汉	nán zǐ hàn	a real man; a manly man — 男子 nánzǐ (male, man) + 汉 hàn (fellow). A man in the sense of courage and backbone, the one who keeps his word and bears hardship. 男子汉大丈夫 "a true man and great fellow" is the full heroic flourish.
闹洞房	nào dòng fáng	The wedding-night hazing — The wedding-night hazing: guests entering the bridal chamber to subject the couple to games and pranks. The custom is ancient, widely practiced, and occasionally criticized for going too far. The word 闹 (nào) means to make noise, to create commotion.
内家拳	nèi jiā quán	internal martial arts — 内 nèi (internal) + 家 jiā (school; tradition) + 拳 quán (fist; boxing). The internal arts (Taijiquan 太极拳, Baguazhang 八卦掌, Xingyiquan 形意拳) emphasize cultivation of internal energy (气 qì), sensitivity, and relaxed power over brute muscular force.
你吃了吗	nǐ chī le ma	have you eaten? (intimate register)
你好	nǐ hǎo	hello (formal register)
你好吗	nǐ hǎo ma	how are you? (borrowed register)
你们	nǐ men	you (plural)
你说嘛	nǐ shuō ma	just say it / tell me then (urging)
你在做什么？	nǐ zài zuò shén me ?	What are you doing? — The standard colloquial question for asking what someone is currently doing. 在 alone (not 正在) is the norm in this question. 你正在做什么？is grammatical but sounds slightly unnatural or formal in casual conversation. The question naturally invites a 在/正在 answer.
年龄	nián líng	age; years of life — 年 nián (year) + 龄 líng (age; years of life , used specifically for age of living things). The formal word for a person's age. 年龄段 niánlíng duàn = age group. 适龄 shìlíng = of appropriate age (e.g., 适龄儿童 school-age children).
年轻	nián qīng	young — 年 nián (year; age) + 轻 qīng (light; young; not heavy). The standard adjective for "young" , the opposite of 老 lǎo. 年轻人 niánqīngrén = young people. 年轻化 niánqīnghuà = to become younger/more youthful. 越活越年轻 "getting younger as I live" , a compliment to someone who looks youthful.
年夜饭	nián yè fàn	New Year's Eve reunion dinner — the most important meal of the year — 年 nián (year) + 夜 yè (night/eve) + 饭 fàn (meal). The reunion dinner on New Year's Eve, the sacred center of Spring Festival. Every family member is expected to return home for this meal, regardless of distance.
涅槃重生	niè pán chóng shēng	to be reborn through the fire; the modern metaphor
牛逼	niú bī	badass, awesome (vulgar)
牛奶	niú nǎi	milk (cow's) — 牛 (ox/cow) + 奶 (nǎi, milk/breast). The default word for drinking milk in Mandarin; 奶 alone leans toward breast milk or the act of nursing, so the 牛 makes the source explicit.
牛年	niú nián	Year of the Ox
牛排	niú pái	steak — 牛 (ox) + 排 (pái, a slab/cutlet, from 排骨 spare-rib). A Western-style cut of beef cooked as one piece, ordered by doneness the way English orders rare to well-done.
牛肉	niú ròu	beef — 牛 (ox) + 肉 (ròu, meat). Chinese names meat by its animal, so beef is literally ox-meat, parallel to 猪肉 (zhūròu, pork) and 鸡肉 (jīròu, chicken).
牛仔	niú zǎi	cowboy
牛仔裤	niú zǎi kù	jeans — 牛仔 (niúzǎi, cowboy, literally ox-lad) + 裤 (kù, trousers). The denim of the American cowboy, named in Chinese after the herder himself.
弄错	nòng cuò	to get wrong; mix up
排他	pái tā	exclusive; to exclude others
旁人	páng rén	bystanders; those on the sidelines (literary) — 旁 páng (side; beside) + 人 rén. Literally "people at the side": observers, onlookers, those not directly involved in a situation. The word carries a slightly theatrical quality: you, the protagonist, and 旁人 who watch. More literary than 别人. Common in classical and modern literary prose.
朋友圈	péng you quān	circle of friends; WeChat Moments
皮蛋	pí dàn	century egg, preserved egg — 皮 (pí, skin) + 蛋: a duck egg cured in lime, ash, and salt until the white turns to dark amber jelly and the yolk to creamy green-grey. The leathery surface gives it the "skin" name.
偏见	piān jiàn	prejudice, bias
漂亮的花	piào liang de huā	beautiful flower — adjective + 的 + noun — The most basic use of 的: an adjective modifying a noun. In this pattern, 的 functions like the English suffix "-'s" or the phrase "that is." 漂亮的花 = the flower that is beautiful = the beautiful flower.
破坏	pò huài	to destroy, to undermine — 破 (to break, smash) + 坏 (to ruin). Two near-synonyms doubled for force. Destroys physical things (buildings, environment) and abstract ones alike (trust, an atmosphere, a plan, the rules).
浦东	Pǔ dōng	Pudong — Pudong: the eastern bank of the Huangpu River and its hinterland, designated a special economic development zone in 1990. Its skyline of supertall towers built since the 1990s is the visual symbol of reform-era China.
普陀山	Pǔ tuó shān	Putuoshan (Guanyin's sacred island)
七夕	qī xī	the Qixi Festival; the Chinese Valentine's Day — 七 qī + 夕 xī (evening). The seventh evening of the seventh lunar month, the annual meeting of the Weaver Girl (织女) and the Cowherd (牛郎). Once a women's festival for praying for skill in needlework (乞巧, "begging for cleverness"), it is now celebrated as a day for lovers.
七月	qī yu è	July; the seventh month — 七 qī + 月 yuè (month). July in the solar calendar. The seventh lunar month is the Ghost Month (鬼月), when the gates of the underworld are believed to open and spirits roam among the living; its fifteenth day is the Ghost Festival (中元节).
妻子	qī zi	wife — 妻 qī (wife) + 子 zi (suffix). The standard modern word for wife. Note the tone: in classical Chinese 妻子 qīzǐ meant "wife and children," but the modern toneless reading qīzi means the wife alone.
期间	qī jiān	period; during the time of — 期 qī (a fixed period) + 间 jiān (interval). A defined stretch of time, often used after an event to mean "during": 假期期间 "during the holidays," 会议期间 "in the course of the meeting."
齐国	Qí Guó	the state of Qi — first hegemon's domain
骑马	qí mǎ	to ride a horse
起飞	qǐ fēi	to take off — 起 (to rise) plus 飞 (to fly): to rise into flight. Used literally of aircraft leaving the runway and figuratively of an economy or career that is about to surge.
签字	qiān zì	to sign
钱包	qián bāo	wallet; purse — 钱 qián + 包 bāo (bag; to wrap). The wallet, literally the bag for money. In the smartphone era, 钱包 also refers to mobile payment wallets: 电子钱包 diànzǐ qiánbāo = digital wallet. 钱包里没钱 = the wallet is empty (a relatable condition).
钱给人骗了	qián gěi rén piàn le	the money was scammed (colloquial 给 passive) — 给 gěi as passive marker: Subject + 给 + Agent + Verb + Result. 给 in this passive function is particularly common in certain regional varieties of Mandarin. The adversarial connotation is strong , money being deceived out of someone is a very 被-appropriate scenario.
乾坤大挪移	qián kūn dà nuó yí	"the great cosmic shift" — a dramatic all-encompassing maneuver; a total reconfiguration — 挪移 nuó yí: to move; to shift; to relocate. 乾坤大挪移 names a fictional martial-arts technique in Jin Yong's (金庸) novel "The Smiling Proud Wanderer" (笑傲江湖) and appears in other wuxia fiction as the name for a supreme technique that redirects any force at will.
亲朋	qīn péng	kin and friends: one's close circle — 亲 qīn (kin, relatives) + 朋 péng (friends). Relatives and friends taken together, the whole circle of those close to you. Most often heard in the four-character 亲朋好友, the standard phrase for everyone you would invite to a wedding or a feast.
亲人	qīn rén	loved ones; dear family
钦天监	qīn tiān jiàn	Imperial Observatory / Bureau of Astronomy — Imperial Observatory / Bureau of Astronomy: the government department responsible for astronomical observation, calendar production, and astrological consultation in imperial China.
青菜	qīng cài	green vegetables; leafy greens — 青 qīng (green) + 菜 cài (vegetable). A general word for leafy green vegetables, and in the south the name of a specific small green cabbage. 一盘青菜 "a plate of greens."
倾听	qīng tīng	to listen attentively; to lean in and hear — 倾 qīng (to lean; to incline; to pour out) + 听 (to listen). Listening with the body inclined toward the speaker , attentive, empathetic, fully directed.
请多关照	qǐng duō guān zhào	please look after us; I look forward to working with you — The standard closing line of a first introduction, asking the other side's goodwill for the relationship ahead. 初次见面，请多关照 ("meeting for the first time, please look after me") is the full formula. 请多指教 asks for guidance and is slightly humbler.
请教	qǐng jiào	to consult; to ask for advice (politely) — 请 qǐng (to request) + 教 jiào (instruction). To respectfully ask someone to teach you, the polite way to seek advice from a senior or an expert: 向您请教 "may I ask your guidance." Here 教 returns to the fourth tone.
请客吃饭	qǐng kè chī fàn	hosting a meal — baijiu is the host's primary tool
请问	qǐng wèn	may I ask; excuse me (before a question) — 请 qǐng (please; to request) + 问 wèn (to ask). The polite opener for putting a question to a stranger, much like English "excuse me, may I ask." One of the first phrases a learner needs for asking directions.
秋天	qiū tiān	autumn
取名	qǔ míng	to name; to give a name — 取 qǔ (to take; to select; to acquire) + 名 míng (name). The act of choosing a name , for a child, a product, a shop, a poem.
去吧	qù ba	go ahead; you may go; off you go — 去 qù (to go; to depart) + 吧 ba (softening particle, suggestion or permission). A gentle dismissal or permission to leave. In a parent-child conversation: 去吧，好好玩 (Go on , have fun). In a formal context: 你去吧，我在这等 (Go ahead , I'll wait here).
去除	qù chú	to remove; to eliminate; to get rid of — 去 qù (to send away; to remove) + 除 chú (to eliminate; to remove; to divide out). To cause something to go away , to strip out an unwanted element. Used in formal and technical contexts: 去除污染 (remove contamination), 去除杂质 (remove impurities), 去除顾虑 (dispel concerns).
去掉	qù diào	to get rid of; to remove; to drop (something) — 去 qù (to cause to go) + 掉 diào (resultative complement: dropped away; gone). A colloquial and direct expression for removal. 掉 as a complement emphasizes that the thing has been fully removed and is gone. More informal than 去除; appropriate for everyday speech. 把它去掉！= Get rid of it!
去年	qùn ián	last year — 去 qù (to have gone; to have departed) + 年 nián (year). The year that has departed , last year. A vivid temporal use: the year that went away. Contrast: 来年 láinián (the year that is coming , next year; somewhat literary) and 今年 jīnnián (this year).
缺点	qu ē diǎn	shortcoming
群体	qún tǐ	group; collective
热爱	rè ài	to love ardently
热点	rè diǎn	hot spot; trending topic
热敷	rè fū	hot compress — 热 (hot) plus 敷 (fū, to apply to the skin): to apply heat, a hot compress. The home remedy for a stiff neck or a sore back, the warm counterpart to 冷敷 (lěngfū), a cold compress.
热烈	rè liè	fervent, intense
热门	rè mén	popular, in demand — 热 (hot) plus 门 (mén, gate, field): a hot gateway, hence a popular field, trending topic, or in-demand option. The opposite is 冷门 (lěngmén), a cold and obscure one.
热闹	rèn ao	lively, bustling — 热 (hot) plus 闹 (nào, noisy, to make a din): the heat of a busy, crowded, festive scene. Note the neutral tone on 闹 in this word. A market, a holiday street, a full restaurant are all 热闹.
热水	rè shuǐ	hot water — 热 (hot) plus 水 (shuǐ, water). The everyday word for hot water, the staple drink offered to a guest and the cure a Chinese parent prescribes for almost anything.
人称	rén chēng	grammatical person
人际关系	rén jì guān xi	interpersonal relationships; social connections — 人际 rén jì = between people (人 person + 际 boundary, junction). 人际关系 is the standard term for the full web of interpersonal relationships in Chinese social and professional life. Appears constantly in business advice, psychology, and self-help contexts.
人家	rén jia	other people; someone's home; (colloquial) I/me — 人 rén (person) + 家 jiā (home; family). This single compound has three distinct uses in modern Mandarin: (1) other people in general; (2) someone's specific household; (3) an informal first-person pronoun used primarily by women, expressing mild protest or vulnerability.
人间天堂	rén jiān tiān táng	paradise on earth; heaven in the human world — 天堂 (tiāntáng) = paradise; heaven. 人间天堂 collapses the cosmological distance between 人间 and 天界: a place so beautiful it brings the celestial into the human world.
人间烟火	rén jiān yān huǒ	the smoke and fire of human life; the warmth of ordinary domestic existence — 烟火 (yānhuǒ) = smoke and fire , specifically the smoke of cooking fires and the warmth of a hearth. 人间烟火 refers to the sights, smells, and sounds of ordinary human life: street food stalls, the smell of garlic in a wok, a neighborhood at dusk.
人类	rén lèi	humanity; humankind; the human species — 人 rén + 类 lèi (species; category; kind). Refers to humanity as a biological and civilizational category, the human species in its entirety across all time. Used in philosophical, scientific, and grand-historical registers. Much larger in scope than 人们, and without political coloring.
人脉	rén mài	network; professional connections (lit. "person-veins") — 人 rén + 脉 mài (vein; pulse; artery, the channels through which blood flows). Your social and professional network, imagined as a circulatory system. 脉 is the same character used in 经脉 jīngmài (meridians) in TCM.
人们	rén men	people (indefinite plural) — 人 rén + 们 men (plural suffix for animate nouns). A general, indefinite plural: "people" in the broad social sense, as in "one finds that people…" or "people say…" 们 cannot be combined with numbers; you cannot say ＊三人们. This plural is for social generality, not counting.
人群	rén qún	crowd
人缘	rén yuán	people-affinity; popularity; being well-liked — 人 rén + 缘 yuán (fate; karmic bond, a Buddhist term for the predestined connections between people). Literally: one's karmic bond with people. In practice: whether others naturally warm to you.
肉麻	ròu má	sappy, cloying, cringe-inducing — 肉 (flesh) + 麻 (má, numb, tingling). The skin-crawling feeling of words too sweet or sentimental, the goosebumps of secondhand embarrassment.
肉松	ròu sōng	meat floss, dried pork floss — 肉 (meat) + 松 (sōng, loose, fluffy). Meat dried and shredded into airy fibers, scattered over porridge or buns.
肉体	ròu tǐ	the physical body, the flesh — 肉 (flesh) + 体 (tǐ, body). The bodily, corporeal self, set against 精神 (jīngshén, spirit) or 灵魂 (línghún, soul).
儒家	Rú jiā	Confucianism — the school of ritual and moral cultivation — Founded by Confucius (孔子 Kǒngzǐ, 551–479 BCE). Core values: 仁 rén (benevolence/humaneness), 礼 lǐ (ritual propriety), 义 yì (righteousness), 忠 zhōng (loyalty), 孝 xiào (filial piety).
入门	rù mén	to get started; to cross the threshold of a subject; a primer — 入 rù (to enter) + 门 mén (door). To step across the doorway of a field, to grasp its basics. A book titled 《汉语入门》 is "Chinese: A Primer," a doorway into the language.
闰月	rùn yu è	Intercalary month, the extra month inserted approximately every 2–3 years (7 times in 19 years) to reconcile the lunar and solar cycles. A year with a 闰月 has 13 months.
萨满教	sà mǎn jiào	shamanism
三宝	Sān bǎo	Three Jewels (Buddha, Dharma, Sangha)
三餐	sān cān	three meals a day — 三 sān + 餐 cān (meal). The three daily meals: breakfast (早餐 zǎocān), lunch (午餐 wǔcān), and dinner (晚餐 wǎncān). The phrase 一日三餐 (three meals a day) is the standard way to refer to daily sustenance, and by extension to reliable, ordinary livelihood.
三毒	sān dú	the three poisons that 涅槃 extinguishes
三伏	sān fú	The Three Hottest Periods — The Three Hottest Periods: the three intervals of approximately 10 days each during Major Heat and surrounding terms, traditionally considered the most intensely hot and humid days of the year.
三纲五常	sāng āng wǔ cháng	the Three Bonds and Five Constants — the Confucian social order — The three bonds (三纲): ruler-minister, father-son, husband-wife. The five constants (五常): benevolence (仁), righteousness (义), ritual propriety (礼), wisdom (智), and trustworthiness (信). Systematized in the Han dynasty, this framework defined Confucian social structure for two thousand years.
三国	Sāng uó	the Three Kingdoms; the era of three rival states — 三 sān + 国 guó (kingdom; state; country). The Three Kingdoms period (220–280 CE), when China split into Wei (魏), Shu (蜀), and Wu (吴) after the collapse of the Han dynasty.
三星堆	Sān xīng duī	Sanxingdui bronze site — Sanxingdui , "Three Star Mounds," the Bronze Age archaeological site near Guanghan. The bronze masks, standing figure, and sacred tree uncovered there belong to a culture with no connection to the contemporaneous Shang dynasty. 堆 means "mound" or "pile."
嗓子	sǎng zi	throat; voice
骚体	sāo tǐ	The "Sao style" , the poetic tradition derived from Qu Yuan's 离骚, characterized by longer lines, the medial particle 兮 xī, lavish botanical and spiritual imagery, and the theme of exile and unrecognized virtue. One of the two foundational styles of classical Chinese poetry.
山峰	shān fēng	mountain peak; summit — 山 shān + 峰 fēng (peak , itself contains 山). The redundancy is expressive emphasis. Used literally (climbing a peak) and figuratively (事业的山峰 "the peak of one's career").
山脉	shān mài	mountain range — 山 shān + 脉 mài (vein; pulse; connected system, the word for pulse in traditional Chinese medicine). A mountain range as a vein of the earth, all peaks connected underground. Same logic as 水脉 (water vein) and 血脉 (bloodline). 秦岭山脉 the Qinling Mountains.
山水诗	shān shuǐ shī	landscape poetry; the mountain-and-water poem genre — 山水 + 诗 (shī, poem). The poetic genre that parallels the painting genre. Founded in the Six Dynasties period by Xie Lingyun (谢灵运, 385–433) and brought to its high point in the Tang by Wang Wei (王维) and Meng Haoran (孟浩然).
赏月	shǎng yu è	to appreciate the moon; moon-gazing
上班	shàng bān	to go to work; to be at work; on duty — 上 shàng (to mount; to attend) + 班 bān (shift; work roster; class). To show up for and engage in one's work shift. The most common word for "going to work" in daily speech. Contrast 下班 (to finish work; to get off work): the 上/下 pair governs the entire working day.
上帝	shàng dì	God; the Lord on high
上坟	shàng fén	to visit and offer at the grave
上火	shàng huǒ	to get internal heat; to be inflamed; to get irritable — 上 shàng (to rise; to ascend) + 火 huǒ (fire). A TCM concept: excess heat (fire) in the body rising upward, causing sore throat, mouth ulcers, red eyes, or irritability. Used medically and colloquially. 吃辣的容易上火 "Eating spicy food easily causes 上火."
上进	shàng jìn	motivated; ambitious; striving to improve — 上 shàng (upward; advancing) + 进 jìn (to advance; to enter). Moving upward and forward. 上进心 shàngjìnxīn (the drive to improve; ambition) is one of the most positively valenced personal traits in Chinese educational culture: a quality parents want to see in children and teachers in students.
上来	shàng lái	come up; manage to (ability)
上面	shàng miàn	above; on top; the upper surface — 上 shàng (above; up) + 面 miàn (face; side; surface). The upper side of something; the surface on top; the direction above. In spatial description, 上面 is the default way to say "on top of" or "above." In discourse, 上面 can also mean "what was mentioned above" or "the higher authorities."
上去	shàng qù	go up; from outside appearance
上山下乡	shàng shān xià xiāng	up to mountains, down to countryside (policy)
上天	shàng tiān	heaven above; God/fate; to ascend into the sky — 上 shàng (above; to ascend) + 天 tiān (heaven). As a noun: the heavens conceived as a moral agent, equivalent to "God" or "fate" in many colloquial contexts. As a verb: to ascend to the sky (a rocket launching; a person dying).
上网	shàng wǎng	to go online; to use the internet — 上 shàng (to mount; to get onto) + 网 wǎng (net; network; the internet). To mount the internet. The spatial metaphor is the same as getting on a vehicle or into an institution: you step up onto a platform. Formed in the early 1990s as Chinese internet usage began, and immediately adopted the 上 frame.
上下	shàng xià	up and down; approximately; around (a number) — 上 shàng (above) + 下 xià (below). The full vertical range , top to bottom , which collapses into an estimate of the middle. When placed after a number, it means "approximately" or "give or take." 三十上下 = around thirty.
上学	shàng xu é	to go to school; to attend school — 上 shàng (to attend; to mount into) + 学 xué (learning; school). To attend school, covering both the daily action (going to school today) and the general state (being a school student). Contrast: 上大学 (to attend university), 上幼儿园 (to go to preschool).
上瘾	shàng yǐn	to become addicted; to get hooked — 上 shàng (to mount into; to be caught by) + 瘾 yǐn (addiction; craving, a disease radical character). To have mounted into the craving, to be in its grip. Works for any powerful habit or compulsion: 游戏上瘾 (game addiction), 咖啡上瘾 (caffeine dependency), 手机上瘾 (phone addiction).
上座	shàng zuò	The seat of honor , the seat facing the door, assigned to the most honored guest. The term literally means "upper seat." Understanding the seating logic around 上座 is a practical necessity at any formal Chinese meal. Equivalent: 主位 zhǔwèi.
烧纸钱	shāo zhǐ qián	burning paper money — offerings for the dead — 烧 shāo (burn) + 纸 zhǐ (paper) + 钱 qián (money). Paper replicas of currency, goods, and , in contemporary practice , smartphones, cars, designer bags, and houses, burned so that the smoke carries them to the deceased in the underworld.
少年	shào nián	youth; adolescent; teenager — 少 shào (young) + 年 (year; age). A person of young years , typically ages 10–18 in modern usage, though classically it could refer to anyone young.
少数	shǎo shù	minority; a small number — 少 (few) + 数 (number, quantity). A small quantity , the minority. 少数民族 shǎoshù mínzú (ethnic minority , literally "small-number people-clan") is the official Chinese term for China's 55 recognized ethnic minorities (the Han majority plus 55 groups).
什么东西	shén me dōng xi	what (thing); what on earth; exclamation of disgust — 什么东西 has two registers. As a genuine question, it means "what is this thing?" As an exclamation, it expresses shock, disgust, or contempt , "what the heck is this?" or simply "ugh!" The exclamatory reading is a complete utterance on its own: 什么东西！registers revulsion at something offensive, absurd, o…
什么时候	shén me shí hou	when? (question word) — The question word "when," literally "what time/moment." It sits where the time answer would go, not at the front as in English: 你什么时候走？("When are you leaving?"). One of the core question patterns alongside 哪儿 (where), 谁 (who), 怎么 (how).
生病	shēng bìng	to fall ill, get sick — 生 (shēng) means to give rise to or produce, so 生病 is literally "to produce illness," the standard verb for getting sick. It is a separable verb: an object or aspect marker can split the two halves.
生抽	shēng chōu	light soy sauce
生存	shēng cún	to survive; to subsist — 生 shēng + 存 cún (to exist; to preserve). Survival , continuing to exist under difficult conditions. 生存竞争 "survival competition" · 生存危机 "survival crisis." A more pressured, primal register than 生活.
生旦净末丑	shēng dàn jìng mò chǒu	opera role types
生机	shēng jī	vitality, life-force
生疏	shēng shū	rusty, out of practice
生肖	shēng xiào	zodiac animals; birth sign
生长	shēng zhǎng	to grow (of plants/children)
圣人	shèng rén	sage; fully realized moral exemplar
失去	shī qù	to lose; to be deprived of — 失 shī (to lose; to miss; to fail) + 去 qù (to go away). Something that was yours has gone away , loss. 失去 covers losing people (失去亲人 , lose a loved one), opportunities (失去机会), abilities (失去记忆 , lose one's memory), and qualities (失去耐心 , lose patience). A highly emotive verb.
诗人	shī rén	poet
施事	shī shì	agent (linguistics)
十二	shí èr	twelve — 十 shí (ten) + 二 èr (two). The separator apostrophe in pinyin marks the syllable boundary: shí·èr, not shí·ér. Twelve is culturally loaded: 十二生肖 shí'èr shēngxiào (the twelve zodiac animals) and 十二个月 shí'èr gè yuè (twelve months) both run on this number.
十二木卡姆	Shí èr Mù kǎ mǔ	the Twelve Muqam — Uyghur classical music tradition — The Twelve Muqam (木卡姆 mùkǎmǔ, from Arabic maqam, meaning mode/scale) is the comprehensive musical tradition of the Uyghur people: twelve suites, each a multi-movement composition comprising song, instrumental interlude, and dance, totaling hundreds of pieces.
十二生肖	shí èr shēng xiào	The twelve zodiac animals
十分	shí fēn	very; extremely; completely (lit. "ten parts") — 十 shí (ten; complete) + 分 fēn (part; portion; to divide). Ten out of ten parts: the full measure of something. The most natural intensifier in formal and written Mandarin, sitting between 很 hěn (colloquial "very") and 非常 fēicháng (extremely, emphatic).
十干	shí gān	the Ten Heavenly Stems — 十 shí (ten) + 干 gān (stem; heavenly stem). The cycle of ten: 甲 jiǎ, 乙 yǐ, 丙 bǐng, 丁 dīng, 戊 wù, 己 jǐ, 庚 gēng, 辛 xīn, 壬 rén, 癸 guǐ.
十年	shín ián	a decade; ten years — 十 shí + 年 nián (year). A decade. In Chinese cultural memory, ten years functions as the basic unit of historical reckoning: the Cultural Revolution lasted "ten years" (1966-1976, the 十年浩劫 shínián hàojié , ten-year catastrophe); a generation is roughly one decade; the proverb 十年树木，百年树人 places educati…
十有八九	shí yǒu bā jiǔ	eight or nine times out of ten; most probably — 十 (ten) + 有 (have) + 八九 (eight or nine). Out of every ten, eight or nine, hence almost always, in all likelihood. A common way to state strong probability: 这件事十有八九是真的, "this is most probably true." The form 十之八九 (eight or nine out of ten) is equivalent.
十月	shí yu è	October; the tenth month — 十 shí + 月 yuè (moon; month). The standard Mandarin name for October and for the tenth month of any lunar calendar. Chinese month names are purely numerical: 一月 through 十二月, January through December. The naming system makes 十月 the last single-digit-ten month before 十一月 (November) and 十二月 (December).
十字	shí zì	cross shape
时辰	shí chen	two-hour period (traditional)
时光	shí guāng	time (poetic)
时机	shí jī	the right moment; timing; an opportune occasion — 时 shí (time) + 机 jī (machine; pivot; crucial moment; opportunity). 时机 is the Confucian classical meaning of 时 made into a compound: the moment that is ripe, when conditions align. It is strategic time, the narrowing window before it closes.
时间焦点	shí jiān jiāo diǎn	time focus — "it was [then] that…" — When the focused element is a time expression, it appears directly after 是. This answers the implicit question "when?" about a past event both parties know occurred. The event itself is not in question , only its timing is being asserted.
时期	shí qī	period; phase
时中	shí zhōng	timely centrality; acting appropriately for the moment
识见	shí jiàn	insight; discernment; perceptive judgment
史记	Shǐ jì	Records of the Grand Historian (Sima Qian, c. 94 BCE) — 史 (historical record) + 记 (to record; to note). The foundational work of Chinese historiography, written by Sima Qian (司马迁, c. 145–86 BCE) over decades and completed after his castration by order of Emperor Wu of Han.
使坏	shǐ huài	to play a dirty trick — 使 (to make, to set going) + 坏 (bad). To deliberately make trouble for someone behind their back, to pull a malicious prank or sabotage on the sly.
市长	shì zhǎng	mayor
事后诸葛亮	shì hòu Zhū gě liàng	a Zhuge Liang after the fact; hindsight wisdom
事上磨炼	shì shàng mó liàn	tempering oneself through practical affairs — Wang Yangming's insistence that moral cultivation is not a retreat into contemplation but a process that occurs within engagement with actual affairs. The battlefield, the courtroom, the family, the market , all are sites where 良知 is extended or blocked.
事先	shì xiān	beforehand; in advance; prior to the event — 事 shì + 先 xiān (before; first). Before the matter happens, in advance. Its mirror is 事后 shìhòu (afterward), so the pair frames an event as before-and-after.
是不是	shì bu shì	is it or isn't it? · right? (A-not-A question / tag) — The A-not-A question form of 是 , a canonical Chinese question-formation strategy. Verb + 不 + Verb creates a yes-no question with slightly more rhetorical directness than a rising-intonation question. 是不是 also functions as a sentence-final tag question: 你也去，是不是？ ("You're going too, right?").
是的	shì de	yes; that's right; indeed (affirming something just said) — 是的 in its collapsed, fully reduced form is simply an affirmation: "yes, that is the case." The 是 confirms the truth of what was said; the 的 creates a sense of definitiveness or "that's just how it is." It is warmer and more emphatic than a bare 对, and more measured than an enthusiastic 当然.
是非	shì fēi	right and wrong; the rights and wrongs; trouble; gossip — 是 shì (right; this; is) + 非 fēi (wrong). The pairing of right and wrong as a single notion: 明辨是非 "to clearly distinguish right from wrong." By a common slide it also means the quarrels and gossip that arise from disputes: 惹是非 "to stir up trouble."
是否	shì fǒu	whether or not; if (formal written) — 是 (yes/right) + 否 fǒu (no; to negate , a classical negative particle). A formal, written-register conjunction meaning "whether or not." The colloquial equivalents are 有没有 or 是不是. 是否 appears in journalism, academic writing, formal correspondence, and polished speech.
是吗	shì ma	"Is that so?" or "really?" Slightly cooler than 真的吗, often signaling polite interest without strong surprise. The neutral acknowledgment that you have heard and registered what was just said.
