是这样啊	shì zhè yàng a	"Oh, so it's like that — "Oh, so it's like that." A softer, gentler version of 原来如此. The 啊 particle gives it a reflective, taking-it-in quality. Useful when someone has just shared something personal or explained a situation.
守江山	shǒu jiāng shān	to defend the realm; to hold and preserve what was won — 守 shǒu (to guard; to protect; to hold steady , the character shows a hand over a roof, the posture of protection) + 江山. The harder and longer task: not the dramatic moment of conquest but the slow, unglamorous work of governing, maintaining order, and preventing the realm from unraveling.
受不了	shòu bu liǎo	can't stand it; unbearable; past one's limit — 受 shòu (to endure; to receive) + 不了 liǎo (cannot complete the action of enduring). The potential complement with 不了 means the action cannot reach completion , endurance hits its limit. One of the most frequently used expressions in daily speech for frustration, exhaustion, or overwhelm.
受事	shòu shì	patient (linguistics)
蔬菜	shū cài	vegetables (collectively) — 蔬 shū (vegetables) + 菜 cài (greens). The formal, category word for vegetables as a food group, the term you see on nutrition labels and in recipes: 多吃蔬菜 "eat more vegetables."
熟能生巧	shú néng shēng qiǎo	practice makes perfect
数学	shù xu é	Mathematics — Mathematics: number-study. 数学家 (shùxuéjiā) is a mathematician; 数学课 (shùxué kè) is math class, the collocation every student uses daily.
数字	shù zì	Number, numeral, digit — Number, numeral, digit. The everyday word for a figure, whether spoken, written, or on a screen. 数字化 (shùzìhuà) extends it to "digitization," the same move English made with "digit."
数字系统	shù zì xì tǒng	the number system
数字象征	shù zì xiàng zhēng	Number Symbolism — 4 (四 sì) , inauspicious; sounds like 死 sǐ (death). Buildings skip floor 4, hospitals avoid room 4. 8 (八 bā) , extremely auspicious; sounds like 发 fā (prosper). License plates with 8s sell at premium.
帅哥	shuài gē	handsome guy; hey, handsome — 帅 (handsome) + 哥. A good-looking young man, and also a friendly way to flag down any young man whose name you don't know, the male counterpart of 美女 (měinǚ).
双重	shuāng chóng	double; dual; twofold — 双 shuāng (pair; double) + 重 chóng (layer). Two layers of the same thing: 双重标准 "double standard," 双重身份 "dual identity." Here 重 is the layer-counting chóng.
水单	shuǐ dān	bank remittance slip — The bank's proof that you sent the money, also 汇款水单. The factory will ask for it the moment you say you have paid, and production often waits for it. Send it promptly; it is the fastest way to start the lead-time clock.
水果	shuǐ guǒ	fruit
水磨腔	shuǐ mò qiāng	Water-ground tone , the smooth, polished vocal style developed by Wei Liangfu that defines 昆曲 singing. The metaphor refers to the way stone is ground by water to a perfect smoothness. Characterized by slow tempos, elaborate ornamentation, and seamless melodic flow.
水墨	shuǐ mò	ink wash; water-and-ink; the classical Chinese painting medium — 水 shuǐ (water) + 墨 mò (ink , a slab of compressed carbon pigment, dissolved in water to create the medium). The classical Chinese painting medium: ink dissolved in varying concentrations of water, applied with a brush to paper or silk to create images through gradations of tone from dark to light.
水牛	shuǐ niú	water buffalo
水袖	shuǐ xiù	Water sleeves — Water sleeves: the long white silk extensions attached to the costume sleeves of major 昆曲 characters.
睡不着	shuì bu zháo	cannot fall asleep — Sleep + not + take-hold, with 着 in its zháo reading. The 3 a.m. word, and the proof that the complement is about achieving a result: you can 睡 (lie down to sleep) all night and never 睡着.
顺便说一下	shùn biàn shuō yī xià	"By the way — "By the way." Marks an aside or a piece of side information that occurs to you mid-conversation. 顺便 literally means "in passing." The English equivalent fits cleanly. Often shortened in casual speech to just 顺便.
顺便问一下	shùn biàn wèn yī xià	"While I'm at it, let me ask…" Lets you slide an unrelated question into the conversation without it feeling like an interruption. Pairs naturally with 顺便说一下 (the side-remark version).
瞬间	shùn jiān	an instant; the blink of an eye — 瞬 shùn (a blink) + 间 jiān (interval). The smallest interval of time, the moment it takes to blink: 一瞬间 "in a flash." The interval shrunk almost to nothing.
说得对	shuō de duì	"Well said" or "you're right — "Well said" or "you're right." Compliments the rightness of what was just said. Slightly more formal than 没错; acknowledges the other person's reasoning, not just the conclusion.
说服	shuō fú	to persuade; to convince — 说 shuō + 服 fú (to submit; to be convinced; to be at someone's service). To speak until someone submits, to persuade successfully. The complement 服 marks the result: the persuasion worked.
说话	shuō huà	to speak; to talk; to say something — 说 shuō (to say) + 话 huà (words). The everyday verb for speaking, formed as verb-plus-object: literally "to say words." Because 话 is already the object, you do not say 说话一件事; to speak about something you use 说 alone: 说一件事.
说着说着她哭了	shuō zhe shuō zhe tā kū le	as she kept talking, she started to cry — The model sentence for this pattern. The action (talking) repeated in the V + 着 frame signals continuation; the main clause introduces the unplanned outcome (crying). Used whenever a shift happened gradually, through sustained activity, not at a discrete moment.
司马迁	Sī mǎ Qiān	Sima Qian
四川盆地	Sì chuān Pén dì	the Sichuan Basin — the geography that produced this cuisine's ingredients and intensity — The Sichuan Basin , the large inland depression enclosed by mountains on all sides. 盆 means "basin" or "bowl"; 地 means "land" or "ground." The shape of a basin (四面环山 sì miàn huán shān, "mountains on all four sides") is the defining geographic fact of the region.
四大名著	sì dà míng zhù	the Four Great Classical Novels — 四 sì + 大 dà (great) + 名著 míngzhù (famous work). The four canonical novels of Chinese literature: Romance of the Three Kingdoms (三国演义), Water Margin (水浒传), Journey to the West (西游记), and Dream of the Red Chamber (红楼梦).
四方	sì fāng	the four directions; all around; everywhere — 四 sì + 方 fāng (direction; side; square). Literally the four cardinal directions, and by extension every direction, all around. 四面八方 sìmiàn bāfāng ("four faces, eight directions") intensifies it to mean from every conceivable side. A 四方形 sìfāngxíng is a square or quadrilateral, the four-sided shape.
四季	sì jì	the four seasons — 四 sì + 季 jì (season). Spring (春 chūn), summer (夏 xià), autumn (秋 qiū), winter (冬 dōng). 四季如春 sìjì rú chūn ("four seasons like spring") describes a mild climate where it feels like spring all year, a stock phrase of praise for places such as Kunming.
四君子	sì jūn zǐ	Four Gentlemen — Four Gentlemen: the four emblematic plants of the literati painting tradition: prunus plum 梅 (perseverance), orchid 兰 (secluded virtue), bamboo 竹 (resilience), chrysanthemum 菊 (dignified retirement). Together they map the principal Confucian virtues onto natural forms.
四人帮	Sì rén bāng	Gang of Four (arrested 1976)
四象	sì xiàng	The Four Symbols — The Four Symbols: the four mythological animal guardians of the cardinal directions: Azure Dragon (east, spring), Vermilion Bird (south, summer), White Tiger (west, autumn), Black Tortoise (north, winter).
送别	sòng bié	to bid farewell, see off — 送 (see off) plus 别 (to part). A literary, emotionally weighted word for the moment of parting, the subject of countless Tang poems written at the roadside or the river crossing.
送达	sòng dá	to deliver, serve (formally) — 送 (send) plus 达 (reach, arrive). A formal verb stressing successful arrival, used for delivered mail and, in law, for serving legal documents.
送给	sòng gěi	to give to (someone) — 送 (give) plus 给 (to, for the benefit of). The recipient-marking pattern: 送给 + person + thing. The thing crosses over and stays with the receiver.
送魂	sòng hún	soul guiding — escorting the dead to the afterlife — 送魂 (guiding the soul , lit. "sending the soul") is the funerary function of the shaman: after death, the soul of the deceased may be disoriented, confused about its condition, or unable to find the path to the spirit world.
送货	sòng huò	to deliver goods — 送 (send) plus 货 (goods). Door-to-door delivery: the courier who 送货 to your home, the staple verb of online shopping and takeout.
送子	sòng zǐ	child-giving (form of Guanyin)
苏菜	Sū cài	Jiangsu Cuisine
苏式面	Sū shì miàn	Suzhou-Style Noodles
算是	suàn shì	counts as; could be considered
孙中山	Sūn Zhōng shān	Sun Yat-sen, father of the republic
孙子	sūn zi	grandson
所爱	suǒ ài	that which is loved; the one one loves
所见	suǒ jiàn	that which is seen; what one sees
所说的话	suǒ shuō de huà	the words spoken; what was said
所有	suǒ yǒu	all; every; everything one has — 所 suǒ (that which; a place; nominalizing particle) + 有 (have/exist). 所有 as a determiner: 所有的学生 (all the students). As a noun: 他失去了所有 (He lost everything he had). As a formal possession compound in legal/written Chinese: 所有权 suǒyǒuquán = ownership rights, title.
所在	suǒ zài	where something is; the location of; what something lies in — 所 suǒ (that which; nominalizing particle) + 在 (to be located). A formal noun meaning "the place/point where something is located; the essence of what something consists in." Commonly appears in 问题所在 (the crux of the problem; where the problem lies) and 关键所在 (the key point; where the key lies).
所做的事	suǒ zuò de shì	the things done; what was done
他回来就走	tā huí lái jiù zǒu	he leaves as soon as he comes back (no pause between) — When 就 connects two verbs or clauses, it signals immediate, seamless sequence , the second action follows the first without pause. This is the "as soon as" function: the first event triggers the second instantly. No 一 is needed here , just the bare sequence: Clause 1 + 就 + Clause 2.
他们	tā men	they (mixed or male group)
他们正在开会	tā men zhèng zài kāi huì	They are in a meeting right now — One of the most useful office and workplace phrases. 开会 kāihuì = to hold/attend a meeting. The progressive here signals that interrupting is not appropriate. Variant: 他们在开会，请稍后再来 (They are in a meeting , please come back later). 正在 gives it a slight formality appropriate to professional contexts.
他人	tā rén	others; other people (formal/literary) — 他 tā (he; other) + 人 rén. The formal and written equivalent of 别人. Appears in legal texts, philosophical writing, ethical discussions, and formal prose. In classical Chinese, 他 itself meant "other" before it was narrowed to the third-person pronoun, making 他人 doubly "other."
他是开车去的	tā shì kāi chē qù de	he went by car (FOCUS: by car, not by train, not walking) — Manner focus: the verb of transportation or method of doing something sits between 是 and 去的 (or the main verb + 的). The presupposition is that he went somewhere , the new information is how. 开车 (drive a car) is the manner. Other manners: 坐飞机 by plane, 骑车 by bike, 走路 on foot, 打电话 by phone.
他是去年来的	tā shì qùn ián lái de	he came last year (FOCUS: last year, not any other year) — Both speakers know he came; the question is when. 是去年 places "last year" in the focus position, bracketed by 是 and …来的. The construction answers a question or corrects a misunderstanding about the timing: "No, it wasn't three years ago, it was last year."
他乡	tā xiāng	a strange land; a place away from home
她们	tā men	they (all-female group) — 她 + 们 (men, plural marker). Used only when every member of the group is known to be female. If the group is mixed-gender or the gender is unknown, the default is 他们 (tāmen) , the male-radical form is the unmarked plural.
她是今天早上走的	tā shì jīn tiān zǎo shang zǒu de	she left this morning (FOCUS: this morning, not yesterday) — More precise time focus , this morning, as opposed to last night or yesterday. The presupposition is clear: she has already left (both speakers know this). The new information is the precise time of departure. Commonly used in conversations about arrivals, departures, and events.
她字	tā zì	"the 她 character" — shorthand for the gendered-pronoun debate — A meta-term in linguistic and feminist writing. 她字之争 ("the dispute over 她") refers to the recurring debate over whether to use 她, abolish it, or replace it with a non-marked alternative.
太后	tài hòu	empress dowager; the emperor's mother — 太 tài (grand; senior; beyond; supreme) + 后 (empress). The emperor's mother or grandmother who has survived her husband's reign.
太极	tài jí	the Supreme Ultimate; Taiji (the source of yin and yang) — 太 tài (supreme) + 极 jí (the pole; the ultimate). In the Yijing tradition, the Supreme Ultimate from which 阴 yīn and 阳 yáng divide. The familiar swirl symbol is the 太极图. The slow martial art is 太极拳 tàijíquán.
太极拳	tài jí quán	Tai Chi Chuan — the soft internal martial art based on yin-yang principles — 太极 tàijí (the Supreme Ultimate , the cosmological concept) + 拳 quán (fist; boxing style). The soft, circular martial art based on the principles of 阴阳: yielding overcomes force, softness overcomes hardness, stillness overcomes motion. A paradigm of 内功 (internal cultivation).
太监	tài jiān	eunuch official
太了	tài le	too… (excessive)
太太	tài tai	Mrs.; madam; wife (polite) — The doubled 太, the senior lady of the house. Used as a title after a surname (王太太 "Mrs. Wang") and as a polite word for one's own or another's wife. The 太 marks seniority and respect.
太学	Tài xu é	Imperial Academy
坦白	tǎn bái	to confess; come clean
汤面	tāng miàn	noodles in soup; noodle soup
堂哥	táng gē	older male cousin (paternal)
套房	tào fáng	suite, self-contained flat
特别是	tè bié shì	"Especially" or "particularly — "Especially" or "particularly." Singles out the most important case within a broader claim. 我喜欢中国菜, 特别是川菜 ("I like Chinese food, especially Sichuan food"). Direct, useful, common.
特长	tè cháng	special skill; particular strength; forte — 特 tè (special; particular) + 长 cháng (strength; advantage, the area where one "goes long"). 你有什么特长？ "What are your special skills?" is the standard job interview form. 特长生 = a student admitted on the basis of a special talent (music, sports, art) rather than exam scores alone.
提问	tí wèn	to ask a question; to raise a question — 提 tí (to raise; to bring up) + 问 wèn (to ask). To put a question forward, typically in a class or meeting. 老师提问 "the teacher asks a question," often of a student called on to answer.
体会	tǐ huì	to grasp through experience; the understanding so gained — 体 tǐ (body) + 会 huì (to understand). To come to understand a thing by living it, and the insight that results. 深刻的体会 "a deep understanding (won from experience)."
体积	tǐ jī	volume; bulk
体面	tǐ miàn	decent; respectable; dignified
体贴	tǐ tiē	considerate; thoughtful; attentive to others' needs — 体 tǐ (to feel in the body) + 贴 tiē (to stick close). To feel another's situation closely and act with care: 体贴的丈夫 "a considerate husband." It is warmth shown through attention to small needs.
体系	tǐ xì	framework; structured system (of ideas or institutions) — 体 tǐ (body; structure) + 系 xì (connection). An organized framework, usually of something abstract: 理论体系 "a theoretical framework," 教育体系 "the education system." More architectural and conceptual than the everyday 系统.
体验	tǐ yàn	to experience; to live through; an experience — 体 tǐ (body) + 验 yàn (to test, to verify). To know something by passing through it in person: 体验生活 "to experience real life." As a noun, the experience itself: 难忘的体验 "an unforgettable experience."
体育	tǐ yù	physical education; sports — 体 tǐ (body) + 育 yù (to nurture, to educate). The cultivation of the body: school PE, and sport in general. 体育馆 tǐyùguǎn is a gymnasium; 体育课 is a PE class.
体育馆	tǐ yù guǎn	gymnasium, indoor arena — 体育 tǐyù (physical education, sport) + 馆. The roofed sports hall, as opposed to the open-air 体育场 (chǎng, a field/stadium). The 馆 marks the covered, indoor venue.
体质	tǐ zhì	Constitution — Constitution: one's inherent physical and energetic type, which determines predispositions to certain diseases and guides appropriate diet, lifestyle, and treatment choices. Nine constitutional types are defined in modern TCM standardization.
体重	tǐ zhòng	body weight — 体 tǐ (body) + 重 zhòng (weight). How much a person weighs: 量体重 "to weigh oneself." The weight that the body itself carries.
天大	tiān dà	sky-high; enormous (informal)
天地间	tiān dì jiān	between heaven and earth; in this world — 间 jiān (between; the space between two things). 天地间 names the space of human existence. A frequent location phrase in classical and literary prose.
天地人	tiān dì rén	Heaven, Earth, Humanity — the Three Powers (三才 sāncái) — The three constitutive powers of the cosmos in classical Chinese thought. Heaven (天) provides the patterns and conditions from above; Earth (地) provides the material ground and resources from below; Humanity (人) stands between them, participating in both and responsible for the mediation.
天赋	tiān fù	natural gift; innate ability; heaven's endowment — 天 tiān (heaven) + 赋 fù (to endow; to bestow; to compose, originally the government's levy on subjects, then poetic composition). Heaven's formal bestowal. 天赋 is slightly more formal and literary than 天才, referring to specific aptitudes rather than a person of exceptional overall genius.
天干	tiāng ān	Heavenly Stems
天干地支	tiāng ān dì zhī	heavenly stems and earthly branches; the traditional Chinese calendrical system — The 60-year calendrical cycle built on the same yang/yin cosmology that underlies 乾坤. 天干 tiāngān (heavenly stems): ten yang elements cycling through the years (甲 jiǎ, 乙 yǐ, 丙 bǐng, 丁 dīng, etc.).
天国	tiāng uó	Kingdom of Heaven (Christian usage) — 天 tiān (heaven) + 国 guó (kingdom; nation). The standard Chinese translation of "Kingdom of Heaven" in Christian scripture.
天后宫	Tiān hòu Gōng	Tianhou Palace — her temples across the diaspora — After her elevation to 天后 (Heavenly Empress) by the Qing Emperor in 1683, Mazu's temples were formally renamed 天后宫 Tiānhòu Gōng (Tianhou Palace).
天界	tiān jiè	the celestial realm; heaven; the realm of gods and immortals — 天 (heaven; sky) + 界 (boundary; realm; world). The realm above 人间, populated by gods (神 shén), immortals (仙 xiān), and celestial officials. In folk-religion cosmology it mirrors the imperial bureaucracy below, with the Jade Emperor (玉皇大帝 Yù Huáng Dàdì) at its head.
天山	Tiān shān	The Heavenly Mountains — The Heavenly Mountains: the mountain range dividing northern and southern Xinjiang, with peaks reaching 7,443 m. A UNESCO World Natural Heritage Site, the Tianshan is the water source for the oasis cities of the Tarim Basin and one of the great mountain ranges of Central Asia.
天生	tiān shēng	innate; born with; naturally endowed — 天 tiān (heaven) + 生 shēng (to be born; to live). "Born of heaven" = innate, not acquired. The counterpart to 后天 hòutiān (acquired; postnatal). In classical philosophy, the 先天/后天 dichotomy maps roughly onto nature/nurture, one of the most enduring debates in Chinese intellectual life.
天台宗	Tiān tái Zōng	Tiantai — the comprehensive synthesis school — Founded by Zhiyi 智顗 (538–597 CE) on Mount Tiantai 天台山 in Zhejiang. Tiantai's great achievement was doctrinal classification 判教 pānjìao: organizing all Buddhist scriptures into a hierarchical scheme of "five periods and eight teachings," with the Lotus Sutra 法华经 at the apex.
天堂	tiān táng	paradise; heaven as a place of bliss — 天 tiān (heaven) + 堂 táng (hall; grand hall; a place of gathering). The "heavenly hall," used in Chinese Buddhism for the Pure Land, and in general usage for any paradise-like place. Also commonly used hyperbolically: 美食天堂 (food paradise), 购物天堂 (shopping paradise), 自行车天堂 (cycling paradise).
天下第一	tiān xià dì yī	number one under heaven; the best in the world; the supreme superlative — The hyperbolic superlative: beyond which there is nothing greater. 天下 replaces "in the world," and 第一 means "first/number one." Used for scenic spots (桂林山水甲天下 , Guilin's scenery is finest under heaven), for self-proclaimed champions (自称天下第一 , to call oneself the best in the world), and in historical…
天志	tiān zhì	the will of Heaven — Mohist moral authority — Unlike Confucian ethics, which grounds moral authority in tradition, the sage-kings, and the cultivation of 仁 rén (humaneness), Mozi appealed to 天 tiān (Heaven) as a purposive moral agent that actively wills universal love and punishes those who violate it.
天子	tiān zǐ	Son of Heaven — the Emperor — 天 tiān (heaven) + 子 zǐ (son; child). The Chinese emperor's formal title: he is heaven's son, the intermediary between the cosmic order and humanity, not a god himself but heaven's appointed representative on earth. The title dates to the Zhou dynasty and was used through the Qing (ended 1912).
听从	tīng cóng	to comply with; to follow (advice, instructions) — 听 (to listen, to heed) + 从 cóng (to follow; to comply). To hear and act accordingly , to accept and follow guidance, instructions, or authority. Used for following medical advice (听从医嘱 tīngcóng yīzhǔ), following a superior's direction, or complying with a decision.
听话	tīng huà	obedient; to do as one is told — 听 (to listen) + 话 huà (words; speech). Obedient in the sense of listening to what is said and acting accordingly. The standard word for a well-behaved child: 这孩子很听话 (this child is very obedient).
听见	tīng jiàn	to hear
听起来	tīng qǐ lái	sounds like
听说	tīng shuō	to have heard (that); reportedly; I've heard that — 听 (to hear) + 说 shuō (to say; to speak). A hedging marker for secondhand information: "I've heard it said that…" Placed at the start of a clause to flag that the speaker did not witness the information directly. 听说他去北京了 (I heard he went to Beijing).
听着！	tīng zhe !	Listen! / Listen up! (and stay listening) — The sustained-attention imperative with 着. 听 alone is a simple command to listen; 听着 adds the sense of "maintain that listening state." Common before announcements, reprimands, or instructions that require focused, continuous attention.
同伴	tóng bàn	companion; fellow
同胞	tóng bāo	compatriot; fellow citizen
同时	tóng shí	at the same time; meanwhile; moreover — 同 tóng (same) + 时 shí (time). The same moment: 同时进行 "to proceed simultaneously." As a connector it also means "at the same time, moreover," adding a further point.
同事	tóng shì	colleague; co-worker — 同 tóng (same) + 事 shì (work; affair). One who shares the same work, a colleague. Built on the same pattern as 同学: the shared station named by what is held in common.
同学	tóng xu é	classmate; fellow student; (as address) student — 同 tóng (same) + 学 xué (study). One who studies alongside you. A teacher also uses 同学 to address a pupil: 这位同学 "this student here." 老同学 means an old schoolmate.
同样	tóng yàng	same; alike; likewise — 同 tóng (same) + 样 yàng (kind). Of the same kind, used before a noun (同样的方法 "the same method") or as a sentence adverb meaning "in the same way, likewise."
同志	tóng zhì	comrade; (colloquial) a gay person — 同 tóng (same) + 志 zhì (aspiration; will). One of the same aspiration. The standard revolutionary address for "comrade," and in contemporary informal use a self-chosen word for gay people.
铜人	tóng rén	Bronze man — Bronze man: the life-sized bronze figure commissioned in 1026 CE with channel lines and point locations engraved on the surface and a hollow interior. Filled with water and wax for examination purposes, it was the world's first standardized anatomical teaching model for acupuncture instruction.
童话	tóng huà	fairy tale; children's story — 童 tóng (child) + 话 huà (story). A tale told for children. Its kin 神话 shénhuà (god-story) means a myth, and 神话 and 童话 together cover the world of fabulous stories.
童年	tóng nián	childhood
统一	tǒng yī	unification; to unify; uniform — 统 tǒng (to govern; to bind together) + 一. The political constant of Chinese civilization: 天下统一 (unification of all under heaven). As verb: 统一思想 (unify thinking), 统一标准 (standardize). As adjective: 统一规格 (uniform specifications).
投票	tóu piào	to vote: to cast a ballot — 投 tóu (to throw; to cast; to drop in) + 票 piào (slip; ballot). To vote, literally to throw a slip into the box. The image is exact: a ballot is a slip of paper, and casting it is dropping it into the urn.
团体	tuán tǐ	organization; group
退热	tuì rè	to bring down a fever — 退 (tuì, to recede) plus 热 (heat, fever): to make the fever fall. The word on a fever-reducing medicine box and what a worried parent waits for through the night.
袜子	wà zi	socks
外丹	wài dān	outer alchemy — elixirs of immortality — 外 wài (outer) + 丹 dān (cinnabar). The earlier tradition: physically preparing elixirs in a laboratory by heating and transforming minerals (especially cinnabar, lead, gold, and sulfur) to produce an immortality drug.
外国	wài guó	foreign country; abroad — 外 wài (outside) + 国 guó (country). A country other than one''s own. A person from one is 外国人 wàiguórén (foreigner); the things of one are 外国 X, as in 外国文化 "foreign culture."
外交	wài jiāo	diplomacy
外卖	wài mài	takeout; food delivery — 外 wài (out) + 卖 mài (to sell). Food sold to be taken away rather than eaten in. In modern usage it means app-based delivery: 点外卖 "to order takeout," 外卖员 "a delivery rider."
外面	wài miàn	outside; the outside; outdoors — 外 wài (outside) + 面 miàn (side; surface). The everyday word for the area beyond an enclosed space, or the outer surface of a thing. Its pair is 里面 lǐmiàn (inside).
外婆	wài pó	maternal grandmother (mother''s mother) — 外 wài (outside the surname) + 婆 pó (old woman). One''s mother''s mother, marked 外 because she belongs to the maternal line. Its pair is 外公 wàigōng (maternal grandfather); the father''s parents are 奶奶 and 爷爷.
外滩	Wài tān	The Bund , the waterfront promenade along the Huangpu River, lined with the banking and trading buildings of the concession era. The name means literally "outer mudflat." Now a heritage district and one of the most photographed urban frontages in China.
玩笑	wán xiào	a joke; a prank; banter — 玩 wán (to play) + 笑 xiào (laugh). Play-laughter: a joke made between people who are at ease together. 开玩笑 kāi wánxiào (to crack a joke, to be kidding) is one of the highest-frequency phrases in spoken Mandarin.
玩笑话	wán xiào huà	words said in jest
万里	wàn lǐ	ten thousand li; immense distance; vast — 万 (ten thousand) + 里 lǐ (Chinese unit of distance, roughly 500m). Ten thousand li is nominally 5,000 km, but the compound functions as a hyperbole for any vast, daunting distance. 万里长城 (Wànlǐ Chángchéng, "the ten-thousand-li Great Wall") is the most famous usage.
万岁	wàn suì	ten thousand years; long live (acclamation) — 万 (ten thousand) + 岁 suì (years of age; year). The imperial acclamation: 皇帝万岁 (huángdì wàn suì), "long live the Emperor." Reserved exclusively for the emperor in traditional usage; to use it for anyone else was a capital offense.
万象	wàn xiàng	all phenomena; the ten thousand forms
万一	wàn yī	just in case; in the unlikely event that; one in ten thousand — 万 (ten thousand) + 一 (one). Literally, one part in ten thousand , the probability so small it barely registers. In usage: a concession to contingency.
网站	wǎng zhàn	website — 网 (wǎng, net) + 站 (station): a website, a fixed point on the internet. The everyday word for any site you visit, from a shop to a search engine.
往年	wǎng nián	former years
往生	wǎng shēng	going toward rebirth — the Pure Land term for death as aspiration — 往 wǎng (to go toward) + 生 shēng (life; rebirth). The Pure Land term for a practitioner's death, framed not as loss but as aspiration fulfilled.
忘记	wàng jì	to forget
忘我	wàng wǒ	selfless absorption; to forget oneself
微小	wēi xiǎo	tiny; minuscule; microscopic — 微 wēi (minute; subtle; trace) + 小 xiǎo (small). Doubles the smallness: 微 already indicates near-invisibility (as in 微生物 microorganism, 微波 microwave), and 小 compounds that. Used for things at the scale of cells, particles, or imperceptible contributions. A higher register than 很小.
微笑	wēi xiào	to smile; a smile — 微 wēi (faint; slight; minute) + 笑 xiào (laugh/smile). A small, soundless laugh: the smile. The word that names the warm, closed-lipped expression, distinct from the open-mouthed 大笑.
唯一	wéi yī	the only one; sole; unique — 唯 wéi (only; alone) + 一. Used before nouns: 唯一的选择 (the only choice), 唯一的儿子 (only son). Stronger than 只有 zhǐyǒu in its emphasis on singularity , the uniqueness is total, not merely circumstantial. In formal or literary contexts: 唯一真神 (the one true God, in religious translation).
维系	wéi xì	to hold together; to maintain (a bond) — 维 wéi (to tie; to hold) + 系 xì (bind). To keep a relationship or order from coming apart: 维系感情 "to keep a bond alive," 维系和平 "to maintain peace." Two binding words doubled, the threads kept firmly in hand.
伟大	wěi dà	great; magnificent; lofty — 伟 wěi (towering, magnificent) + 大 dà. The highest register of greatness, used for great historical figures, great achievements, and (frequently in political contexts) the greatness of the Party or state. In everyday speech it can be slightly ironic if overused.
伪君子	wěi jūn zǐ	hypocrite; false gentleman — 伪 wěi (false, counterfeit) + 君子. A person who performs the outward markers of virtue without the inward character , the one who speaks of 义 while calculating 利. The word's force comes precisely from the prestige of what it negates.
卫生	wèi shēng	hygiene; health
卫生间	wèi shēng jiān	bathroom; toilet; restroom — 卫生 wèishēng (hygiene) + 间 jiān (room). The polite, standard word for a bathroom in homes and public buildings. A common synonym is 洗手间 xǐshǒujiān.
卫星	wèi xīng	satellite (natural or artificial) — 卫 (guard) + 星: the guard star that attends a planet. Covers the moon, the GPS constellation, and the dish antenna (卫星天线). The Great Leap Forward idiom 放卫星 ("launching a satellite") still means announcing an inflated achievement.
未来	wèi lái	the future (not yet arrived)
温和	wēn hé	gentle; mild; moderate
问候	wèn hòu	to send regards; to greet; to ask after someone — 问 wèn (to ask after) + 候 hòu (to wait on; to attend). To inquire warmly after another person, in speech or in writing: 致以亲切的问候 "to extend warm regards."
蜗牛	wō niú	snail — 蜗 (wō, the snail's shell, with the 虫 insect radical) + 牛. The little horns of a snail earned it the ox; the word doubles as a byword for anything slow.
我的	wǒ de	my, mine
我见	wǒ jiàn	personal view; one's own opinion
我觉得	wǒ ju é de	"I feel" or "I think — "I feel" or "I think." The default opinion frame in spoken Chinese. Softer than 我认为 (wǒ rènwéi, "I hold the view that"), which is the more formal written-register equivalent. 我觉得 carries no special weight; it is the natural way to introduce any personal take.
我来帮你 · 他去买菜	wǒ lái bāng nǐ tā qù mǎi cài	I came to help you · he went to buy groceries — 来 and 去 can be followed directly by a verb of purpose: "come/go in order to do [V]." This is structurally simple: 来/去 + verb phrase. The motion establishes the direction; the following verb specifies the purpose of the trip. This is one of the most frequent patterns in everyday speech.
我们	wǒ men	we, us
我也这么觉得	wǒ yě zhè me ju é de	"I feel the same way" or "I think so too — "I feel the same way" or "I think so too." Personal endorsement that aligns your view with the speaker's. Warmer than a simple agreement; signals shared perspective.
卧房	wò fáng	bedroom — 卧 (wò, to lie down) plus 房. The room for sleeping. In everyday speech 卧室 (wòshì) is now more common, but 卧房 survives and shows the same logic: the chamber where one lies down.
五常	wǔ cháng	the Five Constants; the five cardinal virtues of Confucian ethics — 仁 rén · 义 yì · 礼 lǐ · 智 zhì · 信 xìn. The five virtues held to be universal moral constants across cultures and times.
五谷	wǔ gǔ	the five grains; staple crops — 五 wǔ + 谷 gǔ (grain). The five staple grains of ancient China, variously listed (commonly rice, two kinds of millet, wheat, and beans). The exact list shifted by region and era, but the number stayed fixed at five.
五官	wǔ guān	the five facial features; the five senses — 五 wǔ + 官 guān (organ; office). The five sense organs of the face: eyes, ears, nose, tongue, and (in the common reckoning) the mouth or skin.
五四	Wǔ Sì	May Fourth (Movement) — context for 她's birth
五四运动	wǔ sì yùn dòng	May Fourth Movement — May Fourth Movement: the intellectual and cultural movement sparked by student protests on May 4, 1919, against the terms of the Treaty of Versailles. It catalyzed demands for science and democracy, rejection of Confucian tradition, and the development of a new vernacular literature.
五味	wǔ wèi	the five flavors — sour, bitter, sweet, pungent, salty — 五 wǔ (five) + 味 wèi (flavor, taste). Sour 酸 suān (Wood/Liver), bitter 苦 kǔ (Fire/Heart), sweet 甘 gān (Earth/Spleen), pungent/spicy 辛 xīn (Metal/Lung), salty 咸 xián (Water/Kidney). Each flavor enters and supports its corresponding organ system in moderate amounts; in excess, it taxes it.
五脏	wǔ zàng	the five yin organ systems — heart, liver, spleen, lung, kidney — 五 wǔ (five) + 脏 zàng (solid, yin, storing organ). In TCM, 五脏 refers to five functional systems, not five anatomical organs in the Western sense. Heart (心 xīn) , Fire; Liver (肝 gān) , Wood; Spleen (脾 pí) , Earth; Lung (肺 fèi) , Metal; Kidney (肾 shèn) , Water.
西安	xī ān	Xi'an, the nearby city
西餐	xī cān	Western food; Western cuisine — 西 (western) + 餐 (meal, cuisine). As opposed to 中餐 zhōngcān (Chinese food). In Chinese urban culture, 西餐厅 (Western restaurant) typically means a European-style restaurant with table settings, bread, and fork-and-knife service.
西藏	Xī zàng	Tibet
西方	xī fāng	the West; western direction; Pure Land (Buddhist) — 西 (west) + 方 (direction; region). The context determines which of the three senses is meant: cosmological/Buddhist (the Pure Land), historical-geographic (Western Regions), or modern geopolitical (the liberal West). When paired with 东方, it is almost always the modern cultural/civilizational sense.
西施	Xī Shī	Xi Shi, one of the Four Beauties
西洋	Xī yáng	Western Ocean (Indian Ocean world)
西医	xī yī	Western medicine
西游记	Xī yóu jì	Journey to the West (16th-century novel) — 西 (west) + 游 (journey, travel) + 记 (record, account). The novel by Wú Chéng'ēn (吴承恩, c. 1500–1582) following the monk Xuánzàng, the Monkey King (孙悟空 Sūn Wùkōng), Pig (猪八戒 Zhū Bājiè), and Sandy (沙僧 Shā Sēng) on their journey to India to retrieve Buddhist scriptures.
西域	xī yù	the Western Regions
西周	Xī Zhōu	Western Zhou
喜爱	xǐ ài	to love; to be fond of; to favor — 喜 xǐ (to delight in) + 爱 ài (to love). A warmer, more literary cousin of 喜欢, often used of things admired or cherished: 深受喜爱 "deeply loved (by the public)," 喜爱的作家 "a favorite author."
喜好	xǐ hào	a liking; a preference; to be keen on — 喜 xǐ (to like) + 好 hào (to be fond of, 4th tone). Both a verb and a noun for a settled preference or taste: 个人喜好 "personal preferences," 喜好读书 "fond of reading." Note 好 here is hào, not hǎo.
喜钱	xǐ qián	happy money — cash gifts at joyous occasions
喜庆	xǐ qìng	Auspicious, festive — Auspicious, festive: the quality of being celebratory and lucky. Red is described as 喜庆 by default; white and black are explicitly not 喜庆 in gift or festive contexts.
喜丧	xǐ sāng	A "joyful funeral" — A "joyful funeral": the death of a person who lived to old age and left many descendants. Treated as cause for celebration alongside mourning, and marked with comparatively festive elements: the music may be more exuberant, the occasion more public.
喜事	xǐ shì	a happy occasion; a joyous event; (esp.) a wedding — 喜 xǐ (joy) + 事 shì (matter, affair). Any auspicious event, and by default a wedding. Its standing opposite is 丧事 sāngshì, a funeral, the unhappy event.
喜悦	xǐ yu è	joy; delight; gladness — 喜 xǐ (joy) + 悦 yuè (pleased). A literary, slightly elevated word for inner gladness, the quiet glow of a good thing: 丰收的喜悦 "the joy of a good harvest," 内心的喜悦 "the delight within."
系列	xì liè	series; set (of related things) — 系 xì (string together) + 列 liè (row; to arrange). A run of related items strung in a row: 系列产品 "a product series," 一系列问题 "a whole series of problems." The connecting 系 makes the separate items read as one ordered set.
系统	xì tǒng	system; systematic — 系 xì (connection) + 统 tǒng (to unify). An ordered whole of interworking parts: 操作系统 "operating system," 神经系统 "nervous system." As an adjective it means systematic: 系统地学习 "to study systematically."
下班	xià bān	to finish work; to get off work; end of shift — 下 xià (to descend; to leave the higher platform) + 班 bān (shift; work roster). To come down from the work platform , to leave one's post at the end of the day. The indispensable pair of 上班. 下班后 (after work) is the default framing for evening activities in Chinese urban conversation.
下蛋	xià dàn	to lay eggs
下课	xià kè	class is over; end of class; to dismiss class — 下 xià (to end; to come down from) + 课 kè (lesson; class). Class has descended from its height, releasing students and teacher. The verb pair 上课/下课 (class begins/class ends) is the daily rhythm of Chinese school life. 下课了！is the joyful announcement that class is over.
下来	xià lái	come down; slow to a stop
下面	xià miàn	below; underneath; the lower surface; the following — 下 xià (below; under) + 面 miàn (face; side; surface). The underside; what is below. Used for physical position (床下面 = under the bed) and for discourse organization (下面请看 = please see the following).
下去	xià qù	continue; go down
下水	xià shuǐ	to enter the water; sewage
下载	xià zài	To download — To download: carry down. Paired with 上传 (upload). 载 also reads zǎi meaning "year" in classical texts, a polyphone trap for advanced readers.
下周	xià zhōu	next week — 下 xià (next; the following) + 周 zhōu (week). The temporal use of 下: what descends next into the present from the future. Consistent across all time units: 下周 (next week), 下个月 (next month), 下年 / 明年 (next year).
夏天	xià tiān	summer
先生	xiān sheng	Mr.; teacher; husband; gentleman — 先 xiān (first; ahead; earlier) + 生 shēng (one born; person). "One born earlier" , originally a teacher or Confucian master; now the standard polite term for "Mr." and also "husband" in formal contexts (我先生 "my husband"). A word whose meaning has drifted beautifully from teacher → gentleman → Mr.
鲜花	xiān huā	fresh flowers; fresh-cut flowers — 鲜 xiān (fresh) + 花 huā (flower). Living, fresh-cut blossoms, the flowers you buy and give: 一束鲜花 "a bouquet of fresh flowers." Distinct from 假花 jiǎhuā, artificial flowers.
咸蛋	xián dàn	salted egg
现场	xiàn chǎng	the scene; on-site; live (in person) — 现 xiàn (present) + 场 chǎng (site; field). The very place where something is happening: 案发现场 "the scene of a crime," 现场直播 "a live broadcast."
湘菜	Xiāng cài	Hunan Cuisine
享有	xiǎng yǒu	to enjoy (a right or privilege)
巷子	xiàng zi	alley, lane
小乘	xiǎo chéng	Theravada — the Lesser Vehicle (pejorative Mahayana term)
小弟	xiǎo dì	this humble younger brother (self-deprecating first person) — Used by a male speaker to refer to himself deferentially, regardless of birth order. The formula downsizes the speaker relative to the listener. Also used literally for a younger brother. In gangster/triad contexts, 小弟 means a low-ranking follower , the hierarchy is explicit.
小哥	xiǎo gē	young fellow (polite address)
小孩	xiǎo hái	child; kid — 小 xiǎo (small) + 孩 hái (child). The standard neutral word for a child. Related: 小朋友 xiǎopéngyou (young friend , the standard address for children, used by teachers and adults), 小孩子 xiǎoháizi (a slightly more affectionate variant with the diminutive 子).
小鸡	xiǎo jī	chick, baby chicken — 小 (xiǎo, small) + 鸡 (chicken). The fluffy yellow chick just out of the shell, a favorite in children's songs and picture books.
小姐	xiǎo jiě	miss; young lady — 小 (small, young) plus 姐 is the classic polite title for a young, unmarried woman, like English "Miss." Register matters: in some mainland contexts a bare 小姐 can sound off, so service settings often prefer 美女 (měinǚ) or a surname plus 姐.
小令	xiǎo lìng	Short lyric form — Short lyric form: tune patterns of 58 characters or fewer. The most concentrated 词 form, requiring the greatest compression. Contrasted with 长调 chángdiào or 慢词 màncí, the extended long forms of 91 characters or more.
小名	xiǎo míng	Childhood nickname , a pet name used within the family, often deliberately humble or silly to avoid attracting bad luck to the child. 狗蛋 (Gǒudàn, "Dog Egg") or 二毛 (Èrmáo, "Second Fuzz") are classic examples.
小时	xiǎo shí	hour (unit of time) — 小 xiǎo (small) + 时 shí (time; hour). A small slice of time: one hour. The compound reflects a traditional view of time's granularity , the hour as the smallest standard unit. Contrast 小时候 xiǎoshíhou (childhood, "when one was small"), where 小时 carries the older sense of "young age."
小时候	xiǎo shí hou	in childhood; when (one) was young — "When little," a fixed expression for one's childhood. 我小时候 = "when I was a child." Compare 那时候 (back then, at that time) and 到时候 (when the time comes). Note: 小时 (without 候) means "an hour," a different word, so the 候 matters.
小说	xiǎo shuō	novel; fiction — 小 xiǎo (small; minor) + 说 shuō (speech; theory). Literally "minor talk" , the genre of fiction was long considered a lower form of literature compared to history and philosophy. The term was coined to contrast with 大道 "the Great Way" of classical learning.
小篆	xiǎo zhuàn	small seal script — the Qin standardized writing form — Lesser Seal Script , standardized by the Qin minister Li Si as part of the First Emperor's unification project. Characterized by even-weight strokes, systematic construction, and balanced proportions.
小组	xiǎo zǔ	small group
校长	xiào zhǎng	school principal; university president — 校 xiào (school) + 长 zhǎng (elder; head; chief). The head of a school at any level, from primary school principal to university president. The pattern [institution] + 长 = head of that institution is extraordinarily productive: 院长 (dean), 部长 (minister), 市长 (mayor), 省长 (governor), 班长 (class monitor).
笑话	xiào hua	a joke; to laugh at (someone) — 笑 xiào (laugh) + 话 huà (speech; words). Laugh-words: a joke, a funny story. As a verb it flips to "to laugh at, to make a fool of." 讲笑话 = to tell a joke; 闹笑话 = to make an embarrassing blunder that others laugh at.
笑着说	xiào zhe shuō	said with a smile; said, smiling — The canonical attendant circumstance: 笑着 (smiling) + 说 (to say). The 着 links the two actions , the smiling frames and accompanies the speaking. This construction is the default way to describe facial expression or gesture accompanying speech in Chinese narrative.
写下来	xiě xià lái	write it down — For technical terms and every figure. Hand over your notebook or phone. Pinyin input is fine; most people will type the characters for you.
写照	xiě zhào	a portrayal; a true reflection of reality
写字	xiě zì	to write characters; to write by hand — 写 xiě (to write) + 字 zì (character). The fundamental literacy act: hand meeting surface, character taking form. Specifically implies handwriting rather than typing or printing. In Chinese civilization, handwriting is never merely functional.
写作	xiě zuò	to write; to compose — literary register
星期	xīng qī	week; day of the week — 星 (star) + 期 (period): the star period, from the seven-luminary week. Days attach numbers: 星期三 is Wednesday. Asking the day is 今天星期几? , one of the few questions where 几 needs no measure word.
星宿	xīng xiù	the lunar mansions
星星	xīng xing	Star, in the affectionate reduplicated form used in speech and songs. Single 星 does the compounding work: 星空 (starry sky), 星系 (galaxy, "star system"), 星座 (constellation, and the zodiac sign small talk runs on).
星座	xīng zuò	constellation; zodiac sign — 星 + 座 (seat): the seats of the stars. In young urban small talk it means the Western zodiac sign: 你是什么星座的? has largely displaced asking one's Chinese zodiac animal among the under-forty, an ironic import given the antiquity of native Chinese astral lore.
姓名	xìng míng	full name — surname + given name — 姓 xìng (surname) + 名 míng (given name). The formal term for a full name. Note: Chinese names are surname-first, given-name-second, the reverse of Western convention. 你叫什么名字？= "What is your name?" (informal, just the name). 请问您的姓名？= "May I ask your full name?" (formal).
胸有乾坤	xiōng yǒu qián kūn	"heaven and earth in the breast" — a person of sweeping strategic vision and depth — 胸 xiōng (chest; breast; the seat of mind and intention in classical Chinese thought) + 有 (to have; to hold) + 乾坤. A grand classical expression of praise for someone who holds the entire world in mind: a strategist, a statesman, a general, a writer who sees at the largest possible scale.
需要	xū yào	to need; to require (necessity, not merely desire) — 需 xū (to need; to require, a more formal or objective necessity) + 要. 需要 focuses on objective need rather than personal desire. 我需要帮助 (I need help , genuinely require it) vs. 我要帮助 (I want help , requesting it).
宣读	xuān dú	to read out formally (a statement, verdict, decree) — 宣 xuān (to announce; to proclaim) + 读. To read aloud in a formal setting , a court verdict, a wedding vow, an official document at a ceremony. The 宣 marks the public, declarative force.
选票	xuǎn piào	ballot: a ballot paper; a vote (as a counted unit) — 选 xuǎn (to choose; to elect) + 票 piào (slip; ballot). The physical ballot paper you mark and cast, and by extension a vote as a unit to be counted. The tally of votes is 票数 piàoshù, and to win is to gather the most 选票. Here 票 is the slip that carries a single person's choice.
学坏	xu é huài	to pick up bad habits, to be led astray — 学 (to learn) + 坏 (bad). To learn the wrong things, the classic parental worry that good company makes good children and bad company corrupts them. 坏 here works as a result complement: the learning turns out badly.
学会	xu é huì	to master, to learn (successfully) — Study + acquire-the-skill. 学了三年 states duration; 学会了 states victory. The complement 会 restricts itself to learnable skills, which is a nice grammatical definition of what a skill is.
学了三年	xu é le sān nián	studied for three years (bounded period) — 了 with a duration complement signals an action sustained for a period of time and treated as a completed, bounded episode. This construction is essential for talking about study, work, residence, and sustained experience. The duration frames the action as a finished chunk of time.
学生	xu é sheng	student — 学 xué (to study; to learn) + 生 shēng (one who is still "green"; a junior person). The student as someone still growing, not yet ripe. One of the most common nouns in Chinese , every school context relies on it.
学识	xu é shi	scholarly learning; erudition
学问	xu é wèn	learning; knowledge; scholarship — 学 xué (to study) + 问 wèn (to ask). Knowledge built by studying and asking, the deep, earned kind. 很有学问 "highly learned." The word itself teaches that real knowledge grows from questions, not answers alone.
学习	xu é xí	to study; to learn (ongoing process) — 学 xué (to learn) + 习 xí (to practice; to review; to get accustomed to). The compound captures the Confucian dual process: 学 (receiving new knowledge) + 习 (practicing and consolidating it). Not just acquisition but repeated reinforcement. The most common word for "to study" in general.
学校	xu é xiào	school
学院	xu é yuàn	college, institute
学者	xu é zhě	scholar; academic; researcher — 学 (to study) + 者. The standard modern word for a scholar or academic researcher. This compound has been in continuous use since at least the Han dynasty. A 学者 is someone whose work consists of systematic study , the word appears in book reviews, conference programs, and academic citations alike.
询问	xún wèn	to inquire; to ask about; to seek information — 询 xún (to inquire) + 问 wèn (to ask). A formal verb for asking in order to gather information, common in writing and service contexts: 询问情况 "to inquire about the situation," 向某人询问 "to make inquiries of someone."
荀子	Xún zǐ	Xunzi
压岁钱	yā suì qián	lucky money for children at New Year — New Year's money, given in red envelopes; literally "money that suppresses the year-beast." The year-beast (岁 suì) was a mythological monster; the money and noise were protective against it.
鸭蛋	yā dàn	duck egg; a zero (slang) — 鸭 (yā, duck) + 蛋: a duck egg, larger and richer than a hen's. Among students it means a score of zero, since the round 0 on a test paper looks like an egg sitting there.
揠苗助长	yà miáo zhù zhǎng	pulling up seedlings to help them grow
烟火	yān huǒ	fireworks; smoke and fire; the warmth of lived life — 烟 yān (smoke) + 火 huǒ (fire). Literally: fireworks. Poetically: 人间烟火 rénjiān yānhuǒ , "the smoke and fire of the human world" , the warmth and vitality of ordinary life.
严重	yán zhòng	serious — Say plainly how serious it is; understatement is heard as "not urgent." 影响很大 (the impact is large) and 不能接受 (bù néng jiēshòu, cannot be accepted) are firm without being rude.
炎热	yán rè	blazing hot (summer)
演唱	yǎn chàng	to perform (a song); vocal performance — 演 yǎn (to perform) + 唱 chàng (to sing). To sing before an audience as a performance. 演唱会 yǎnchànghuì is a concert, the standard word for a pop or vocal live show.
羊肉	yáng ròu	mutton, lamb — 羊 (yáng, sheep/goat) + 肉 (meat). The meat of choice in the northwest, central to hotpot and skewers.
洋务运动	Yáng wù Yùn dòng	Self-Strengthening Movement
养生	yǎng shēng	health cultivation; nourishing life
样板戏	yàng bǎn xì	model operas (sole permitted art form)
样子	yàng zi	appearance; look; manner; the way it seems — 样 yàng (appearance) + 子 zi (noun-suffix). The look or manner of a person or thing. The idiom 看样子 kàn yàngzi means "from the looks of it." For the toneless suffix 子, see the 子 entry.
药食同源	yào shí tóng yuán	food and medicine share an origin
要 · 打算 · 准备	yào dǎ suàn zhǔn bèi	intention and plan: what someone is going to do — 要 before a verb marks intention or imminence: 我要回家 (I'm going home / I want to go home; context decides). 打算 dǎsuàn (to plan) and 准备 zhǔnbèi (to prepare, to be about to) make the plan explicit: 我打算明年去中国 (I plan to go to China next year).
要是	yào shi	if; supposing (colloquial conditional) — 要 (want/will) + 是 shì (be/this). A colloquial conditional meaning "if; supposing that." Slightly warmer and more personally invested than the neutral 如果 rúguǒ. Implies the speaker has some stake in the condition. Typically paired with 就 jiù in the consequence clause.
也 , 追加	yě , zhuī jiā	也: one more added to an existing set — 也 always implies that the predicate has already been established for another entity. The sentence using 也 adds one more item. This requires a prior context (explicit or implicit) in which the predicate was already true of someone or something else. 也 cannot start a discourse out of nowhere.
也好	yě hǎo	that works too; either way is fine
也就是说	yě jiù shì shuō	"In other words" or "what I mean is — "In other words" or "what I mean is." The restatement marker. Used to reframe something you just said in clearer or simpler terms. Indispensable when you sense the listener has not quite caught your point.
也是	yě shì	that is also true; that's fair
也许	yě xǔ	maybe; perhaps
也许吧	yě xǔ ba	"Maybe so — "Maybe so." Noncommittal acknowledgment that signals you are not convinced but are not interested in arguing the point. The 吧 keeps it soft. Functionally close to English "I guess" or "could be."
叶子	yè zi	leaf
一把汗	yī bǎ hàn	a palm's worth of sweat — anxious, breaking out in a sweat — 把 can measure abstract quantities that could theoretically fill a cupped hand , a handful of something. 一把汗 (a palm of sweat) is an idiom for being anxious or nervous to the point of sweating. 一把泪 (a handful of tears) appears in literary contexts for weeping.
一把年纪	yī bǎ nián jì	getting on in age; a certain age (often with mild reproach) — 一把年纪 literally "a handful of years" , used to describe someone who is getting on in age. Often carries a mildly reproachful or surprised tone: someone that age should know better, or it's surprising they're doing something. Common in colloquial speech and semi-formal contexts.
一把椅子	yī bǎ yǐ zi	a chair (one handle-shaped piece of furniture) — 把 as a measure word applies to objects that have a handle or can be grasped, or that you can take in hand , chairs (by the back), tools, weapons, fans, keys, locks, umbrellas, guitars. The connection to the core meaning of 把 (to grip) is transparent.
一般	yī bān	general; average; so-so
一杯水	yī bēi shuǐ	a cup / glass of water — 杯 bēi — 杯 bēi (cup, glass) counts liquids held in cups or glasses. The container becomes the classifier. This is the most transparent type: the classifier is the vessel. A large class of classifiers work this way , 碗, 盘, 瓶, 桶, 壶 , each named for the physical container holding the substance.
一点	yī diǎn	a little; a bit (post-verbal, neutral or positive) — 一 yī (one) + 点 diǎn (dot; bit). Appears after adjectives and verbs, typically in commands, requests, or comparisons. Neutral to positive in coloring. 便宜一点 (a little cheaper , as a request), 快一点 (a little faster), 多吃一点 (eat a little more). In comparisons: 比昨天冷一点 (a little colder than yesterday).
一点儿	yì diǎn r	a little; a bit (neutral, often comparative) — After an adjective, 一点儿 makes a neutral comparison, "a little more X": 大一点儿 (a bit bigger), 快一点儿 (a bit faster), 便宜一点儿 (a little cheaper). After a verb, it means "a little / some": 喝一点儿水 (drink a little water), 说一点儿 (say a bit).
一个	yí gè	one (with general measure word) — 一 + 个 (general measure word). 一 before tone 4 (个 gè) → yí. The most frequent combination of 一 in everyday speech: 一个人 yí gè rén (one person), 一个问题 yí gè wèntí (one question), 一个地方 yí gè dìfang (one place). 个 gè is the default measure word when no specific one applies.
一共	yí gòng	altogether; in total
一块儿	yí kuài r	together (colloquial)
一面	yī miàn	one side; one surface; (measure) one flat object — 一 yī (one) + 面 miàn (measure word). 面 counts flat, surface-like objects: 一面镜子 (a mirror), 一面墙 (a wall), 一面旗 (a flag). As a noun 一面 also means "one side" of an argument or "one aspect."
一生	yī shēng	one's whole life
一同	yì tóng	together (formal)
一下子	yí xià zi	all at once; suddenly — A related but distinct adverb: 一下子 means "all at once, in one go, suddenly," describing rapid or abrupt change rather than softening a verb: 天一下子黑了 ("the sky went dark all at once"). Do not confuse it with the verb-softening 一下: 看一下 ("have a look") versus 一下子看完了 ("finished reading it in one go").
一些	yī xiē	some (partitive)
一些 · 有的 · 有些	yī xiē yǒu de yǒu xiē	some: indefinite vs. some-of-a-set — 一些 introduces an indefinite amount: 我买了一些水果 (I bought some fruit). 有的 and 有些 pick out a subset of a known or generic whole, and they live at the front of the sentence: 有的人喜欢，有的人不喜欢 (some people like it, some don't). The classic restriction: 有的 phrases resist the object position.
一样	yí yàng	the same; alike; equally — Marks two things as the same. The frame is A 跟 B 一样 (跟 gēn or 和 hé link the two). Add an adjective to compare a specific quality as equal: A 跟 B 一样 + Adj. Negate as A 跟 B 不一样 ("different") or A 跟 B 不一样 + Adj. 一样 alone can also modify a noun: 一样的衣服 ("the same clothes").
一直	yī zhí	continuously, all along
一致	yī zhì	unanimous; consistent; in agreement — 一 + 致 zhì (to reach; to arrive at). All arriving at one point. 意见一致 (unanimous opinion), 行动一致 (acting in concert), 保持一致 (maintain consistency). Higher register than 一样 yīyàng , used in formal and official contexts. 众口一致 (everyone speaking with one mouth = unanimous) is a near-chengyu variant.
伊斯兰教	Yī sī lán jiào	Islam — practiced by the 色目人 administrators and merchants of the Yuan
医生	yī shēng	doctor; physician — 医 yī (medicine; to heal) + 生 shēng (practitioner). The standard word for doctor in mainland China. Note the 生 suffix , it originally meant "one who practices/studies" (compare 学生, 先生). 看医生 = "to see a doctor."
移动	yí dòng	to shift; move
疑问	yí wèn	doubt; query; an unresolved question — 疑 yí (to doubt) + 问 wèn (to ask). A question that arises from uncertainty: 毫无疑问 háo wú yíwèn "without the slightest doubt." Also the grammatical term, as in 疑问句 yíwènjù "interrogative sentence."
已读不回	yǐ dú bù huí	read but no reply
易安体	yì ān tǐ	The "Yi-an style" — The "Yi-an style": the distinctive lyric mode of Li Qingzhao (whose courtesy name was Yi-an 易安). Characterized by colloquial precision, physical specificity, and a technically innovative use of reduplicated binomes to convey emotional states.
驿站	yì zhàn	postal relay station
引进	yǐn jìn	to introduce; to bring in (technology, talent, methods) — 引 yǐn (to draw; to lead) + 进 jìn (to take in). To bring something in from outside, typically from abroad: 引进技术 "introduce technology," 引进人才 "bring in talent."
窨花	xùn huā	scenting — the process of infusing tea with flower fragrance
影子	yǐng zi	shadow
拥有	yōng yǒu	to own; to cherish; to hold close — 拥 yōng (to embrace; to hold close; to crowd together) + 有. More emotionally warm than 所有 or 具有. Suggests not just legal or abstract possession but holding something dear. Used for things of personal value: relationships, opportunities, qualities of character, cherished experiences.
优点	yōu diǎn	strong point; merit; advantage — 优 yōu (excellent) + 点 diǎn (point). A good point, a strength. Its exact opposite is 缺点 quēdiǎn, a shortcoming. Together they are the standard pair for weighing a person, plan, or product.
邮票	yóu piào	postage stamp: a stamp for mailing letters — 邮 yóu (post; mail) + 票 piào (slip). A postage stamp: a tiny printed slip stuck to an envelope that stands for prepaid postage. It is 票 in miniature, a small piece of paper carrying a fixed value, and a favorite object of collectors (集邮 jíyóu, stamp collecting).
友好	yǒu hǎo	friendly; amicable
有的	yǒu de	some (of a set); certain ones — 有 + 的 (nominalizing particle). Functions as an indefinite partitive , "some of [a set already mentioned or implied]." The pattern 有的…有的… (some…others…) distributes a set into contrasting subgroups and is one of the most natural ways to express "some people…some people…" in Chinese.
有点	yǒu diǎn	a bit; slightly (pre-verbal, negative or complaint coloring) — 有 + 点 diǎn (a dot; a small amount). Always appears before verbs and adjectives as a pre-modifier. The key nuance: 有点 carries a slightly negative or complaint-inflected tone. You use it for problems, inconveniences, or things that fall short.
有点儿	yǒu diǎn r	a bit; somewhat (often "a bit too…") — Placed before an adjective (or a feeling-verb) to say something is "a bit / somewhat" that way, usually with a tinge of complaint or an unwanted state. 有点儿 + 累 / 冷 / 贵 / 难 = a bit tired / cold / expensive / hard. It describes a current condition the speaker finds slightly undesirable.
有关	yǒu guān	related, relevant; concerned (with)
有关系	yǒu guān xi	to be related; to matter; to have a bearing on — 有关系 means there is a connection, relevance, or effect between two things. 这跟我有关系吗？= "Does this concern me / affect me?" Used to establish relevance, causation, or connection. Contrast with 有关 yǒuguān (related to; concerning , slightly more formal, often in 有关部门, "relevant departments").
有没有	yǒu méi yǒu	do you have? / is there? (A-not-A question) — The A-not-A question form of 有. Used both for possession ("do you have X?") and for existence ("is there X?"). In rapid speech, 有没有 is often compressed or the object follows immediately: 有没有时间？ Also used as a sentence-final tag: 你来过北京，有没有？ — "You've been to Beijing, haven't you?"
有面子	yǒu miàn zi	to have face; to be prestigious; to feel honored — To possess and display face , to be in a position of recognized prestige or to feel that your standing has been acknowledged or elevated. Certain possessions, positions, and associations give face: luxury goods, titles, famous friends, prestigious education.
有名	yǒu míng	famous; well-known — 有 yǒu (to have; to possess) + 名 míng (name; fame). To have a name that people know , the most common adjective for "famous" in spoken Mandarin. Neutral in register, works for people, places, dishes, and brands alike.
有钱	yǒu qián	to have money, rich
有人	yǒu rén	there is someone; someone (existential + indefinite reference) — 有 in the existential construction introduces a person into the discourse. 有人 can function as the subject of a following verb: 有人说… (Someone said…; There are people who say…). This is a common way to introduce an indefinite agent , "some people say," "someone thinks," "there are those who believe."
有时	yǒu shí	sometimes; at times — 有 + 时 shí (time). One of the most basic time adverbs: "at some times; sometimes." Often appears in the paired pattern 有时…有时… (sometimes…sometimes…) to describe alternating or variable states. 有时候 yǒu shíhou is the slightly more colloquial form (literally "at some time-instances").
有时候	yǒu shí hou	sometimes — "Sometimes," literally "there are times." Often shortened to 有时. The paired form 有时候…有时候… means "sometimes… and other times…," a neat way to describe alternating situations.
有事	yǒu shì	to be busy; to have something come up; to have a matter to attend to — 有 yǒu (have) + 事 shì. "Have a matter." Used to beg off (something has come up) or to flag that you need something from someone: 我有事找你 "I have something to discuss with you."
有所	yǒu suǒ	somewhat; to some extent; there is some degree of
有效期	yǒu xiào qī	validity period — How long a quote holds. Factories tie it to material prices, especially steel, and to the exchange rate (汇率 huìlǜ). A quote with no date can be reopened at any time.
有余涅槃	yǒu yú niè pán	nirvana with remainder; liberation while still embodied — 有余 (with a remainder) + 涅槃. The state of an awakened person, an arhat or a buddha, who has extinguished the three fires but still carries a body whose karmic momentum continues. The Buddha after his awakening at Bodh Gayā lived for forty more years; that life was 有余涅槃.
有缘	yǒu yuán	to have a karmic connection; fated to meet — 有缘 means that a karmic connection exists between people , that the conditions for their meeting were in place.
又来了	yòu lái le	here we go again — Deployed at the first bars of a familiar tune: the uncle's stock speech, the app's renewal popup, the roommate's diet announcement. 你又来了 aims it at a person, affection and exasperation mixed to taste.
玉皇大帝	Yù huáng Dà dì	the Jade Emperor — supreme deity of the folk pantheon — The Jade Emperor is the supreme ruler of Heaven in Chinese folk religion, governing both the divine and human realms from his celestial palace. His court mirrors a full imperial bureaucracy: he has ministers, generals, and a vast apparatus of supernatural governance.
遇见	yù jiàn	to run into, to encounter
愈来愈	yù lái yù	more and more (Taiwan variant)
原来	yuán lái	originally; as it turns out; so that's how it is — 原 yuán (original; the root; where something came from) + 来 lái (came from; arrived from). "From the original state," used in two senses. Temporal: originally, at first (他原来住在北京 = He originally lived in Beijing). Revelatory: "so that's how it is / now I understand" (原来如此！= So that's the reason!).
缘分未尽	yuán fèn wèi jìn	the karmic connection is not yet exhausted; there is still fate between us — 未 wèi = not yet; 尽 jìn = exhausted, used up. The karmic thread still has length to run , the connection between two people has not reached its natural conclusion.
缘分已尽	yuán fèn yǐ jìn	the karmic connection is exhausted; the fate between us has run its course — 已 yǐ = already; the connection has used up its allotted length. A peaceful, fatalistic way to accept a relationship ending , romantic, platonic, or professional.
月饼	yu è bǐng	mooncake — 月 yuè + 饼 bǐng (flat cake; pastry). The round cake eaten during Mid-Autumn Festival, shaped to mirror the full moon. Traditional fillings include lotus seed paste and salted egg yolk (representing the moon).
月光	yu è guāng	moonlight — 月 yuè + 光 guāng (light; beam; glory). Moonlight. One of the most evocative words in Chinese poetry , from Du Fu to Xu Zhimo, moonlight is the quintessential image of homesickness, longing, and beauty.
月亮	yu è liàng	the moon (colloquial) — 月 yuè + 亮 liàng (bright; to shine). The bright moon , the standard colloquial word for "the moon." More grounded and everyday than the poetic 明月 míngyuè. 看月亮 "to look at the moon" · 月亮出来了 "the moon has come out."
岳飞	Yu è Fēi	City God of Hangzhou — the martyr general — 岳飞 (1103–1142), the Song dynasty general celebrated for his campaigns against the Jurchen Jin invaders, was executed on fabricated charges at the order of the chancellor Qin Hui.
阅读	yu è dú	to read (with comprehension); reading (as a skill) — 阅 yuè (to inspect; to review) + 读. The formal, written-register word for reading-with-understanding. The standard term in education: 阅读理解 yuèdú lǐjiě "reading comprehension," 阅读量 yuèdú liàng "amount one has read." More deliberate than 看 (to look-at) and more comprehension-focused than 读 alone.
粤菜	yu è cài	Cantonese cuisine — 粤 yuè (Guangdong; Canton) + 菜 cài (cuisine). The cooking of Guangdong, prized for lightness, freshness, and steaming that keeps each ingredient tasting of itself. Dim sum (点心) belongs to this tradition.
运动	yùn dòng	sport; exercise; to work out; a (social or political) movement — 运 yùn (to move; to transport) + 动 dòng (to move). Bodily exercise and sport: 做运动 "to exercise," 运动员 yùndòngyuán (athlete). The same word names an organized social or political movement, a mass of people set in motion.
运送	yùn sòng	to transport, convey — 运 (move, transport) plus 送 (send). To carry goods or people over a distance, the language of logistics, supply lines, and freight.
在乎	zài hu	to care about; to mind; to take to heart — 在 (to be present) + 乎 hū (classical particle meaning "in; within; at"). Literally "to be located within [something]": one's attention or concern is present inside something. 我不在乎 (I don't care / I don't mind) is one of the most frequent phrases in spoken Chinese.
在我看来	zài wǒ kàn lái	"In my view — "In my view." Slightly more formal than 我觉得, and more deliberately frames what follows as a personal perspective. Useful when you want to signal that you have actually considered the question, not just reacting.
在一起	zài yì qǐ	together (in one place); as a couple — With 在, 一起 marks being gathered in one place: 我们住在一起 ("we live together"), 把书放在一起 ("put the books together"). It also carries the romantic sense of being a couple: 他们在一起了 ("they're together now / they're dating").
怎么说呢	zěn me shuō ne	"How should I put it…" Buys a moment while signaling that you are searching for the right phrasing. Implies you have a thought but are weighing how to express it, which sounds thoughtful rather than blank. Useful before delivering a nuanced opinion.
怎么样	zěn me yàng	how is it; how about it — The evaluating cousin of 怎么. It asks about a state or opinion rather than a method: 这家餐厅怎么样？("how's this restaurant?"), 你最近怎么样？("how have you been?"). It also floats a suggestion: 我们去看电影，怎么样？("let's see a movie, how about it?"). Negated, 不怎么样 means "nothing special, so-so".
怎么走？	zěn me zǒu ?	How do I get there? / Which way? — asking for directions — 怎么 zěnme (how; what way) + 走 zǒu (to go). The standard question for asking directions , "How do I get to X?" Response patterns: 一直走 yīzhí zǒu (go straight), 左转 zuǒzhuǎn (turn left), 右转 yòuzhuǎn (turn right), 过了红绿灯 guò le hónglǜdēng (past the traffic light). 往哪边走？= which direction to go?
怎样	zěn yàng	how; in what way; what about — 怎 zěn (how) + 样 yàng (manner). Asks after the manner or outcome of something: 这件事你打算怎样处理 "how do you plan to handle this?" Close to 怎么 zěnme, slightly more formal.
赠送	zèng sòng	to present, give as a gift — 赠 (bestow, present) plus 送 (give). A formal compound for presenting something gratis, common in commerce and ceremony: free gifts with purchase, donated copies, complimentary tickets.
炸鸡	zhá jī	fried chicken — 炸 (zhá, to deep-fry) + 鸡 (chicken). The crisp street-food and fast-food staple, often eaten with 啤酒 (píjiǔ, beer) in the Korean-style 炸鸡啤酒 pairing.
展览馆	zhǎn lǎng uǎn	exhibition hall — 展览 zhǎnlǎn (to exhibit) + 馆. The hall where exhibitions are mounted, cousin to the 博物馆 but temporary in what it shows.
占有	zhàn yǒu	to possess; to occupy
战国	Zhàng uó	the Warring States period
战国策	Zhàng uó Cè	Stratagems of the Warring States — the source text
站点	zhàn diǎn	site; node; stop — 站 (station) + 点 (diǎn, point): a designated point or node, whether a physical service point or a website. Broad and slightly technical, common in logistics, networks, and field operations.
站岗	zhàng ǎng	to stand guard — 站 (to stand) + 岗 (gǎng, a post or sentry position): to take up a guard post. A verb-object phrase, literally "to stand a post," used of soldiers, guards, and watchmen holding their station.
站立	zhàn lì	to stand erect — 站 (stand) + 立 (stand): two near-synonyms stacked for emphasis, a written-register word for standing upright. More formal than plain 站, common in instructions, ceremonies, and description.
站起来	zhàn qǐ lái	to stand up — 站 + 起来 (the directional complement "up"): to rise from sitting to standing. The 起来 makes the verb a completed upward motion rather than a static posture.
站台	zhàn tái	platform — 站 (station) + 台 (tái, raised platform): the boarding platform at a station, the raised edge alongside the track. By extension, 站台 also means to publicly back someone, to "stand on the platform" for them.
站长	zhàn zhǎng	station chief; webmaster — 站 (station) + 长 (zhǎng, head, chief): the person in charge of a station. Originally a stationmaster; now also the administrator of a 网站, a webmaster.
长安	Cháng ān	Chang'an, the Tang capital
长辈	zhǎng bèi	senior generation
长江	Cháng Jiāng	the Yangtze River — lit. "the long river" — 长 cháng (long) + 江 jiāng (large river; river south of Huai River). The Yangtze, Asia's longest river at 6,300 km, is simply "the Long River." Compare: 黄河 Huáng Hé (Yellow River) is named for its color; 长江 is named for its length.
长生	cháng shēng	immortality; long life
长寿	cháng shòu	longevity; long life
折子戏	zhé zi xì	Individual scenes — Individual scenes: excerpts from longer 昆曲 dramas performed as self-contained pieces. As the full-length drama tradition declined, individual scenes that are particularly beautiful or dramatically concentrated became the standard performance unit.
这不是嘛	zhè bú shì ma	isn't this exactly it / this is clearly the case
这个	zhè ge	this; this one — 这 zhè (this) + 个 ge. The demonstrative "this one," with 个 supplying the classifier slot. Its pair is 那个 nàge (that one). Often heard as a filler too, like an English "um."
这个嘛	zhè ge ma	"Well, this…" or "as for this…" The 嘛 particle gives the phrase a casual, considering tone. Often used to introduce a response to a question that does not have a simple answer. Roughly: "well, about that…"
这回	zhè huí	this time; on this occasion — 这 zhè (this) + 回 huí (time). The colloquial "this time," interchangeable with 这次 zhè cì but a touch more casual: 这回我一定赢 "this time I'll win for sure."
这里	zhè lǐ	here
这里 · 那里	zhè lǐ nà lǐ	here · there — 这 (zhè, this) + 里 (interior space) = 这里 (this place, here). 那 (nà, that) + 里 = 那里 (that place, there).
这么说吧	zhè me shuō ba	"Let me put it this way — "Let me put it this way." A colloquial reframing marker, used when you are about to take another run at explaining something. The 吧 particle softens it and invites the listener to follow you into the next attempt.
这样	zhè yàng	like this; this way; so; in this manner — 这 zhè (this) + 样 yàng (manner). Points at a way of being or doing close at hand: 这样做 "do it like this," 就这样吧 "let it be this way, then."
浙菜	Zhè cài	Zhejiang Cuisine
挣钱	zhèng qián	to earn money
整个	zhěng gè	the whole; entire — 整 zhěng (whole) + 个 gè. "The whole of" something taken as one piece: 整个城市 "the whole city," 整个晚上 "the entire evening." Here 个 keeps its sense of a single complete unit.
整体	zhěng tǐ	the whole; the entirety; as a whole — 整 zhěng (whole, complete) + 体 tǐ (body). A thing taken as one integrated body rather than as separate parts. Its standing contrast is 部分 bùfen, a part: 整体利益 "the interests of the whole."
正常	zhèng cháng	normal; regular — 正 zhèng (proper; upright) + 常 cháng (ordinary). On the standard track, as things should be: 一切正常 "everything is normal." Its opposite is 反常 fǎncháng or 不正常.
正好	zhèng hǎo	just right; exactly; as it happens — 正 zhèng (exactly) + 好 hǎo (good). Landing exactly on the mark of fit or timing: 大小正好 "exactly the right size," 你来得正好 "you've come at just the right moment."
正名	zhèng míng	rectification of names; calling things by their correct names — A key political-philosophical doctrine of Confucius. When a disciple asks what he would do first if given control of a state, Confucius says: rectify names 必也正名乎.
正确	zhèng qu è	correct; right; accurate — 正 zhèng (straight; right) + 确 què (sure; firm). Correct in the sense of matching the truth or the standard: 正确答案 "the correct answer," 正确的方法 "the right method." Its opposite is 错误 cuòwù.
正式	zhèng shì	formal; official
正是	zhèng shì	precisely; exactly (formal confirmation)
正体字	zhèng tǐ zì	Traditional characters — Traditional characters: the term used in Taiwan for the traditional character system that Taiwan retained when the PRC introduced simplified characters in the 1950s. Taiwan uses 正體字 (traditional/correct form characters); PRC uses 简体字 (simplified characters).
正统	zhèng tǒng	orthodoxy; legitimate succession
郑和	Zhèng Hé	admiral of the seven voyages
政治家	zhèng zhì jiā	statesman; skilled politician — 政治 zhèngzhì (politics) + 家 jiā. More elevated than 政客 zhèngkè (politician, often pejorative, implying opportunism and maneuvering). 政治家 implies vision, skill, and genuine mastery of governance: a statesman rather than a mere political operator. The 家 suffix confers dignity.
纸钱	zhǐ qián	spirit money — currency for the afterlife — 纸钱 (also 冥纸 míngzhǐ, 金纸 jīnzhǐ in Taiwan, or 冥币 míngbì) are paper facsimiles of money and goods burnt as offerings; the smoke carries them to the dead.
至少	zhì shǎo	at least — 至 zhì (to reach; at the extreme of) + 少 (few, little). At the minimum: "reaching the least amount." 至少要三天 (it'll take at least three days); 至少有一百人 (at least a hundred people). The counterpart is 至多 zhìduō (at most). 至少 sets a floor; 至多 sets a ceiling.
治病	zhì bìng	healing — the shaman's primary function — The most common reason a community calls a shaman is illness. In shamanic cosmology, sickness typically results from one of three spirit-world causes: soul loss (魂魄丢失 húnpò diūshī , the soul has been captured by a spirit or wandered away in fright), spirit intrusion (邪灵侵入 xiélíng qīnrù , a harmful s…
质问	zhì wèn	to interrogate
中国话	Zhōng guó huà	Chinese (the language, casual)
中国梦	Zhōng guó mèng	the Chinese Dream
中国人	Zhōng guó rén	Chinese person
中华	Zhōng huá	China (cultural/civilizational sense)
中间	zhōng jiān	middle; center; in between; among — 中 zhōng (center) + 间 jiān (space). The space in the middle, whether of a row, a room, or a stretch of time: 坐在中间 "to sit in the middle," 在两座山中间 "between two mountains."
中秋拜月	Zhōng qiū Bài Yu è	Mid-Autumn — Moon Offerings — The Mid-Autumn Festival (中秋节 Zhōngqiū Jié), 15th day of the 8th lunar month, centers on honoring the moon (月亮 yuèliàng) and the moon goddess 嫦娥 Cháng'é, who fled to the moon after drinking the elixir of immortality.
中午	zhōng wǔ	noon
中药	zhōng yào	Chinese herbal medicine — 中 zhōng (Chinese) + 药 yào (medicine; drug). Chinese herbal medicine: the pharmacological branch of TCM. The classical formulary contains thousands of substances: plant-based (ginseng 人参 rénshēn, astragalus 黄芪 huángqí, wolfberry 枸杞 gǒuqǐ), mineral (cinnabar 朱砂 zhūshā), and animal-derived.
中庸	zhōng yōng	the Doctrine of the Mean
中原	Zhōng yuán	Central Plains; the heartland — The Central Plain , the heartland of the Yellow River basin, corresponding broadly to modern Henan province and adjacent areas. To "take the Central Plain" (逐鹿中原 zhú lù Zhōngyuán, "chase the deer across the Central Plain") is a classical idiom for competing for control of China.
终点站	zhōng diǎn zhàn	terminus; last stop — 终点 (zhōngdiǎn, end point) + 站 (stop): the final stop on a route, where the line ends and everyone gets off. Its opposite is 起点站 qǐdiǎnzhàn, the first stop.
种地	zhòng dì	to till the land
种子	zhǒng zi	seed (of a plant); (sports) seeded player — 种 zhǒng (kind; to sow) + 子 zi. The seed sense of 子 made explicit. By extension it names a "seeded" competitor in a tournament, the one expected to grow into a winner.
众生	zhòng shēng	all sentient beings
重点	zhòng diǎn	key point; focus; emphasis; priority — 重 zhòng (heavy; important) + 点 diǎn (point). The heavy point, the part that matters most. 重点大学 "key universities," 划重点 "to mark the key points," the things to underline.
重点是	zhòng diǎn shì	"The point is" or "the main thing is — "The point is" or "the main thing is." Close cousin to 关键在于, slightly more conversational. Often used to refocus a discussion that has drifted: 重点是, 我们要不要去 ("the point is, are we going or not").
重读	chóng dú / zhòng dú	to re-read (chóng); to stress (a syllable, zhòng) — 重 + 读 , but 重 is a polyphone. chóngdú = "again-read," to re-read a book or article. zhòngdú = "heavy-read," to stress or accent a syllable in pronunciation. Same characters, different reading, different meaning entirely.
重复	chóng fù	to repeat; to duplicate; repetition — 重 chóng (again) + 复 fù (to return; again). To do or say the same thing over: 重复一遍 "to repeat once more." Note the chóng reading: a common error is to read it zhòng.
重视	zhòng shì	to value; to attach importance to; to take seriously — 重 zhòng (to weigh heavily) + 视 shì (to regard; to view). To treat something as carrying weight, to give it due attention: 重视教育 "to value education." Its opposite is 忽视 hūshì, to overlook.
重要	zhòng yào	important; significant — 重 zhòng (heavy; weighty; important) + 要 (essential; necessary). 重要 means "important" in the sense of having significant weight or consequence. One of the most common adjectives in Chinese. 非常重要 (extremely important) · 最重要的是… (Most importantly…).
诸子百家	zhū zǐ bǎi jiā	Hundred Schools of Thought
猪八戒	zhū bā jiè	Zhu Bajie — the pig, comic appetite — A former marshal of Heaven banished into a pig's body for misconduct, ruled by his appetites for food, sleep, and women, lazy, cowardly, and always scheming to abandon the quest. Yet he is also warm, funny, and ultimately faithful.
猪肉	zhū ròu	pork — 猪 (zhū, pig) + 肉 (meat). The most-eaten meat in China, so common that 肉 alone often means pork by default in recipes.
竹林七贤	Zhú lín Qī Xián	Seven Sages of the Bamboo Grove
煮蛋	zhǔ dàn	boiled egg
专门	zhuān mén	specialized; specially; on purpose — 专 zhuān (focused; sole) + 门 mén (field). Concentrated on one doorway. As an adjective, specialized: 专门知识 "specialized knowledge." As an adverb, specially or on purpose: 我专门来看你 "I came specially to see you."
赚钱	zhuàn qián	To make money, to be profitable — To make money, to be profitable. The opposite pair is 赔钱 (péiqián) or 亏本 (kuīběn). 这个生意不赚钱: this business doesn't make money, the verdict that ends ventures.
庄子	Zhuāng zǐ	Zhuangzi, the Daoist text and its author
准备好	zhǔn bèi hǎo	to be ready — Prepare + brought-to-good-state. 准备好了吗? ("ready?") starts every race, road trip, and presentation in the language. The reply 好了 borrows the complement alone, an answer made of pure result.
桌子	zhuō zi	table; desk — 桌 zhuō (table) + 子 zi. A textbook example of the bare suffix: the root 桌 rarely stands alone, and 子 makes it a usable noun.
子午	zǐ wǔ	Midnight and noon branches
字典	zì diǎn	character dictionary — 字 zì (individual character) + 典 diǎn (canon; standard reference; classic text). A dictionary organized by radical, stroke count, or pronunciation that defines individual characters. The 典 suffix appears in 经典 jīngdiǎn (canonical classics); a 字典 is a canonical reference for individual characters.
字母	zì mǔ	alphabet; letters — 字 zì (character) + 母 mǔ (mother; source; matrix). Literally: "character-mothers," the foundational elements of an alphabetic system from which all words are born. The term applies to any alphabetic system: the English alphabet, the pinyin romanization system, the Cyrillic alphabet.
字幕	zì mù	subtitles; captions — 字 zì (character; text) + 幕 mù (curtain; screen, as in 银幕 yínmù, the silver screen). Literally: "character-curtain." The text that appears across the screen like writing on a curtain. A modern compound that elegantly extends the ancient components into a contemporary context.
字体	zì tǐ	font; typeface; style of writing — 字 zì (character) + 体 tǐ (body; form; style). The physical form a character takes, whether in print, on screen, or in handwriting. Covers both digital fonts and handwriting styles. The same character can appear in multiple 字体: the same 字, different bodies.
宗教	zōng jiào	religion — 宗 zōng (ancestor; lineage) + 教 jiào (teaching). The general word for religion, the handed-down teaching that a community is built around: 宗教信仰 "religious belief."
粽子	zòng zi	rice dumpling wrapped in leaves — Sticky rice dumplings wrapped in bamboo or reed leaves, traditionally triangular or cylindrical. Made with various fillings: red bean paste, salted egg yolk, cured pork. The characteristic food of the Dragon Boat Festival, eaten to commemorate Qu Yuan.
走遍天下	zǒu biàn tiān xià	to travel the whole world; to have been everywhere — 走遍 zǒu biàn (to walk/travel all over; to cover thoroughly , 遍 is a comprehensive coverage complement) + 天下 (all under heaven; the whole world). The ambition of the traveler: to have seen everything, to have left no corner of the world unvisited.
走关系	zǒu guān xi	to use personal connections to get something done — 走 zǒu = to go through; to travel via. 走关系 means to go through the channel of personal connections to accomplish something that might otherwise be difficult or slow.
走后门	zǒu hòu mén	Literally "going through the back door" — Literally "going through the back door": using gifts and personal relationships to obtain favors, preferential treatment, or access that would not be available through formal channels. The phrase acknowledges the reality of this practice without fully endorsing it.
走廊	zǒu láng	corridor; hallway
走运	zǒu yùn	to be lucky
走资派	zǒu zī pài	capitalist roader (target of persecution)
租房	zū fáng	to rent a place
尊重	zūn zhòng	to respect
昨天	zuó tiān	yesterday — 昨 zuó (yesterday; past) + 天 tiān (day). The 昨 character is rarely used outside this compound and 昨晚 zuówǎn (last night) in modern Chinese. Note the tonal transition: 昨 zuó is tone 2, 天 tiān is tone 1, two rising pitches in sequence, which requires attention in spoken practice.
作弊	zuò bì	to cheat (on an exam) — 作 (do) + 弊 (fraud, malpractice). Exam cheating is a criminal offense in China for national exams, policed with metal detectors and signal jammers, which measures how much the tests are worth. The honest sibling 打小抄 (crib notes) covers the classroom misdemeanor.
作家	zuò jiā	writer; author (literary) — 作 zuò (to create; to make; to compose) + 家 jiā. The term implies literary seriousness and sustained creative output, not just anyone who writes. The 家 suffix distinguishes this from 记者 jìzhě (journalist) or 编辑 biānjí (editor), which are professional roles without the mastercraft implication.
作者	zuò zhě	author; writer; the one who made it — 作 zuò (to compose) + 者 zhě (the one who). The maker of a work, most often a writer but usable for any creator. Compare 作家 zuòjiā, which specifically means a professional writer or author by trade.
做菜	zuò cài	to cook; to prepare dishes — 做 zuò (to make) + 菜 cài (dish). To cook, in the sense of making the dishes for a meal. 他很会做菜 "he is a good cook." Its near-neighbor 做饭 zuò fàn frames the same act around the staple rice.
做饭	zuò fàn	to cook (a meal)
做梦	zuò mèng	to dream; (figuratively) to fantasize
做七	zuò qī	to observe the seven-day mourning rites — 做 zuò (to perform) + 七 qī (seven, a seven-day period). The funeral custom of holding rites for the dead every seventh day for seven cycles, forty-nine days in all. Each 七 marks a stage in the soul's passage; the seventh and final period (七七 or 满七) closes the formal mourning.
做青	zuò qīng	working the green — the oxidation control step
做人	zuò rén	to conduct oneself; to be the kind of person one should be — 做 zuò (to do; to act as; to make) + 人 rén. The verb that captures the Confucian view that being human is an achievement, not a given. 做人 is learned: how to navigate relationships, honor obligations, maintain dignity, and treat others with genuine consideration.
做事	zuò shì	to handle matters; to work; to conduct oneself in action
做作	zuò zuo	affected, pretentious
